**Verse 12.** *{Men to walk upon you}* That is, upon the mountains of Jerusalem. Some render the words and sense thus, I will bring men unto you, with my people Israel. Men of other nations, together with the Jews, into the inheritance of my people, to be my people. And in this sense they take also the last verse of the 34. Chapter. But this seems not so genuine and proper to the nature and letter of the original words.

*{be their inheritance}* Figuring that 1 Pet. 1.4.

*{bereave them of men}* By consuming them in the land, or spewing them out of the land. See Lev. 18.28. and 20.22. and 26.20,22. God seems to attribute here the desolations which happened to his people, by hunger, pestilence, and war, to the country; by a similitude of evil women, that do kill their burden in the womb.

**Verse 13.** *{Thou land devourest up men}* You are an accursed country, condemned to all manner of misery; the inhabitants of which cannot live; the Canaanites having first been rooted out of it; and since that, the Israelites. This the enemy imputed, as the reproach of the land, which God did for the sins of the people, according to his just judgments, Num. 13.32.

**Verse 14.** *{Therefore thou shalt devour men no more}* You shall no more be subject to those frequent calamities, which have made you infamous.

*{bereave}* Or, *cause to fall.*

**Verse 15.** *{the nations to hear any more}* By ministering to them any occasion of that reproach, ver. 13. But you shall foster your inhabitants, and yield forth to them all accommodations, and commodities, Hos. 2.22,23.

**Verse 17.** *{as the uncleanness of a removed woman}* Which is extremely abominable, Lev. Chap. 12. and Chap. 15.

**Verse 20.** *{they profaned my holy name}* By their sinning amongst them, Rom. 2.24. and likewise through their misery, and slavery, occasioned by their own sins, they have given their enemies occasion of blaspheming me; as if I their God could not have saved them; or as if I were not faithful in my promises. See Esay 52.5.

*{when they said}* These are they which call themselves the Lord's people, and come as it were out of his school, and yet are so wicked. Thereby they profaned God's holy name among the heathen.

**Verse 21.** *{but I had pity for mine holy name}* Or, *tendered mine.* For to free my name from these imputations, I have resolved to forgive my people, and to reestablish them.

**Verse 22.** *{but for my holy name's sake}* Ver. 32. This excludes from man all dignity, and mean to deserve anything, by seeing that God refers the whole to himself, and that only for the glory of his holy name, Deut. 7.7, &c. and 9.5. Isa. 43.25.

**Verse 23.** *{I will sanctify my great name}* Chap. 28.22. I will cause my divine perfections to be known by the effects, contrary to those slanders; that my holy name may be respected, and honored by all men, as it ought to be.

*{their eyes}* Or, *your.*

**Verse 24.** *{I will take you from among, &c.}* By the return from Babylon, is figured and comprehended the salvation of the Church by the Messiah; together with the abundance of all spiritual graces, of vocation, justification, regeneration, and holiness of life, in the verses following.

**Verse 25.** *{Then will I sprinkle clean water upon you}* I will largely communicate my grace unto you, and grant you pardon for all your sins, purchased with the price of my Son's blood, which was shed for the sins of the world, Eph. 5.26. Heb. 9.14. 1 Joh. 1.7. Isa. 44.23. 1 Pet. 1.2.

**Verse 26.** *{A new heart also will I give you}* Chap. 11.19. Jer. 31.33. and 32.39.

*{the stony heart out of the flesh}* Namely, the hard heart, which has been inflexible to my Spirit, of my Word and judgments, and impenetrable to my grace.

*{of flesh}* Pliable, and capable of being enlivened and governed by my Spirit; as amongst natural bodies, there is none fitting nor apt to receive and contain the vital spirit, but only the fleshy body.

**Verse 27.** *{Spirit within you}* Chap. 11.19.

**Verse 29.** *{Save you}* By my free justification, I will free you from that punishment which your filthiness deserves: and by the sanctification of my Spirit, I will free you from the corruption and slavery of sin.

*{I will call for the corn}* By mine almighty Word, I will cause all manner of good things to be brought forth unto you, as if they came upon a call; as Psal. 105.16.

**Verse 31.** *{Then shall ye remember your own evil ways}* Chap. 6.9. and 20.43.

*{loathe yourselves}* You shall come to true repentance, and think yourselves unworthy to be of the number of God's creatures for your ingratitude against him.

**Verse 32.** *{Not for your sakes do I this}* Not for any desert or worth that is in you; as ver. 22.

**Verse 35.** *{the garden of Eden}* Chap. 28.13. Esay 33.20, &c.

**Verse 36.** *{shall know}* He declares, that it is God that brings a kingdom and country, from ruin and desolation, to a flourishing estate; and that it ought not so much to be referred to the soil or plentifulness of the earth, that any country is rich and abundant, as to God's mercies: as his plagues, and curses do declare, when he can, and does make it barren for sin, Psal. 107.33,34.

*{I the LORD have spoken it}* Chap. 17.24. and 22.14. and 37.14.

**Verse 37.** *{I will yet}* I will be prompt and ready to do it: or, though I have promised to bestow on them these good things, yet I will by prayer be sought unto for them, and will give them multitudes of people to enjoy them: as ver. 10,11.

*{enquired}* Or, *sought to;* as Isa. 55.6. Psal. 111.2.

**Verse 38.** *{as the holy flock}* Heb. *flock of holy things,* which was brought into Jerusalem, at the three solemn feasts, for sacrifices, in innumerable quantities; so shall the multitudes of the faithful be, who are all made spiritual priests in Christ, to offer up spiritual sacrifices, Rom. 12.1.

## CHAPTER 37

**Verse 1.** *{The hand}* Under a type of dried bones is shadowed out the Jews' desperate condition in, and wonderful deliverance out of, the captivity of Babylon; and under the type of two sticks united, is set forth the union of Judah and Israel; of Jew and Gentile, in the perfect instauration of the Church, under Christ.

*{hand of the LORD}* Chap. 1.3.

*{in the spirit}* Chap. 3.12. The Lord by his spirit took me up in a spiritual vision, to the seeming of my spirit.

*{of a valley}* Mesopotamia, and all Chaldea, were full of champaigns, plains, and valleys, Gen. 11.2.

*{full of bones}* He shows by a great miracle, that God has power, and will also deliver his people from their captivity in Babylon; inasmuch as he is able to give life to the dead bones, and bodies, and to raise them up again.

**Verse 2.** *{very many}* Vers. 10.

*{valley}* Or, *champaign.*

**Verse 3.** *{these bones}* So dry.

*{live?}* By any way, or natural power, that you know, be brought to their former state of life in this world? for the article of resurrection is not here questioned.

**Verse 4.** *{Prophesy upon these bones}* Be you the instrument and interpreter of mine almighty will and Word; by which in vision these bones shall seem to you, to have life put into them: for a sign that by my power; my people shall be reestablished in their former estate.

**Verse 7.** *{there was a noise}* A foregoing sign of God's glorious presence, showing his power, in a miraculous and supernatural work, Chap. 3.12. Act. 2.2.

*{bone to his bone}* Joining themselves again into their natural joints.

**Verse 9.** *{wind}* Or, *breath.*

*{And say to the wind}* To the soul of man, or to the Holy Ghost itself; whose personal property is to quicken, and cause all things to live, Psal. 104.30.

*{Come from the four winds}* For to signify the restauration of the people from all those places where they had been scattered.

*{And breathe upon these slain}* Send the soul and spirit of life into every one of these dead bodies, by the operation of your divine power. He seems to have respect to the manner of the creating, and infusing the soul into Adam: described Gen. 2.7.

**Verse 11.** *{these bones are}* The interpretation and application of the foregoing type.

*{our bones are dried}* We are destroyed beyond hope of restauration; we are in Babylon like dried bones, in a sepulcher, or scattered at the grave's mouth, Psal. 141.7.

**Verse 12.** *{I will open your graves}* Isa. 26.19. Hos. 13.14. Joh. 5.28,29.

**Verse 13.** *{when I have opened your graves}* That is, when I have brought you out of those places, and towns, where you are captives.

**Verse 14.** *{have spoken it, and}* Chap. 17.24. and 22.14.

**Verse 16.** *{take thee}* A type of the perfect restauration of Church by Christ.

*{one stick}* Or, *rod of wood.* See Num. 17.17.

*{his companions}* That is, Benjamin and Levi, 2 Chr. 11.12,13.

**Verse 17.** *{into one stick}* Which signified the joining together of the two houses of Israel and Judah, ver. 22.

**Verse 19.** *{I will take}* The interpretation, and application of this type.

*{stick of Joseph}* Of the ten Tribes represented by the stick of Joseph: by reason that the tribe of Ephraim, the son of Joseph, had ruled over them.

**Verse 22.** *{And I will make them one nation}* Vers. 19. Isa. 11.13. Jer. 3.18. Hos. 1.11. And under this as a type, may be shadowed and comprehended, the gathering together of Jew and Gentile, into one visible Church, under Christ; annihilating by the Cross of Christ, the divisions and enmities of the nations; as of Judah, and of the ten tribes; which oftentimes are used by the Prophets for a figure of the division, which was between the Jews and the Gentiles, Isa. 11.13. Eph. 2.14,15,16. And lastly, the uniting of all the elect, of what nation soever they be, into one spiritual body.

*{one King shall be King to them all}* Ver. 24. Joh. 10.16. the Messiah.

**Verse 23.** *{their dwelling places}* Wheresoever they shall be in exile or captivity, as in Babylon, in Egypt, and other places of the world; where either willingly, or by compulsion, they have been partakers of pagan idolatries, Ezek. 14.3. and 20.30.39. Jer. 44.8.

**Verse 24.** *{David}* Chap. 34.23. Isa. 40.11. Jer. 23.5. and 30.9. Dan. 9.24,25. Hos. 3.5. Jesus Christ the son of David according to the flesh.

*{my servant}* Who came in the form of a servant, to do his Father's will: and therefore called a servant, 1 Chr. 17.9. Esay 42.1. Phil. 2.7.

*{and they shall have one shepherd}* Joh. 10.16.

**Verse 25.** *{dwell in the land, That}* Meaning that both Judah and Israel shall return to Canaan, a type of the heavenly Jerusalem, wherein all the elect, by Christ, shall dwell.

**Verse 26.** *{Covenant of peace}* Chap. 34.25. Psal. 89.3.

*{multiply them}* Chap. 36.37,38.

*{and will set my sanctuary in the midst of them}* I will be present in my grace, word, and spirit, in their assemblies, and in their heart. Of which spiritual presence, the Tabernacle and Sanctuary, heretofore were signs and pledges, Lev. 26.11,12. 1 Cor. 3.16. 2 Cor. 6.16. Exod. 2.21,22.

**Verse 27.** *{I will be their God}* Chap. 11.20. and 14.11.

**Verse 28.** *{Do sanctify Israel}* That is, do take it, and consecrate it to myself, and by my presence do keep it holy, and inviolable, against all manner of injuries and offenses.

## CHAPTER 38

**Verse 1.** *{And}* The army malice, and mischief of Gog and his adherents, against the Jews, after their return out of captivity, and before the coming of the Messiah. God's judgments upon Gog therefore.

*{the word of the LORD came}* That these two Chapters following belong to the same thing, it plainly appears. In the Chapters before, the Prophet foretold two things: first, the restoring of his people from the Babylonish captivity: Secondly, their uniting in Christ's kingdom. But after their return, and before the coming of the Messiah, and setting up of his kingdom, God shows them aforehand, in these two Chapters, that many calamities should happen to them, and many judgments from God should fall upon the enemies of the Church. The like anticipation is used, Jer. 31. verse 15.

**Verse 2.** *{Set thy face against}* Chap. 6.2. and 35.2. This prophecy is to be referred to the desolation that should come upon the Jews after their return from Babylon, Dan. 8. and 11. Chapters.

*{Gog}* Rev. 20.8. that is, the Kings of Asia Minor, and Syria, whose history is in the books of the Maccabees: where it is showed how it came to pass, that they, and their armies fell in Judea. And these two names Gog and Magog largely note the kingdom of the Seleucidae; in Asia Minor, Graecia, Syria, and those parts, wherein divers of the name of Seleucus, as Antiochus, Demetrius, Nicanor, did reign and rule. To the same also do belong Meshech, Tubal, Paras, Gomer, and Togarma, vers. 5,6.

*{the land of Magog}* Or, *in the land.* Most likely the inhabitants of Scythia, or Sarmatia: which did overrun the countries, even to Syria, and built Hierapolis, called in the Syrian tongue Magog; as some note out of Pliny, lib. 5. Chap. 23.

*{the chief prince of Meshech}* Or, *prince of the chief;* that is, of a province which shall rule over others, or be governor of many: for the Prophet recites the names of certain nations, by whom are to be understood, all they, whose endeavor and help the Kings of Syria used to invade Judea; as appears ver. 5. and in the following verses.

*{of Meshech and Tubal}* See Annotations on Chap. 27. ver. 13.

**Verse 3.** *{O Gog}* That is, all the nations, ministers to the King of Syria, to overthrow my people. Gog, or Gyges properly, was servant to Candaules King of Lydia, whom the Queen would have killed, because the King might set him where he might see her naked, going to bed to her husband, except he would agree with her to kill his master Candaules, and marry her, which he did perform; and to root out Candaules his name, who came of Lydus, of whom the country was named Lydia, he called the country Lydia, by the name of Gygea, or Gog's land. Croesus, Gog's successor, was conquered by Cyrus the Persian. The Persians were conquered by the Greeks, who holding that land, are here called Gog and Magog.

**Verse 4.** *{I will turn thee back}* Chap. 39.2. After you have assaulted my people, you shall turn into Syria, and to your own place. The Syrian Kings made many incursions into Judea. Or rather, After the aforesaid assaults, and return, I will turn you back thither again to reassault them; but to your own destruction.

*{and put hooks into thy chaws}* 2 King. 19.28. Ezek. 29.4.

*{I will bring thee forth}* That is, I will bring you, and the nations, which are named in the following verses, forth out of their countries: showing that the enemies should bend themselves against the Church; but it should be to their own destruction.

*{all of them}* Chap. 23.12.

*{clothed with all sorts of armour}* That is, completely furnished with military necessaries.

**Verse 5.** *{Persia}* Whereof part paid tribute to Antiochus Epiphanes, Macc. 3.31.

*{Ethiopia}* In Hebrew, Cush: he was the firstborn of Cham, Gen. 10.6. The Ethiopians came of him; and here, as divers times elsewhere, the first Fathers are put for the posterity arising from them.

*{Libya with them}* Or, Phut, the third son of Ham, Gen. 10.6. of whom came the Cyreneans. By the help of the Ethiopians (spoken of before) and the Cyreneans, Alexander was put to flight, and Demetrius restored to the kingdom by Ptolemy Evergetes, 1 Macc. 18. and 11. Chapters.

**Verse 6.** *{Gomer}* Gomer was Japhet's first son, and Togarmah the third son of Gomer, Gen. 10.2,3. and are thought to be they that inhabit Asia Minor, whose posterity were subject to the Seleucidae.

*{Togarmah}* See Annotations on Chap. 27.14.

**Verse 7.** *{Prepare for thyself}* Signifying, that all the people of these countries, should assemble themselves against the Church of the Jews, and that the land of Gog and Magog, by Grecians, should afflict them; with great helps from many Northern countries, ver. 15. Chap. 39.2.

**Verse 8.** *{a guard unto them}* That is, be you their Emperor, to keep them from hostile assault. Some say, that Gog should compass the mountains of Israel, as in the next verse, with the multitude of his armies, and think to hold all the Jews, as shut in prison, or enclosed in a pit. And that Kings of Syria have done so, all histories testify; so vers. 9. Others say, it is an ironical speech; as if he should say, Defend them, if it may be, from my power and battle, which I will give them.

**Verse 8.** *{after many days}* To wit, in the times of the Maccabees, above two hundred years after this prophecy; in the parted kingdom of the Greeks, under the Seleucidae, and Lagidae; the Kings of the North, and South, Dan. 11. This fell out.

*{visited}* That is, in anger. Or, God will punish you, O Gog; and from the times of Seleucus Nicanor, the wonderful judgments of God began to be executed, for defense of his people, against the Grecian Kings.

*{in the latter years}* A little before the coming of Christ in the flesh: whence there should begin a new world.

*{brought back from the sword}* Saved from the precedent ruins of war: a description of the land of Israel. But by the land is to be understood the inhabitants; because it is said to be brought back, as it was promised, Chap. 37.

*{against}* Or, *upon.*

*{always waste}* That is, a long time, and which seemed never to have been restored to its former state.

**Verse 9.** *{like a storm}* See Prov. 1.27. Job. 30.14.

**Verse 10.** *{think an evil thought}* Or, conceive a mischievous purpose, to molest and destroy the Jews.

**Verse 11.** *{of unwalled villages}* Meaning Judea; which was destroyed, and not yet built again. And this black face, or sad condition befell her, from the vastation wrought, by the Chaldeans under King Nebuchadnezzar, who destroyed the cities and fenced places, leaving them but unwalled towns. See Deut. 2.5.

*{rest}* Haply relating hereby, in part, to the simplicity of the godly, who seek not so much to fortify themselves, by outward force, as to depend on the providence and goodness of God.

*{Safely}* Or, confidently: or, securely.

**Verse 12.** *{to take a spoil}* Heb. *to spoil the spoil, and to prey the prey.*

*{to turn thine hand upon the desolate places}* To renew, and bring again the ancient desolations of this poor country, restored from its former ruins; to spoil them again.

*{midst of the land}* In Hebrew, Upon the navel of the land. That is, in the higher places, as mountains and hills; or, in the heart or middle, where there is more security, than in the confines: a metaphor; such as is, Judg. 9.37.

**Verse 13.** *{Sheba and Dedan}* The Arabians; Sheba and Dedan's posterity. They came of Chush, Gen. 10.7. See Annotations on Chap. 27.15,20,22.

*{merchants of Tarshish}* There chiefly are to be understood, they of Tyrus and Sydon, and others passing the seas for gain: meaning the Arabians and marine nation, will come to you to partake in your spoils.

*{young Lions thereof}* The pirates and robbers at sea, which are, as it were, the sea-beasts. Others say, merchants that lie in wait for gain, as the Lion does for his prey.

*{Art thou come?}* Without us? As if they should say, If thereto you consent, we will join with you. Others thus, One enemy shall envy another; because everyone shall think to have the spoil of the Church.

**Verse 14.** *{dwelleth safely}* Or, securely; at peace with all, and suspecting nothing; Then shall you spy your occasions to come against my Church, when they suspect nothing.

*{shalt thou not know it?}* That is, you shall surely know my judgment against you for this; as may appear, by comparing the 16.18. and following verses.

**Verse 16.** *{latter days}* Meaning in the latter age, before the coming of the Messiah.

*{be sanctified in thee}* When in punishing you, O Gog, I shall manifest myself to be a holy and powerful God, Chap. 28.22. See Lev. 10.3.

**Verse 17.** *{Art thou he?}* Meaning, surely you are he.

*{old time}* There are no footsteps of this prediction before Ezekiel's time; and therefore this word, Old, should be understood in comparison, not of the Prophet's age, but of the time of execution, which fell out above two hundred years after this Prophet.

*{by my servants}* Heb. *by the hands.* Hereby God declares, that no affliction can come to the Church, whereof they have not been advertised aforetime: to teach them, to endure all things with more patience, when they know that God has so ordained it. And this affliction, is foretold by this Prophet Ezekiel, in this, and the next Chapter, and by Daniel, Chap. 11. and Zach. 14. See the like, Esay 48.3, &c.

**Verse 18.** *{fury shall come up in my face}* Psal. 18.8. A speech after the manner of men; taken from them that puff in the nose, when they are exceeding angry.

**Verse 19.** *{great shaking}* All creatures shall be astonished, and afraid, at the presence of my glorious majesty, in the punishment of mine enemies.

**Verse 20.** *{so that the fishes of the Sea}* By these strange expressions, God sets forth the grievousness of his anger that was to come.

*{steep places shall fall}* Or, *towers,* or *stairs.* All means, whereby man may think to save himself, shall fail; the afflictions in those days shall be so great, and the destruction of the enemies shall be so terrible.

**Verse 21.** *{I will call for a sword}* This was in part fulfilled by the Maccabees; and partly by civil wars of the Grecian Kings; whence the ruin of that empire did arise.

*{every man's sword}* That is, of the soldiers of Gog; as it happened to the Midianites, Judg. 7.22. 2 Chron. 22.23.

**Verse 21.** *{I will plead against him}* Myself; that is, with many, and great judgments will I destroy him.

*{I will rain upon him}* I will make a miraculous discomfiture and destruction. These terms seem to be borrowed from the fire of Sodom; and the hailstones of Joshua, Josh. 10.11.

**Verse 23.** *{I will magnify myself}* Chap. 36.23. and 28.22.

## CHAPTER 39

**Verse 1.** *{Therefore thou Son of man}* Further judgments against Gog, to be executed on him, and his, in the land of Israel, viz. to be disarmed, and arms burnt, to be slain; and part buried there, set out with the circumstances thereof; part unburied, for a prey to the birds and beasts. The end and use of these judgments, both to the Jews and heathen nations. Conclude with a glorious promise to his people, of bringing them back, and blessing them with grace.

*{against Gog}* Chap. 38.2,14. Further denunciation of judgment against him; chiefly to be executed in the land of Israel.

**Verse 2.** *{And I will turn thee back}* As Chap. 38.4.

*{and leave but the sixth part of thee}* Or rather, as the marginal reading is, *draw thee back with an hook of six teeth.*

*{North parts}* Heb. *the sides of the North.*

**Verse 3.** *{smite thy bow}* I will disarm you. This appears fulfilled in the history of the Maccabees, sundry times.

**Verse 4.** *{fall upon the mountains}* Where they came to vanquish Israel in their own land, there should these enemies be destroyed by them.

*{sort}* Heb. *wing.*

*{to be devoured}* Heb. *to devour.*

**Verse 5.** *{the open field}* Heb. *the face of the field.*

**Verse 6.** *{fire on Magog}* That is, an extreme desolation, and ruin, as Chap. 38.22. will God send upon the inhabitants of the land of Magog.

*{carelessly}* Or, *confidently.*

*{in the Isles}* Isa. 41.1,5. as the Tyrians, and Sidonians, and all the confederates of Magog. Confer with this, Chap. 38.39. In all places where the enemies of my people dwell, seem they never so far separate, or secure.

**Verse 7.** *{Pollute my holy Name}* Be polluted by the blasphemies, and insultations of mine enemies.

**Verse 8.** *{It is Come}* That is, this plague is fully determined in my counsel, and cannot be changed; shall as surely come, as if come already.

**Verse 9.** *{shall go forth}* Hyperbolical terms, to express an exceeding great discomfiture. After which the Church shall have great peace and tranquility; and burn all the weapons of the slain Macedonians; see ver. 10. because they shall no more fear the enemy. And this is chiefly effected at the accomplishment of Christ his kingdom; when by their head Christ, all enemies shall be overcome.

*{hand staves}* Or, *javelins.*

*{burn them}* Or, *make a fire of them.*

*{seven years}* How often in Scripture, is a finite number put for an indefinite? yea, this number of seven. See Gen. 4.15. Job 5.19. Prov. 9.1. and 24.16. Meaning here by seven years, a long time. See ver. 12.14.

**Verse 11.** *{A place there of graves}* Not a possession of the land which he did earnestly covet; but contrarily I will slay them there, so that in that land, part of the enemies shall be buried, unto vers. the 17. Part unburied shall be torn of beasts, thence to the 21. verse: which declares, that the enemies shall have an horrible fall.

*{the valley}* There is no mention elsewhere of this valley. It is likely, it was a fair and large valley, much frequented by strangers that came to Judea. The end is to show the immense slaughter by the place destined for burial, which was so great a valley, which should seem, in some sort, to be filled up, and obstructed to passengers, by bodies and bones.

*{on the East of the Sea}* He understands either the Dead Sea, the lake of Sodom; or else the Sea of Tiberias, the lake of Genesareth.

*{stop}* The noses, by reason of the stink: or, the mouths of the passengers; that seeing such a slaughter there, they shall not dare to open their mouths against the Jews.

*{Hamon-gog}* That is, the multitude of Gog.

**Verse 12.** *{cleanse the land}* Because, according to the Law, a dead body unburied, defiled the land, Deut. 21.23.

*{seven months}* Ver. 14. See Annotations on ver. 9.

**Verse 13.** *{It shall be to them}* This great mountain, as it were, of bodies and bones, shall be to them instead of a glorious trophy and monument.

*{I shall be glorified}* To wit, over those enemies of my people, by cutting them off. See Chap. 28.22.

**Verse 14.** *{sever out}* Because those people were unclean according to the Law, Num. 19.11.

*{men of continual employment}* Heb. *men of continuance.* Men of public employment, for the burying of the dead.

*{to bury}* Partly, that the holy land should not be polluted; and partly for the compassion that the children of God have even on their enemies.

*{with the passengers}* Likely, taking the help of strangers, whom they shall meet in the way.

**Verse 15.** *{He set up a sign}* Heb. *build.* To show to the buriers, that yonder be bones of dead bodies to bury; and so to cleanse the country.

**Verse 16.** *{The name of the City}* There shall remain a perpetual memory of discomfiture; so that the next city, as it may seem, on the top of this valley, shall bear the name Hamonah, that is multitude. Or, according to some, From this multitude of the slain shall grow a name and fame to the city Jerusalem, 1 Macc. 13. and 14. Chapters.

**Verse 17.** *{unto every feathered fowl}* Heb. *to the fowl of every wing.*

*{Assemble yourselves}* Isa. 18.6. and 34.6. Jerem. 12.9. Rev. 19.17.

*{gather yourselves on every side}* Whereby is signified the horrible destruction that should come on the enemies of his Church.

*{to my sacrifice}* Or, *slaughter;* that is, to the supper prepared by me, consisting of slain men, and fatlings.

**Verse 18.** *{the mighty}* Persons not vile or base; and the flesh, not of unclean or starveling beasts: but renowned and delicate flesh, fatted in Bashan; which was a mount excellent for feeding cattle; as you may see, Numb. 32.4, Deut. 32.14.

*{goats}* Heb. *great goats.*

**Verse 20.** *{Table, with horses}* Warriors on horses, and in chariots.

**Verse 21.** *{and I will set my glory}* That is, make it clearly appear. Here is the end of God's judgments.

*{and my hand that I have laid upon them}* My hand of judgments, Job 23.2. and 27.11.

**Verse 23.** *{For their iniquity}* The heathen shall know, that they overcame not my people, by their strength, neither yet by the weakness of mine arm; but that this was for my people's sins.

*{therefore hid I my face from them}* Psal. 10.1. and 13.2. and 27.9. I laid aside the care of them; I withdrew my help from them.

*{so fell they all by the sword}* The whole state or body of the people; or indifferently, of every condition, or quality.

**Verse 25.** *{Now will I bring again}* The Prophet concludes with God's glorious promises to his people, to bring again their captivity, and to bless them with grace.

**Verse 27.** *{am sanctified}* Chap. 36.23.

**Verse 28.** *{which caused them to be led into captivity}* Heb. *by my causing of them, &c.*

**Verse 29.** *{poured out my Spirit}* Joel 2.28. Act. 2.17. whereupon they being sanctified by me, I will take pleasure in them forever. As on the contrary, sin is that which turns God's face from his Church.

## CHAPTER 40

**Verse 1.** *{In}* The difficulty of the right understanding of this vision following; and specially of the three first Chapters of it, the 40,41,42. is such as gave chief occasion to that order and ordinance, among the Jews, that none under the age of 30 years, should be admitted to the reading of it. And some of their learned Rabbis and expositors have professed, that divers passages of it must be left to the coming of their Elias, whom they again expect; despairing, but by him, of the true meaning of them. Some Papists have spent many years, and bestowed much labor, in commenting upon this vision; yet fall short of their own satisfaction; and fully acknowledge their own insufficiency for so hard a task. They tie themselves to their Latin translation, which is a ground of erring to them: and sparingly touching upon the literal sense, they largely run out into allegorical, analogical, and tropological senses, and uses of their own. And some of them, in this regard of difficulty, hardly abstain from blasphemous expressions against the holy text-man, and his text. As Sanctius in his argument upon this Chapter, calls the description of the Temple, with its courts, and chambers, in this vision, *insulsam descriptionem.* And afterwards, it seems, pleases himself in laying the same imputation upon it, the same word, the second time. And among all sorts of divines, ancient and modern, Greek and Latin Fathers, and late and learned writers of the Reformed religion, many have waved this vision of Ezekiel, and not undertaken the exposition of it. We may not yet pass by it, lying thus in our way; But shall endeavor to give some light unto it, and be an help to those that shall come after us.

*{the five & twentieth year of our Captivity}* He reckons, not by the years of Kings, but by the years of their captivity. And this captivity was that of Jehojachin, or Jeconiah, called also Coniah; and falls in with the beginning of the reign of Zedekiah. See 2 King. 24.12——17. and compare Ezek. 24.1,2. with 2 King. 25.1. And the Prophet beginning his account from this date, Ch. 1.2. he still goes on to make the same his constant epoch, or rise of all his reckonings of the times. See Chap. 8.1. and 20.1. and 24.1. and 26.1. and 29.1,17. and 30.20. and 31.1,17. and 33.21. There are twenty years between Ezekiel's first vision, and this vision. That was in the twelfth of Nebuchadnezzar, and first of Ezekiel's captivity. And this is in Nebuchadnezzar thirty-two, and Ezekiel's five and twentieth. This indeed is the fiftieth year from the eighteenth of Josiah; whereof Chap. 1.1. But no Jubilee, (as some would have it,) not falling in with the Jubilees' just account.

*{our Captivity}* He so calls it, because being young, was then led away captive. See Annotations on Chap. 1.1.

*{Captivity}* Heb. *migration,* Matth. 1.11,17. transmigration, transportation carrying away.

*{in the beginning of the year}* Heb. *the head of that year;* that is, the first month. See Annotations on Exod. 12.2. That month was called Abib, and Nisan, which begins with or about our *vernal equinoctial,* and answers to part of our March; and it is still called the first month, wheresoever the months of the year are numerically named, and counted in Scripture. And though the ingatherings, or end of harvest, which fell in their Tizri, and our September be said, to be at the year's end, Exod. 23.16. and Chap. 34.22. and the year of Jubilee be appointed to begin in that same month, Lev. 25.9. yet there itself that month is called the seventh month, not the first: so constantly are the reckonings of the months of the year kept according to that ordinance, Exod. 12.2. And therefore the beginning of the year here must be understood to be in the *Vernal,* not *Autumnal equinoctial.* See Annotations on Chap. 8.1.

*{in the tenth day}* When the Paschal Lamb was to be taken up, and kept, Exod. 12.3,6.

*{in the fourteenth year after}* The fourteenth year from the eleventh of Zedekiah, when the city Jerusalem was destroyed, and the Temple burnt, 2 King. 25.2,3,7,8,9. which fourteen added to the eleven of Zedekiah, make up the five and twentieth mentioned immediately before.

*{in the self-same day}* Heb. *in the bone,* or, *in the strength of the day.*

*{The hand of the LORD was upon me}* Chap. 1.3. and 8.1. and 33.22. and 37.1. His divine power was upon me, as after is showed in this prophetical vision; and I came under the power of his hand, and Spirit, I was in the Spirit, rapt and ravished; as Rev. 1.10. 2 Cor. 12.2.

*{and brought me thither}* To the place where the city and Temple had been.

**Verse 2.** *{In the visions}* There are many parts and particulars in this vision.

*{visions of God}* Great and wonderful, and divine visions. See Annotations on Chap. 1. ver. 1. Thus the goodliest cedars are called the cedars of God, Psal. 80.10. and mighty mountains, the mountains of God, Psal. 36.6. And thus we read of the garden of God, the city of God, the man of God, the increasing of God; the wrestlings of God, Gen. 30.8. Jonah 3.3. These visions set forth the spiritual building, and restauration of the Church, by the coming of Christ, and preaching of the Gospel. They are shadowed out, under types and figures of corporal things. Thus for man's weakness, spiritual things, such as eye has not seen, nor can see, must be set forth by earthly comparisons. And herein the Prophet from God, applies himself to the place where, the time when, and the persons among whom he was, he prefigures here, the perfect state and constitution of the Christian Church, under the types of the rebuilding of the Temple, Chap. 40.41,42. of the restauration of the Levitical worship and service, Chap. 43.44. of the repossession, and inhabitation of the whole land; the kingdom of Canaan, and of the city of Jerusalem, in the four last Chapters.

*{brought he me}* Not in body, but in spirit. Yet the vision of his mind was so clear, and perspicuous, as if not in fantasy; but with bodily eyes he had beheld the same. See Chap. 8.3.

*{And set me upon}* From thence he comes down to the foot of it, vers. 5.

*{a very high mountain}* The mountain of the Temple: on the South side of which, the city itself lay. Mount Moriah, where Isaac, by God's command to Abraham, was bound to be sacrificed, Gen. 22.2,9. where David buying the place, built an altar, in the threshing floor of Araunah, or Ornan, the Jebusite, and there offered to the Lord burnt offerings, and peace offerings, and the plague was stayed, 2 Sam. 24.16,21,25. 1 Chron. 21.16. where afterward by Solomon, the Temple was built, 2 Chron. 3.1. This mount was high, for it was seen far off, Gen. 22.4. This mount Sion (for so most usually it is called) was a type of the Church, Psal. 2.6. and 48.2. And as it was a figure of the spiritual Temple under Christ, when the Church was to be made catholic; see of it, Esay 2.2,3. and 25.6,7,8,9,10. Mic. 4.1,2. Rev. 21.10. The mount may shadow out the Church's strength, and firm stability; its safety, splendor, and visibility. And yet a city upon a mount, may be hid sometimes with clouds, mists, fogs, tempests; and seen only of them that are within it. And it may also mind us to mount our thoughts on high, to heavenward, Col. 3.1,2. The woman has the Moon under her feet, Apoc. 12.1.

*{By which was as a frame of the City on the South}* Or, upon which mountain: was as a frame of a city on the South. The city Jerusalem had formerly been built thereby; and was after by Zerubbabel and Nehemiah built there. But here the city of Jerusalem is not meant: For in this vision it was to be built from the Temple Southwards, sundry miles; as appears, Chap. 45.1,2,3,4,5,6. and Chap. 48.8,9,10,13,15,16,17,18,19,20. See the Annotations on Chap. 48.15. Which among other things, shows this vision of the Temple and city, not to be meant literally; but mystically, of the ample and flourishing estate of Christ's Church under the Gospel. And the words here are, *as the frame of the City;* that is, the Temple here shown in vision, was built like the frame of a city, of such largeness, and with so many walls, gates, courts and towers, a city. And this building appeared on the South to the Prophet, that came in vision from Babylon, which stands thence most Northerly; not that this building was on the South part of the hill. See Psal. 48.2.

**Verse 3.** *{And behold}* To raise attention; See Genes. 28.12,13.

*{a man}* An Angel in the likeness of a man, prefiguring Christ, Chap. 1.26. and 9.2. and 43.6. where he seems distinct from the Lord, speaking out of the Temple, Zeph. 2.12. The man Gabriel is named, Dan. 9.2. but yet here, most likely is meant the Angel of the Covenant, Christ himself, usually appearing as a man, and styled a man, Genes. 18.1,13. and 32.24,30. Josh. 5.13,14. Judg. 13.11, &c. Dan. 7.13. Act. 17.31. Rev. 1.13. and 11.5. and Chap. 14.14.

*{like the appearance of brass}* As burnished, and burning brass; as fire, Chap. 1.7. Rev. 1.15. This may intimate, Christ's purity, sanctity; yea, his divinity, as some will. He appears not here, as an angry judge, as Chap. 1.27. But lovingly, as a master builder.

*{with a line of flax in his hand, and a measuring reed}* Likely the one, in the one hand; and the other, in the other. This line or cord might be to measure the greater spaces; as the compass of the outer wall, the floors of the whole building, and of the courts, the waters, Chap. 47.3. serving well for such uses, Zach. 2.1,2. And the reed, or rod, to measure the height and thickness of the walls, and the proportion of the gates, chambers, porches, and things of the Temple. And thus much used, and most fit, for lightness, stiffness, and other ways, for such purposes, Rev. 11.1. and Chap. 21.15. A golden reed is there, to measure the golden and heavenly Jerusalem. Our measuring line, and reed, must be the sacred Scriptures. By them must God's house be built and ordered. To this does allude the manner of speech, Esay 28.10. Gal. 6.16. Phil. 3.16. See Exod. 25.9,40. Act. 7.44. Heb. 8.5.

*{and he stood in the gate}* Or, *by the gate.* As the keeper of the Temple, as the guider and teacher of the Prophet; He having, indeed, the direction and administration of the spiritual building; as the Lord of the house, and heir of all, having the charge and power of opening and shutting, Heb. 1.2. and 3.6. Joh. 10.2,3. Revel. 3.7. In the North gate of the utmost wall, most likely, next to one coming from Babylon, and next to the East angle or corner on that square. Christ is the gate, and way, and as it were the doorkeeper to the Father, Joh. 14.6. and 10.9. Rom. 5.2. Cant. 2.9.

**Verse 4.** *{Son of Man}* See *Annotations* on Chap. 2.1. Here is the end and use of the vision.

*{behold with thine eyes, and hear}* So did the Lord reveal to Moses in vision, the fashion of the Tabernacle; To David, the fashion of the Temple; and to John, the new Jerusalem. Here Ezekiel was to learn some things by sight, some things, by hearing, Prov. 20.12. yet these words are not to be understood here of the bodily senses properly; for he was now in body, in the country of Babylon: but of the answerable faculties of the mind.

*{Set thine heart upon all}* He was to lay up all in his heart, take it to heart, as the phrases are used, Psal. 62.10. 2 Sam. 21.11. 2 Sam. 19.19. to mark and weigh all, to mind and remember all, with all diligence possible, Mat. 11.15. Apoc. 2.11. Mat. 24.15. Jer. 48.10. and Ezek. 44.5.

*{for to the intent——: declare all, &c.}* The Angel is a Prophet, to the Prophet Ezekiel: and Ezekiel the Prophet, is an Angel to the Church.

*{Declare}* We must learn to teach others: what use of a candle hid under a bushel? Matth. 10.27. 2 Tim. 4.2. For to teach others, for their use and sake; as Joh. 12.30. is Ezekiel here instructed, Ezek. 43.10,11. Thus Paul did, Act. 20.20,27. Thus our Savior prescribes, Mat. 28.20. and practices, Joh. 15.15.

*{declare all}* By words, deeds, gestures, to the Church. Not anything here is superfluous or impertinent.

*{to the house of Israel}* This concerns both Jew and Gentile, Rom. 11.20.

**Verse 5.** *{And behold, a wall on the outside of the house round about}* The utmost wall of stone, enclosing the house, temple, and courts, porches, chambers, and whole buildings. So largely is house taken; as elsewhere, 2 Sam. 7.5,6. 1 Chron. 22.5. So Ezek. 43.12. And this wall was foursquare, as after appears, Chap. 42.16, &c. and so compassing the house round about; or, as the Hebrew is, *round about, round about.* See Esay 26.1. Zach. 2.4,5.

*{A measuring reed of six cubits long, by the cubit, and an hand breadth}* The length of the measuring reed, or rod, is here set down, as the sure rule of the measures following. But here two questions, and doubts are made; First, of the length of the cubit; And secondly, whether the handbreadth is to be added to every one of the six cubits in the reed, or only one handbreadth to the whole reed. The length of the cubit is a thing very considerable. For by cubits are reckoned almost all measures of buildings in Scripture. By cubits are measured the Ark of Noah, and the prevailing of the waters. By cubits are measured Moses' Tabernacle, the Ark, mercy seat, table of showbread, curtains, boards, altar of incense, altar of burnt offerings, court, and hangings of it. By cubits are measured Solomon's Temple, the Oracle, Sanctuary, porch, pillars, chambers, Cherubims, molten sea, lavers, with their bases and wheels, altar of burnt offerings. And so here in Ezekiel's Temple, by cubits are measured, the gate, posts, porches, arches, chambers, courts, tables, altar of burnt offerings, and all within and adjoining to the house itself, porch, temple, and oracle. And so by cubits is measured the wall of the heavenly Jerusalem, Apoc. 21. ver. 17.

Now a cubit properly signifies the elbow, and in measuring it signifies commonly the length from the elbow, to the end of the longest finger: which is somewhat more than the fourth part of a man's stature. And thus it differs, and is uncertain according to the several statures of men, of men in one age, much more in several ages. *Virgil* speaking of the stone lifted up and thrown by *Turnus* has these words,

*vix illud lecti bis Sex Cervice subirent, Qualia nunc hominum producit corpora tellus. Aeneid. lib. 12.* See *August. de Civ. Dei lib. 15. cap. 9.*

We have some distinction of cubits intimated in Scripture by these expressions; Og's bedstead was nine cubits long, four broad, after the cubit of a man, says the text, Deut. 3.11. and Apoc. 21.17. the wall of the new Jerusalem is measured to be 144 cubits, according to the measure of a man, that is of the Angel. The length of Solomon's Temple is said to be sixty cubits, after the first measure, 2 Chr. 3.3. so implying a second measure of cubits: and Ezekiel 41.8. is mention made of great cubits. See Annotations in ver. 42.

And finding the two pillars Jachin and Boaz, to be counted 18 cubits high apiece, in 1 King. 7.15. and to be 35 cubits high apiece, in 2 Chr. 3.15. and the base added to it, making thereto six: This occasions the common distinction of cubits, into common and sacred cubits; as there is, in common opinion, of shekels: the sacred cubits doubling the length of the common cubits; as the sacred shekel does the weight and worth of the common shekel: and this will reconcile the foresaid difference in the height of the two pillars. And hereupon sacred cubits, are conceived to be usually understood in sacred buildings: and the cubits of the measure of the house of the forest of Lebanon to be understood for common cubits, lest otherwise that house should exceed in measure the Lord's Temple. And likewise Cyrus his decree found in a roll at Achmetha, in the province of the Medes, does seem to reckon by common cubits; because it doubles some of Solomon's measure: Though possibly Cyrus, a heathen, might mistake in the right measures of the Temple; or the record be corrupted, Ezr. 6.3. And some learned men, for a seeming need, do apply sacred cubits to the building of Noah's ark. And more than this, we read not of the length of cubits in Scripture. What learned men have added above this; some making the sacred cubit, called also, say they, the royal cubit, and the legal cubit, to consist of one handbreadth more than the common cubit. Some again making the common cubit to consist of four handbreadths, or twelve inches: some of five handbreadths or fifteen inches, some of six handbreadths, or eighteen inches, that is our foot and half foot; and some of four and twenty inches, or two of our feet: And some making the cubit here mentioned to be our yard; Some, our common ell. All these are rather raised upon probable conjectures from uncertain antiquities, and traditions, than grounded on certain truths. And what cubit is here meant, we must leave to conjecture, upon the premises. Now for handbreadths, being four fingers breadth, or three inches; in common account, three handbreadths are our span, that is, twelve fingers, or nine thumbs or inches; and four handbreadth are our foot, that is, sixteen fingers, or twelve thumbs or inches. And so to come to the second question and doubt, To what the handbreadth, here mentioned, must be referred? Some conceive it is to be added to every one of the six cubits in the reed: and this seems to be seconded by that text, Chap. 43.13. where the cubit is defined by a cubit and an handbreadth: and it is further strengthened in them, by their apprehension of the suitableness of such a measure in compliance with the several parts of all the buildings. But that cubit then did not consist of six handbreadths; else most likely the reed would have been reckoned to consist of seven cubits exactly: But the cubit then must be of a longer extent; and specially it will be so, if it be the sacred cubit. But most understand this of one cubit to be added to the whole reed; and that as part of the measure, and not only for the use of the hand to hold it withal. In issue the point rests upon probabilities on both sides. And so the reed itself may rather be guessed at, than positively ascertained. But sure it is between three, and six yards.

*{The breath of the building one Reed, and the height one Reed}* That utmost wall being as a parapet, or breast-wall, or a kind of outward fence, round about all the courts and Temple; and that it was of this breadth and height, one reed or rod. The length of it, in the four squares of it, is set down, as most conceive, Chap. 42.16,17,18,19,20. See the Annotations there, and on ver. 6. of this Chapter, and on Chap. 45.2. This compassed the edge of the hill of mount Zion; very deep and steep valleys being on the outside of it round about. It was useful among other things, against the danger of falling: and this wall, and the other walls, of the same thickness about the several courts, and the Temple, may note the great strength and defensibleness that the Lord sets about his Church, Psal. 34.7. and 46.1, &c. and 125.1,2. Esay 26.1. Zech. 2.5. and 9.8. Rom. 8.31, &c. Joh. 10.28. We read not of such a wall as this compassing Solomon's Temple: which, among many other things, as will after appear, shows, that this Temple shown in vision to Ezekiel, is not the same with Solomon's Temple: Though some learned Papists do stiffly, if not grossly, maintain the contrary. See Annotations on Chap. 8.7.

**Verse 6.** *{Then came he toward the gate which looketh toward the East}* Heb. *whose face was the way, &c.* This is the East gate. But whether of the outward wall, last mentioned, and measured, ver. 5. compassing a court common to the Gentiles, and unclean; (if such a court there were) or of the wall compassing the court for the Jews only, in some respect common both to people and Priests, is a thing wherein learned interpreters do doubt, and differ; and which cannot be easily determined. We conceive it not to be the gate of the utmost wall; because that wall was but as a parapet, or breast-wall: and the height of that wall and gate is so low, and little; and the chambers, and other buildings, within this gate, and annexed to it, are so many, and so great; set down, ver. 6. to 17. which buildings seem most proper to that gate entering into the court for the Jews. The gate may mind us of Christ, the gate, and true door, Joh. 10.7. and 14.6. Zech. 6.12. And in all the gates, in each court, there is a constant regular uniformity observed; which may school us to conformity, and uniformity; that all things in the Church may *be done decently and in order,* 1 Cor. 14.40.

*{And went up the stairs thereof}* The Temple was built on the top of Mount Zion, And we read of three, or rather four degrees of leveled floors, or courts, by which they went up unto it. The first and lowermost had seven steps, by which they ascended from the level or floor, or plain within the utmost wall, to the exterior court for the people. For that pretended court for the Gentiles has no such sufficient warrant for it: Or, if any were to be so called, it was that next within the utmost wall. The Gentiles (when they became proselytes, at leastwise the proselytes, not of the gate, but of the court, as in aftertimes they were distinguished) had the same privileges in God's worship with the Jews. The number of seven steps, though not here mentioned, yet are set down in the North and South gates of this court, ver. 22,26. The second ascent was from the level of this exterior court, to the level of the inner and middle court, for the Priests and Levites; and this was by eight steps, ver. 31,34,37. The third ascent was from the level of the inner court, to the level of the inmost court of the Temple itself; proper it seems to the Priests only. The number of these steps are not set down: But may seem to be gathered from those of the former court. Lastly, there was another ascent from that inmost court, to the porch of the Temple, and Temple itself, ver. 49. The ascent from court to court, or from level to level, was only by the steps: For the gates and buildings in them were of the same level with their courts; and so the steps or stairs, were not behind the gates, or in the thickness of the walls; but before the walls and gates. And therefore he measures here the threshold, after he went up the stairs. These steps and stairs here, no doubt, were not high, and large steps; but easy ones, such as men and cattle for sacrifice might easily ascend. See Annotations on Vers. 17.

*{And measured the Threshold of the Gate}* The Hebrew word here used, signified the threshold properly so called; and also the upper lintel; and likewise the side posts of the gate. Which of these is here understood, is dubious; and accordingly the minds of interpreters various. We conceive, that the word here is taken in its proper signification, for the threshold on the ground. Yet some would understand it so, as including a building of wall thereupon, of the same breadth, and thickness; which might serve as for other uses, so for side posts to the gate. And so the two thresholds on the grounds and the walls on them, (being of the same breadth and thickness with the wall of the court) they conceive them to run from the wall of this court, along by the East side; that is, from South toward the North, one threshold, one reed; and from North towards the South, the other threshold, another reed; And thus shall the middle space be left open, for the entry of the gate: And the chambers within the gate, on each side, being each of the same measure, reeds apiece, shall run in a manner in a line along with these thresholds, and walls built thereupon; and leave still the way in the middle open for passage: and this also is conceived, will best agree with the rest of the buildings and measures mentioned in this gate, and porch. And so likewise do they understand the threshold of the gate, by the porch of the gate within, ver. 7. Or rather, taking it in its most proper signification for the threshold only of the gate, as the very letter of the text runs; then these thresholds, being reeds apiece, had a two-leafed door, meeting in the middle, upon a strong post wherein they shut, and so left the breadth of the entry, of the wideness of the opening of the gate, ten cubits; as it is ver. 11. These thresholds likely were a little lifted above the earth or floor. The height of this gate is set down, vers. 11. Of this, and all the other gates, see that in the Psal. 87.2.

**Verse 7.** *{And every little Chamber}* Within the wall, towards the Temple, was a reed square. So Vers. 12. as, say some, was the threshold and wall upon it, Ver. 6. and so both kept one breadth. It may be doubtful, whether the chambers were each a reed square within the wall, or without. And yet the inner walls of them might run level with the threshold, and wall of the East gate; and the back walls go further out, Northward and Southward. Of their number, and use, see Ver. 10. and the Annotations upon it.

*{and between the little Chambers were five cubits}* The chambers then were not continued together, nor contiguous; but a space betwixt each of them. Some conceive it best, to agree with the letter and words of the text, and with the due consideration of the length of the whole gate, porch and portal, from entrance to end, from East to West, mentioned to be fifty cubits, vers. 15. to understand here five cubits to be between each chamber; and four of those intervals to be on each side. Thus, five cubits between the East wall, and the first chamber; five between the first chamber and the second; five cubits between the second chamber and the third; and five between the third chamber, and the inner gate Westward; so reckoning in all, twenty cubits on each side of the gate between the chambers. And then these several spaces of five cubits apiece, most likely, were taken up with several posts or pilasters, arched on the top, reaching from one post to another, of that breadth apiece of five cubits, as is intimated, vers. 10. And under three of those arches, on either side, were the three chambers placed. Or, possibly the posts might be much less for breadth and thickness, and the far greater part of the five cubits, left for cloisters, or entries into the North and South walks, galleries, or cloisters, that lay within by the sides of the court. Others, understand the distances, or intervals between the chambers, to be but two on a side; and make each of them, to be five cubits. Yet others, understand but five cubits in all, between three chambers on a side. And so other five, between the other three chambers on the other side of the gate. Supposing so the first chamber to join to the East wall, and the third chamber to join to the inner gate Westward; and the middle chamber, to have two cubits and a half on the East side of it, and two cubits and a half on the West side of it. In these uncertainties, men may sooner say their mind, than safely determine, and soundly prove it.

*{and the threshold of the gate, by the porch of the gate within was one reed}* That is, the inner gate, Westward, answering to the outward gate, Eastward, (between which two, the porch, or entry-house, and the chambers were) had the like thresholds, and of the same measure, as the outward gate had. Here one of the thresholds, by analogy of number, or synecdoche of one part for the whole, is only mentioned; implying, the other to be equally understood. See Annotations on vers. 6.

**Verse 8.** *{He measured also the porch of the gate within, one reed}* The threshold of the inner gate was measured, vers. 7. The porch of it, here; both of one measure: these two distinct names, and relations, being contained in the same building. See Annotations on vers. 6. There were two porches, after the gate of the first entrance; the exterior porch, and the inner porch. The exterior, vers. 6. And this here; of the same length, from North to South, with the opening or light of the gate itself, ten cubits; and of the same breadth, from East to West, with the breadth or thickness of the wall of the court, which on the inside of it joined to the chambers: and this was one reed, as is here mentioned. This was, as it were, [unclear Greek], *πυλάριον, πρόστοον,* the outward portal, porch, or entry. And this place seems to lie open to the sky; for there is no mention of any roof over it; but only it is barely measured; and the eaves of the chambers reach into it; and the exterior front of the inner porch, is beautifully garnished; which would not be so, if the outward porch did cover it, by joining to it. The inner porch is meant, and mentioned in the next verse.

**Verse 9.** *{then measured he the porch of the gate eight cubits}* Some conceiving, in these words, a kind of relation had to the length of the whole porch, from East to West, understand them thus, That the posts therein sustaining the arches (between which the chambers were) were at eight cubits distance one from another. Others, that this porch stood crossing, as it were, over the way, that went forward, as you passed through it, to go straight up to the Temple. And it was eight cubits over, from North to South. And that the posts thereof being two cubits, did make it answerable to the breadth of the entry of the outward gate, mentioned after, vers. 11. And indeed, ten cubits is the constant measure of the breadth of all the entrances and entries, in the gates, porches, courts of the Temple, vers. 22,24,28,32,35. Chap. 41.2. But here the easier, and fairer understanding of the word, is, That this porch exceeded the other porch, and other parts of this body of building; in greatness, being eight cubits; and in the garnishing of the frontispieces, both East and West, being more beautified and preciously garnished.

*{and the porch of the gate was inward}* Or, This was the porch of the inner gate; so measured as aforesaid.

**Verse 10.** *{and the little chambers}* Three on each side, stood along the way, on the right and left hand. The measures and quantities of them were set down, vers. 7. These were for the porters to dwell in, to observe and watch the gate, on both sides; to keep that no unclean person, or any other, forbid by the Law, might enter in, 2 Chro. 23.19. These porters were of the Levites; and, were many in number. We read of four thousand, divided by David into courses, 1 Chro. 23.5,6. and assigned by lots, to the several gates, Eastward, Northward, Southward, and Westward, 1 Chr. 26.13——18. They were keepers of the doors, gates, entries. And this they did by wards, by their courses: after seven days still new came. And they lodged round about the house of God, because the charge was upon them; and the opening of the doors thereof every morning, pertained to them. They were also over the treasuries of the house of God; had charge of the ministering vessels, and other holy things; and were in many services, about the sacrifices and offerings, under the Priests. Thus it was in Solomon's Temple. See 1 Chron. 9. Chap. vers. 17——33.

*{And the posts had one measure}* Alike on both sides. See Annotations on vers. 7.

**Verse 11.** *{and he measured the breath of the entry}* See Annotations on vers. 9. The breadth of the first entry, or entering in of that outward East gate; from North to South; or from the one hand to the other: And not the breadth of any other particular place, or space that was within the entrance.

*{and the length of the gate thirteen cubits}* The length of the gate from the bottom to the top, which indeed is the height of the gate, is here rightly understood; and that by the common consent of interpreters.

**V. 12.** *{The space also before the little chambers was one cubit}* *Space;* Heb. *limit* or *bound.* This is variously understood. Some take it for a rail, or some such partition, one cubit broad, from the wall and posts of the chamber, on both sides, running up straight along by them, towards the Temple; and make it so serve as a narrow cloister, or through passage on both sides, north and south, of the gate before the chambers. And so the narrow the entry of ten cubits, contrary to that, ver. 11.

Others, That there was the space of one cubit broad (and as much high) before the chambers, on either side of the gate, north and south; so built as to serve for seats, for the porters, and others, upon occasions to sit on: leaving the distance of the entry, between the chambers on both sides, all along, still ten cubits broad and open. Others, understand this, of one cubit only, between the wall of the threshold and entry gate, and the first chamber, on either side, north and south, at the east end. Some also of the like cubit, between the last chamber; and the wall, on either side at the west end. But we conceive the most easy, useful, and likely interpretation to be this. That this cubit served for eaves at either end, east and west, to keep off the injuries of weather, as sun and rain, on both sides of the chambers, that were on both sides of the gate. See annotations on ver. 13. The other interpretations have their difficulties, encountering, and encumbering them; of which this is more free.

*{And the little Chambers were}* Every chamber on both sides was six cubits; as ver. 7. not six cubits standing inward, and distant so far from that one cubit on either side, last mentioned; For that would cross the measures and frames of the buildings formerly mentioned.

**V. 13.** *{He measured then the gate}* The breadth of the gate which he repeats in every of the gates following, ver. 21, 25, 29, 33, 36. Some here would run this reckoning of five and twenty cubits east and west, from one utmost bound of the chambers, to the other, all along the doors of the six chambers, on both sides of the gate; And so make it five and twenty cubits, on each side thus; The three chambers eighteen cubits; between them five cubits; and one cubit at the east end; and another at the west end of the chambers, for the eaves: The three chambers not having each a several roof, but one continued roof being to serve them all three. Others would run them east and west, from the front of this gate, (& so of the others) to the end of its portal, thus; In the breadth or thickness of the wall six cubits; the gatehouse as it were, or porter's lodge, six cubits; The portal itself eight cubits: Thresholds of the portal at each end two cubits; Eaves for the front of the gate, and the west end of the portal, each half a cubit: In all five and twenty. But what are these to the breadth of the gate? The better reckoning seems to be taken from north to south; for so lies the breadth of the gate. And then the breadth of five and twenty cubits from the roof of one chamber to the roof of another, from the outmost parts of them, seems to be taken up thus; The breadth of the gate ten cubits; The breadth of the chambers on either side of the gate, six cubits; so on both sides twelve cubits: and one cubit and a half the thickness of the back walls of the chambers, on the north side, and so on the south side: In all, five and twenty cubits; from the outmost point north, to the outmost point south, as aforesaid: which is the breadth of the whole building of the gate.

*{door against door}* Not meant of the door of the great east gate, being over against the door of the inner gate, lying west to it; and corresponding to it. But this is meant of the little doors of the little chambers, last named, on the south and north sides of the gate; which either did in a direct line face the one the other: Or, as some more probably, understand them, That the doors to each chamber were two, and so framed, that door was against door, in a line running from east to west; so that the doors standing open, one might see through the three chambers, from end to end, at one sight. And that thus it was on both sides of the gate.

**V. 14.** *{He made also posts of sixty Cubits}* *He made;* that is, by measuring showed, or designed to be made.

*{posts}* Heb. *a post.*

*{Posts of sixty Cubits}* Here the Jews cry for the help of Elias. And here many learned men slide over, or slightly touch upon this: and that with undue, if not unlikely satisfaction to themselves, and others. Some here conceit a number of posts, of sixty cubits high, pitched on end, in the courts; But know not to what end or use. Some speak of a threefold row of posts, or pillars, and three stories built upon them, one above another; each post in each story twenty cubits high; and they standing just one upon another, the floors only between them, they may be so reckoned, as if high sixty cubits a piece. And this they apply to the porches or galleries round about the court; and to the buildings and chambers over them. But the Prophet comes not yet into the court, till ver. 17. And these posts here do but reach unto the (first) post of the court, as the words express it, These words then seem to be confined to the buildings of the gate; taking in it the full length and breadth of it. And then must be understood, either for the fronts of the gate, sixty cubits high; or for certain eaves, to cover some part of the buildings of the gate, and running along sixty cubits: and so posts are interpreted, eaves; and the sixty cubits covered by them, are resolved so many, and no more; without any ground for it. And both these very hardly and harshly. Or else, as by some, for a cloister, or walks, supported with these posts, running upon the ground, from the east gate, before the chambers, up to the opening and entrance into the court before it, towards the Temple. But this shows nothing how, or which way these posts should be of sixty cubits. Or, if they apply the number to the length of the cloister, they so force the words; and lengthen the gate ten cubits beyond its due. Neither does this conceit stand and agree anything well with the other measures and buildings within the gate formerly mentioned. We rather do humbly conceive, (and some such thing, some learned men do seem to intimate,) That in analogy and correspondency to all the buildings round about the courts, there were two stories built above this gate, (and so above the others, ver. 20, 24, 28, 32, 35.) of the full length, and breadth of the gate; one story above another. The posts whereof are here only mentioned: as we meet with divers such abbreviations; over and above that we find many things clean omitted in this short and intricate description, as in due place shall appear) And these posts reckoning from the ground, in such manner as formerly was touched, were each sixty cubits high. And without this construction made, there will appear no small disproportion, and indecorum between the buildings of the gates, and the buildings of the courts adjoining. But thus the buildings of the gates will bear equal height with the buildings about the courts. Yet some reckon these sixty cubits thus; that is the roof of the chambers, with the eaves on the one side, five and twenty cubits; on both sides fifty. And ten cubits the inner porch; as the outward porch was, ver. 11. In all sixty. And that these posts, frontispieces, or forefronts did compass the building.

*{even unto the Post of the Court round about the Gate}* Of this court, see v. 17. This court had posts round about it; and buildings on them. And these joined on both sides of the gate; each gate being in the middle of its own wall.

**V. 15.** *{And from the face}* This is the length of the gate from east to west. And so fifty cubits are constantly reckoned the length of the other gates, ver. 21, 25, 29, 33, 36. These fifty cubits are reckoned by all from east to west: yet for the parcels and particulars, they are clean omitted by some. By others reckoned up; but very uncertainly and obscurely. Some do make this sum up thus; viz. Eighteen cubits the three chambers; twenty cubits the four spaces, or else pillars or posts between them three chambers, and walls at the east and west end of them: Those spaces, or else pillars being five cubits a piece. And six cubits a piece the thickness of the said east and west walls. So eighteen, twenty, and twelve, make up the fifty cubits. And all these covered with buildings. Others reckon thus; from face to face fifty cubits; and make the porch of the inner gate to be ten cubits more, Others, from the face of the gate of the entrance, standing in the outside of the wall encompassing the court, unto the inner gate, opening into the court itself fifty cubits, thus; Eighteen the chambers; five the middle spaces, and two the eaves: All these five and twenty, and all covered. And the other five and twenty from beyond the inner porch, to the entrance of the first court, uncovered and open to the sky. And so they make the portal of one reed, to stand before the face of the gate of the entrance, and not to be included in this number. See annotations on ver. 8. In these uncertainties and obscurities, we can get little, or no light; out of Moses' Tabernacle, or Solomon's Temple; because the courts and gates of them are so scantily touched there; which Ezekiel here does so much, and so long insist upon.

**V. 16.** *{And there were narrow windows}* Having dispatched all the parts of the structure of the gate; Now in this place he comes to describe the windows for light, and palm trees, for ornament and garnishing of the parts of the building.

*{Narrow windows}* That is, narrowed: most likely, placed in the middle of the thickness of the wall; and broader, and wider, both outward and inward: the wall being to this end sloped on both sides, both within and without; the better to gather and receive the light abroad; and to disperse and dilate it within. And these windows haply were like our lattice windows as the original word seems to import, darting in the light: or else, they were with leaves to open and shut; or else with glass, or some such thing, closed and shut up, 2 King. 6.4. These windows for light, may shadow out that light of spiritual illumination, and joy, whereby God shines in the hearts of his people, 1 Thess. 5.16. Rom. 5.2. Phil. 2.17.

*{To the little Chambers}* On which parts of them, we know not.

They might be towards the cloisters, on the east and west sides of the middle chambers.

*{and to their Posts within the gate round about}* Posts, or rather upper lintels; so ver. 9, 10. that is, in the walls above the doors, to give light to the inferior parts of the chambers; the doors, as some say, opening towards, or into the main entry and passage, on both sides ver. 13. Or else the words for posts, may signify the cloisters, arched from post to post, between the chambers. And these were within the gate, round about, or, on both sides; and windows were made to them. But they seem rather to be intended in the next words.

*{and likewise to the Arches}* Or, *Galleries,* or, *Porches;* those specified in the last words of the former annotation.

*{and windows were round about inward}* Windows on both sides, as has been showed; inward, or within the gate; besides the great light of the east, and west ends; or doors of the outward, and inward gate. All which gave abundant light to all the chambers, places, and passages within the whole gate.

*{and upon each Posts were Palm trees}* Engraven for ornament, and beauty on each post, or pillar, or front, and chapiter of the pillar, or tops of the wall within on both sides. This ornament of palm trees may shadow out to us, our ensigns of triumph and victory over the world, the flesh, and the devil, Rom. 8.31–37. Apoc. 20.11. and 7.9. 1 Cor. 15.54, 55. And thus he has wholly finished the east gate, with the buildings in it.

**V. 17.** *{Then brought he me}* The man, ver. 3. did it.

*{into the outward Court}* Here he first enters out of the gate into the court; going on westwards towards the Temple. The gate and buildings in this formerly mentioned, stood in this outward court; we mean within this second wall. And now he is passed between them, and got through them, into the outward court itself; to see what is behind these buildings; and what space before them, and betwixt this and the next court; and what compass on either hand him, towards north and south.

Now in Moses' Tabernacle, we read but of one court: and that was long, from east to west an hundred cubits: Broad from north to south, fifty cubits; and high all about five cubits. And but of one gate in it, of twenty cubits wide, placed in the middle of the east side, Exod. 27. and 38. chapters. And all this, and things in it, were under the hands and charge of the sons of Gershom and Merari: And to the sons of Kohath belonged the charge of the Tabernacle, itself, Num. chap. 3. and 4. In Solomon's Temple we read of two courts; and they so named, and numbered, 2 King. 21.5. and 23.12. 2 Chron. 33.5. The one, the inner court, or the court of the priests, or the court of the house of the Lord, or before the house of the Lord, 1 King. 6.36. and 7.12. and 8.64. 2 Chron. 4.9. and 7.7. The other called the great court, 1 King. 7.9, 12. 2 Chron. 4.9. which was for the people. A new court is mentioned, 2 Chron. 20.5. But that is, the old renewed by Asa, when he renewed the altar of the Lord, 2 Chron. 15.8. Or else, as some would have it, the court of the people, newly divided by a partition wall, to distinguish between men, and women, in it. And a middle court is named also, 2 King. 20.4. But the margin reads, city, for court: showing the word signifies both. And so city, the sense of the place might seem to carry it; for there was a double city of Jerusalem, (the word is of the dual number) beside that called the city of David. And these, part in the tribe of Judah, and part in Benjamin. Compare together Jud. 1.8. 2 Sam. 5.6, 7, 8, 9. 2 King. 22.14. But chiefly, That place seems to import the middle court of King Hezekiah's palace: And that considering the person of Isaiah, and the place where the king lay sick, and Isaiah delivered his message. And of such a like court we read, 1 King. 7. ver. 8. Of an outmost court for the Gentiles, we read not. But some labor to gather it out of, 1 King. 8.41, 42, 43. Esay 56.3, 4, 5. Mar. 11.17.

Now Ezekiel's Temple had more courts. And they are, by divers men, diversely numbered and named. 1 The court or plain within the outmost wall. 2 The outward court, so called, in relation to the inner court. 3 The inner court. 4 The inmost court. Within this, the Temple was, with its porch and structures of chambers, joining to the outsides of the Temple, and of the Oracle, round about. And some add two courts more toward the west. And the first of all these, they make to be for the Gentiles; the second, for the people of Israel that were clean, according to the Levitical Law. The third, for the Levites and priests, and their accoutrements and employments; wherein the altar of burnt-offering was. And the fourth, for the priests only. Yet most writers do reckon, but upon three courts, viz. The first court within the outmost wall: the second, called here the exterior, the outward court, in reference to the inner court; this being the court of the people. The third, called the inner court, belonging to the priests; and in part also to the people, in their services and sacrifices. But to these must be added the fourth court, wherein the Temple was; and that properly to the priests only; as the chapter following does evince. And these four courts also, besides very many other arguments, do evince, that this Temple of Ezekiel is not the same with Solomon's; contrary to the most common tenet of the most learned Papists. Which error of theirs, together with the captivating of their judgments, to the letter of their vulgar Latin text, does much mislead them in the true literal understanding and interpretation of this vision and Temple. Now by court here, we must understand and comprehend, not only the plain floor, lying open to the sky; but also porches about it and their chambers, and other buildings, as the text shows. And these several courts, with the risings and varieties of them, may put us in mind of the diversities of gifts and graces, and offices, in the several members of Christ's mystical body here: and also of several degrees of glory in the courts and mansions of heaven; as there are stars in several spheres; and stars of several magnitudes in the fixed firmaments. Of these courts, see Psal. 84.2, 10.

*{and lo there were Chambers}* Being now in the east floor, and open plain of the outer exterior court, he sees and observes.

*{Chambers}* Cells, repositories, storehouses. For the priests and Levites to dwell in, while in their turns, they kept the service of the Temple; and wherein they did rest and repose themselves; and laid up in them part of the tithes, offerings and other things, belonging to them from the people; and of the frankincense and vessels, and other things belonging to the Temple, and the service of it. See Neh. 13.5, 9, 12, 13. And haply, into these might the people also retire themselves for accommodation, the better to order and compose themselves, and their business about their offerings, and sacrifices of all sorts. Yea, some make these to belong to the use of the people only. Of these chambers, of this, or the other courts, we read not anything in the structure of Solomon's Temple; but what is in David's preparation, and provision for his son Solomon, on this behalf, 1 Chron. 28.11, 12, 13. and that in chap. 9.26, 33. and chap. 23.28. And of them, we read also mention to be made, 2 King. 23.12. 2 Chron. 31.11. and Jer. 35.2, 4. and chap. 36.10.

*{and a pavement made}* For ornament and use; of stone and porphyry most likely; and curiously wrought with checker-work, as some translations have it. See chap. 42.3. 2 Chron. 7.3. See Ahasuerus' pavement, Esth. 1.6. And the use of this was to prevent dust, and dirt.

*{round about}* The pavement was all over the court, and the chambers; round about the court and pavement.

*{thirty chambers}* Fifteen on either side of this east gate, that is, northward and southward. And as some think, fifteen also on either side of the north, and of the south gates, eastward and westward.

*{upon the pavement}* Or, *about the pavement,* or built over the pavement, above, and upon it.

**V. 18.** *{Pavement}* That last mentioned.

*{by the side of the gates}* Not that pavement, within the first, and outmost breast-wall, lying before the east gate, of the second wall and exterior court, as some would have it. But this lies within the second wall, and exterior court; and by the side of the gates of that court.

*{gates}* So plurally, says the text. And the 20. and 24. verses, show, that it had a north gate, and a south gate, besides this east gate, all within this outward (not outmost) wall, or court.
