Which gates, running fifty cubits from the wall into it, did further occasion a division; and break this one exterior court, into more particular and lesser courts.

*{over against the length of the gates}* Not then the pavement, lying in the length of the gates, between the chambers; and other buildings of each gate; as some would have it. But that lying by the sides of the gate, that is, the north and south sides of this east gate; and so also correspondently the east, and the west sides, of the north and of the south gates, of this exterior and outward court: And these were directly, and punctually over against the lengths of these gates, butting thereon: and lying within the pointings in, & ends of the entrances from the three gates. The lengths of these gates were fifty cubits, running from the wall into the courts; counting therein the thickness of the wall. And that same length, was the breadth of these pavements, which did run from each side of these gates, so compassing the court, or little courts. The breadth then, we say, of these lateral pavements, was equal with the lengths of the gates. And the same was the constant breadth of all the porches, or galleries, that were round about all the courts; and built upon, or over and above those pavements. They were made as broad, as the gates were long. Their length was a great deal more, as will after appear.

*{the lower pavement}* Lower then that pavement, in the inner and upper court of the priests. Or, lower then that other part of this pavement, which lay in this same court, from under the porches or galleries, open to the sky; and likely, a little higher then this; or lower in the two sides, then in the middle. This pavement, haply, being a little convex in the middle, and a little declive, or declining on the two sides.

**V. 19.** *{Then he measured the breadth from}* The breadth; that is, the broad flat, and plain floor of this inward court.

*{from the fore-front of the lower gate}* From the inner, and western front of it. For each gate had two forefronts. And so leaving out the length of the gate itself out of this measure.

*{of the lower gate}* That is, of this east gate of the outward court, formerly measured: which is lower then the gate of the inner court, as the steps of ascent show, from the one to the other.

*{unto the fore-front of the inner court}* That is, the east forefront of the priests' court; and of the gate of it.

*{without}* Or from without, that is, leaving out likewise the gate itself, and the length of it out of this measure.

*{an hundred cubits Eastward}* That is, between the two gates, lay this broad plain, in the open air, and hundred cubits from east to west.

*{and Northward}* That is, so much also from north to south, even an hundred cubits. No other ways can this measure be taken or made. The plain so being, not only quadrangular, but a perfect quadrant or square, equilateral. This was the east flat or floor. The like was that of the north, ver. 23. and that of the south, ver. 27. For from each of these gates, to their opposite gates, lay an hundred cubits between. The meaning is, that he measured that floor, or flat, open to the sky, that lay between the gates of the outward and inner courts, on the east side; excluding the gates themselves; and found it to be an hundred cubits broad, and long.

**V. 20.** *{And the Gate}* This north gate, and likewise the south gate, ver. 24. are in all points and particulars, so like, or rather the same with the east gate, formerly described, that there needs little, or no further explanation of them. All things for buildings, parts, measures, ornaments, are common to them all. See ver. 6, 9, 10, 11, 12, 13, 14, 15, 16.

**V. 21.** *{And the little Chambers}* See ver. 7, 10, 11, 13, 15.

*{first gate}* East gate: and so named, ver. 22.

**V. 22.** *{And their windows}* See ver. 16.

*{seven steps}* And so in the south gate of this outward court. Which evinces the number of the stairs in the east, to be likewise seven, ver. 6. though they be not there named: they being all three gates of the same court, and the gates, and court, or little courts in it, being of one level and height. By these steps they came up from the floors of the lower court, to the gates of the higher court. See annotations on ver. 6. And this, with all the other steps in the other courts, and to the porch of the Temple, may mind us of our increasing, rising, and ascending from step to step, from degree to degree, in knowledge, faith, virtue, and all saving graces, 2 Pet. 1.5. still climbing up towards heaven, and God's Temple there, Psal. 84.7. See also, 1 Cor. 13.9, 10, 12. Eph. 4.12, 13, 14, 16.

**V. 23.** *{And the Gate}* The north gate of the inner court was right over against the north gate of the outward court.

*{and toward the East}* And so it was toward the east; that is, the east gate of the inner court, was right over against the east gate of the outward court.

*{an hundred cubits}* That is, between the two north gates, that of the outward, and that of the inner court, was a space of an hundred cubits long, and broad; a just square, altogether like that, ver. 19. The north flat, or floor or little court, was of the same spaciousness with that of the east.

**V. 24.** *{After that he brought me}* The south gate of the outward court. See ver. 21.

**V. 25.** *{and there were windows}* See ver. 22. and 21.

**V. 26.** *{and seven steps}* See ver. 22, 16.

*{and the Arches thereof were before them}* The steps also before the gates may seem to have their posts, arches, and garnishing of palm trees.

**V. 27.** *{And there was a gate}* See ver. 23.

*{an hundred Cubits}* That is southward, and eastward. See ver. 23.

**V. 28.** *{And he brought me to the Inner-court}* So that here is no gate mentioned, on the west quarter, of this outward court; nor yet on the west quarter of the inner court. Josephus records that in his time, there was no gate on the west quarter. Joseph. of the War of the Jews, lib. 6. chap. 6. yet it was otherwise in Solomon's Temple, 1 Chron. 9.24. and chap. 26.14–18. This inner court is within the third wall, and in the court of the priests. And this gate here measured, is the south gate of it. He comes to it directly from the south gate of the outward court.

*{To these Measures}* To the measures of the three former gates in the outward court. And as the gates, so the porches, and arches of the inner court were equal, and of the same measure with the porches and arches of the outward court.

**V. 29.** *{And the little Chambers thereof}* See ver. 7, 10, 13, 15, 21, 25.

**V. 30.** *{And the Arches round about were five and twenty Cubits long}* Some here expound *long,* to be meant *broad;* & five cubits, to be five tens or decades of cubits, alleging the like ellipsis to be the 2 Sam. 6.4. and chap. 10.17. And so, by the last word, *broad,* they understand *long.* And thus the measure of the arches would fall in with the measure in the former verse, and be the same with it; that is, fifty cubits long, and five and twenty cubits broad. And some are so forward for this, that they stick not to charge the text itself with corruption, through error of copies, and default of the scribes; and would have it altered to their opinion aforesaid. But this offers open violence and injury to the sacred text. And that their exposition would seem to be but a needless repetition, falling in with that which is so often set down before. Others therefore understand it thus, That the posts which did bear up the arches within the gates, (for what is said here of one gate, they apply also to the rest of the gates,) and were between the three chambers, on each side of the gates, were long, or high in the top of their arches 25 cubits; and broad in their bottoms or settles at least, five cubits. But besides other things, the next words following in the next verse, that the arches were towards the utter or outward court, do seem to us to cross this interpretation. Others would bring these arches also within the gates. But yet with much more intricacies and difficulties, what arches they should be, and how to fit these measures to them. We rather conceive, That as the Prophet, after finishing the description, and measuring of the first gate in the outward court was brought into the court itself, to see it described; so here, after the description of the first gate in the inner court, he is brought now out of the gate, into the inner court itself, to see it in part described; and finds here the arches of those pillars, round about the inner court, to be 25 cubits long, or high; and five cubits broad a piece. And these arches to look towards the outward court, as it follows in the next words; which they could no ways do, if they stood within the gate.

**V. 31.** *{And the Arches thereof were towards the outward Court}* Herein this inner court differs from the outward. There the arches were before them, ver. 22, 26. that is, forward: Here the arches were toward the outward court, that is, looked backward. And this evinces the truth of the exposition of the former verse.

*{and Palm trees}* See annotations on ver. 16, 22, 26.

*{eight steps}* And so in the other two gates of this inner court, ver. 34, 37. And herein likewise is a difference between this, and the outward court: That had but seven steps in the ascent to it; This eight: which might amount to the height of about two yards.

**V. 32.** *{And he brought me}* The east gate of the inner court. And this lay then next in his way. See ver. 28.

**V. 33.** *{And the little Chambers}* See ver. 29.

**V. 34.** *{And the Arches}* See ver. 31.

**V. 35.** *{And he brought me}* The north gate of the inner court. And this lay next after the east gate. See ver. 28, 32.

**V. 36.** *{The little Chambers}* See ver. 33, 29.

**V. 37.** *{And the Posts}* See ver. 34, 31. Called arches there, and posts here. The wall and outward compass of the outward court, was of stone. But it seems the outward compass & bounds of the inner court, was only distinguished by posts at certain distances. And if so, the people in the outward court might see the sacrifices, and services performed by the priests in the inner court.

**V. 38.** *{And the Chambers}* Here, among the parts and measures of the fabric, he begins to intermingle the use of some of the parts; and shows, at, and about the north gate of the inner court, the use of chambers, to wash the sacrifices in, ver. 38. of tables, to slay the sacrifices on, ver. 39, 40, 41, 42. and of hooks, to hang on them, and their parts, ver. 43.

*{Chambers}* Some imagine one, some two, one upon each side of the gate; some six, viz. three on each side: understanding them of the three little chambers, on each side, within the north gate; whereof ver. 36. But these seem to be other chambers, without any certain number set down, built for this use; with vessels of brass, with water in them, to wash therein the burnt-offerings and sacrifices, their entrails and flesh: and seated by the posts of the gates; either those, by the cloisters, which run east and west, along from the two sides of the north gate, and faced those parts of the inner court; or else, those by the entries into the open flat, or floor of the inner court; which were close by the other, and under the fronts of the arches of that court; and in those vacant places between the bounding posts mentioned in the former verse. Yet all this only on the north quarter of this court; as the text seems to give it: and not round about all the quarters of it, as some upon probabilities do conjecture.

*{washed}* In Solomon's Temple we read of a molten sea, for the priests to wash in; and of ten lavers of brass, to wash in them such things as they offered for the burnt-offerings, 1 King. 7.23, 38. 2 Chron. 4.2, 6. And those may seem to be placed in the Temple, 1 King. 7.39. However, yet herein appears a difference between Solomon's, and Ezekiel's Temple. We read but of one laver in Moses' Tabernacle, Exod. 30.18, 19, 20, 21. and chap. 40.30. and that was between the Tabernacle of the Congregation and the altar.

**V. 39.** *{And in the porch}* We have here place, number, matter, form, measure, and use of the tables, in this and the three verses following. The place and situation of them is the most difficult.

*{in the porch of the gate}* Some place the tables here, even all the eight tables, within the gate; and make the sacred slaughter to be acted there; partly in the very porch of the gate, in the very threshold, and exterior part of it: and partly in the more inward parts of it. But we conceive this place to be too narrow, and full of inconveniences, for such a purpose. And finding these other expressions of the place, at the side without, at the porch of the gate, ver. 40. by the side of the gate, four on this side, and four on that side; we rather understand thereby, not the inward, but the outward sides of the porch and the gate, by the cloisters, which run east and west from the two outsides of the north gate, hard by the washing chambers, and under covert of the cloisters; so to save the priests from the injuries of weather, wind, sun, & rain, whiles they were slaying and preparing the sacrifices; and to prevent disturbances, which in the gate were likely to fall out.

*{Two tables}* Here begins the number of the tables.

*{on this side}* Both, not insides; but outsides; leaving the gate, entrance, and passage free.

*{to slay}* So ver. 41, 42. and to lay the flesh of the offering, on them, ver. 43. and the instruments for the slaughter, ver. 42. Here is the use of these tables. The sacrifice then was not slain close by the side of the altar, but on the north side of it, agreeing therein, with that. Lev. 1.11.

*{the sin-offering, and the trespass-offering}* Levit. 4, 5, 6. and 7. chapters.

**V. 40.** *{And at the side}* As one goes up from without to the entry of the north gate. And therefore they mistake, that place these tables, mentioned in this verse, in the south gate.

**V. 41.** *{Four tables}* The number, site, and use of all the tables. Though the text affords no more, than is said; yet men will raise various opinions, where any the least probability may possibly appear, and give them advantage. And so, some place these tables in the open court, and open air; Some make them in number twelve; that is four for the whole burnt-offerings, for the constant burnt-offering, and for the sin-offering, and trespass-offering of the congregation, ver. 42. And eight more for other sacrifices of particular men, in this verse. Some others make them sixteen; some, four and twenty; assigning to each of the three gates eight a piece. Some (which is strange) deny that there was any table at the north gate. But these all do sooth up themselves in their own fancies, without any warrant from the text.

**V. 42.** *{And the four tables}* Not other four besides the former eight, as some would have it. But four of them, or rather, the four tables, on either side, in such manner as they are reckoned in the former verse, that is, all the eight tables.

*{of hewen stone}* The matter, and form of the tables. It may be of one stone a piece. However, they were of hewn stone, well wrought, and polished; not much unlike those, 1 King. 7.9.

*{of a Cubit and a half long}* These tables may seem very little on the square top, for the uses whereunto they are assigned; unless cubits be here taken to be of the largest measure. A place of special note for this purpose. See annotations on ver. 5.

*{The Instruments}* Knives, basins, bowls, and such other. See Ezra 1.9, 10. Some make four of the tables to be for instruments, and the other four for sacrifices, and flesh of the offerings, ver. 43.

**V. 43.** *{and within were hooks}* Some translate end-irons; some ledges at the edges of the tables, an handbreadth high, and turning inward, to keep in the instruments, and flesh, that lay on the tables, from sliding, or falling off. Some, tripets, or brand-irons, or fire-stones, set two and two together, to set pots or kettles upon, and to keep in the fire. See Psal. 68.13. But we have it translated hooks; and so the word bears. And that best agrees with the business in hand. Hooks then were in the slaughterhouses, where the tables were. And the word being of the dual number, seems to imply, that two tangs were made upon one stem, an handbreadth one from another: and these were fastened round about the wall, at convenient distances, that if need were, the two tangs of one hook might hold two several beasts' legs upon them. These were necessary for the flaying, and quartering of the beasts, and other uses.

*{offering}* The word *Corban,* is here in the Hebrew. The word used by our Savior, Mar. 7.11. and whence *Corbana* is used for treasury, Mat. 27.9.

**V. 44.** *{And without the inner-gate}* Or, rather, *without at the inner-gate.* Here are various translations, and interpretations; and the place is obscure, through such brevity as the Hebrew language uses. However, the learned Papists profess, That they will stick here to their vulgar Latin, and interpret accordingly, though they acknowledge the Hebrew differs from it: which indeed is their grand error, as we have said, and ground of their mistakes, in their expositions of this vision.

*{were the Chambers of the singers in the inner Court}* Sure it is, that the Prophet still here speaks of the inner court of the priests and Levites; and hitherto in the north gate of it, he measured the gate itself, and spoke of the chambers for washing, and places for slaughter. Tables, and hooks, at the sides of that gate. Now he comes from without the inner gate, into the inner court itself, and there sees the chambers of the singers, in this verse; of the priests, the keepers of the charge of the house, ver. 45. and of the priests the keepers of the charge of the altar, ver. 46. The number of these chambers, and the right places of them, in what quarters of this court they are situated, is the greatest point of difficulty. Some make two chambers here to be only spoken of; and them for the singers only, attending the charge of the house, and of the altar. Some make only four chambers. In the inner court, two for the singers, and two for the priests. The priests did offer sacrifice to God. And the singers, during the sacrifice, did offer unto him the sacrifice and song of praise. And of these men, some make these four chambers to be for the singers and priests to dwell in, successively by their weeks, as they attend the service. Others, not for habitation, but for the keeping of the musical instruments, and the vessels, and things requisite for the sacrifices. Other learned men to each of the three gates, both in the outward, and in the inner court, do assign ten chambers, five on the one side, and five on the other side, of each gate. Whence arises the number of 30 in the outward court, mentioned ver. 17. And that in these the ministers of the Temple did dwell by turns, each according to their several degrees, orders, and offices. And that those ten chambers, by this north gate, of the inner court, were for the singers: Those by the east gate were for the priests, the keepers of the charge of the altar: And those by the south gate, were for the priests, the keepers of the charge of the house. Others yet, do place these chambers mentioned in this, and the two verses following, at the back side (*latus* or *dorsum*) of the aforesaid buildings of the north gate of this inner court, joining to the partition wall, or cloisters that ran on the north quarter, between the outward, and this inner court; and so make them opening toward the south. And they assign these chambers to the priests, the keepers of the charge of the house, as it is ver. 45. and likewise one at the side of the east gate; that is, one row or rank of chambers, at the back side of the buildings of the east gate, viz. on the north side of these buildings: which stood not with their backs to the wall, or partition between the courts, as it was with them at the north gate; but with their backs to those buildings of the east gate, that ran along from east to west into the court; and so their prospect and facing was toward the north: and these they assign to the priests, the keepers of the charge of the altar; as they find it, ver. 46. And lastly, some do assign the chambers from the east to the north, to the singers: from the east to the south, to the keepers of the charge of the altar. And in this great and doubtful variety of opinions, we forbear to interpose, otherwise than in the notes following.

*{singers}* We read in Moses' time, that the priests were appointed to blow with the silver trumpets over the burnt-offerings, and peace-offerings, in the solemn feast, Num. 10.10. But of singers, and music, in God's service, we read much more, in the days of David, Solomon, Hezekiah, Josiah, Zerubbabel, Ezra, and Nehemiah, David appointed them; and that from God, and by the counsel of Gad, and Nathan; for the service of song, when the Ark had rest at Jerusalem, 1 Chr. 6.31. and 16.4, 5, 6. and for the service of the Tabernacle at Gibeon, 1 Chr. 16.37, 39. he appointed four thousand of them, all Levites, 1 Chr. 23.5. Teachers 288. 1 Chr. 25.7. whereof Heman, of the Kohathites; Asaph, of the Gerihonites, on his right hand; and Ethan, of the Mezarites, on his left hand; and Jeduthun were chief, 1 Chr. 25.6. and 6.31, 33, 39, 44. and Chenaniah, the master of the song, 1 Chr. 15.22, 27. Others, their brethren, besides the 288 were scholars, or of the second degree. He divided them by lot into 24 orders, and that for the service of the Temple which Solomon was to build. They were to wait by course, in their place and station; their office was for praise and thanks to God: and that with voice, lifting up the voice with joy, and with instruments, cornets, trumpets, cymbals, psalteries, harps, horn; with the instruments of David, says the text, and with the words and songs of David, because both of his making and composure, 1 Chr. 16.7. they did thus all the time of the burnt-offering, 2 Chr. 29.25, 26, 27, 28. they were employed in that work day and night, 1 Chr. 9.33. and 16.6. we read how David used them, in the bringing up of the Ark, from the house of Obed Edom, 1 Chr. 15. Solomon at his bringing of the Ark into the Oracle, they being arrayed in white linen, having cymbals, and psalteries, and harps, stood at the east end of the altar, and with them 120 priests, sounding with trumpets, 2 Chr. 5.12. Hezekiah likewise used them; and Josiah, in their solemn reformations, and passovers. Many of them came with Zerubbabel, and Ezra, out of the captivity. Zerubbabel had them at his laying the foundation of the Temple; and Nehemiah at his dedication of the walls of Jerusalem: all use of music and songs is not excluded in God's worship and service, in the New Testament, Ephes. 5.18. Col. 3.16. 1 Cor. 14.26. See the use of music, 2 King. 3.15. 1 Chr. 25.3.

*{inner court}* Out of the gate.

*{at the side}* East and west sides of the north gate: or, at the back of the then buildings, and next the north partition wall.

*{one at the side}* One row, or rank of chambers, at the north side of the east gate, with their backs to the long side of that gate; and so their prospect was toward the north.

**V. 45.** *{This Chamber}* Or, *row of Chambers;* a change of numbers.

*{for the Priests}* We may here understand the Levites, given by God to and for the use and service of the priests, Num. 3. 1 Chr. 23.27, 30. and Chap. 24.20. and Chap. 25. and Chap. 26. See God's wrath on King Uzziah, for violation of this charge 2 Chr. 26.16. The Levites kept the charge of the house, in cleansing, the holy places whereunto they had access, and observing the laws of all the several sacrifices, 1 Chr. 23.28, 29. and being singers, 1 Chr. 23.30. and porters, 1 Chron. 26.

**V. 46.** *{Toward the North}* From the north side of the length of the east gate: that row of chambers, vers. 44.

*{the Priests}* Properly so called; as the words following import.

*{of the Altar}* About the sacrifices, and specially and particularly, the charge of the perpetual fire upon the altar; which coming down from heaven, was commanded perpetually to be preserved, and never to be suffered to go out, Levit. 6.13. and 9.24. 2 Chr. 7.1, 2. Macc. 2.10, 11. In an apish imitation whereof some ancient heathens had their vestal fire kept by their vestal virgins, which never suffered it to go out: whereof the Poet,

*Vigilemque sacraverat ignem,
excubias divum æternas, Æneid. 4.*

*{These are the sons of Zadok}* Zadock was made high priest, and put in the place and room of Abiathar, by Solomon, 1 King. 2.26, 27, 35. and he was of the right line, descended of Eleazar, the eldest son of Aaron, the ninth from him. Abiather and his ancestor Eli, and some others of that race, however they came to be high priests, yet descended but of the younger brother Ithamar: and Abiathar was deposed, to fulfill the Word of the Lord against the house of Eli. Zadock then was the right high priest. He anointed Solomon King. He first served in Solomon's Temple; and in his race, the high priesthood, afterwards continued. See Chap. 44.16, 17.

*{which come near}* Exod. 19.22. Lev. 10.3. and 21.17, 18, 21, 23.

**V. 47.** *{the court}* Inner court, between the three inner gates, the flat and floor of it.

*{an hundred cubits}* Square, equilaterad. Of the same measure where the three open flats and floors of the outward court, ver. 19, 23, 27. yea, and the gates of the both courts, all six, were all alike each other, in buildings and measures, fifty long, and five and twenty broad, each of them; so uniform was the frame of these buildings: and the porches, or galleries and chambers in both courts, most alike, though with some differences. This inner court had, it seems, but this one open flat or floor, and that lying before the three gates of it: whereas the outward court, by reason of the largeness of it, had three such; one before each gate of it. The west side of this square seems to be some small partition between this court, and the Temple.

*{and the altar that was before the house}* The *altar* before the Temple itself. To distinguish it from the *altar* within the *house,* That was the golden *altar;* this the brazen *altar:* That, to burn incense on it; This, the flesh of the sacrifices. This is described and measured, Chap. 43.13——18. Here the place of it is set down to be before the house. So Exod. 40.6, 29. That *altar* before the door of the Tabernacle of the Tent of the Congregation. This, we doubt, was placed in the very midst of this court, or open flat; over against the three gates of it, and before the house. And these gates standing in a direct line with the three gates of the *outward court,* It follows that the *gates* being open, through them, the *altar* itself, the sacrifices, the priests, and the ministerial actions, might be seen of the people in the *outward court,* yea of them within the outmost wall. Christ is our both *altar* and *sacrifice;* our salvation in the midst of the earth, Psal. 74.12. Out of all the premises in this *Court,* we may gather, That many things which this *inner court* had in common with the *outward court,* for brevity sake are here omitted. And further light will come to both these *courts,* by analogy with the court of Solomon's Temple, by a right understanding of those texts, 1 King. 6.36. and 7.12.

**V. 48.** *{And he brought me to the porch of the house}* Having done with the *courts,* he now comes to the Temple itself, and the *parts, chambers,* and *ornaments* of it: which being the whole subject of the next Chapter, might best have occasioned the beginning of it here.

*{porch}* This porch stood just west from the *altar;* and some think, 20 *cubits* distant from the former *inner court* of the priests, Chap. 41.10. and about 60. from the *altar* itself. But others rather think upon a due and joint consideration of the symmetry of all the buildings in this court of the Temple; that the stairs of this porch joined to the east bounds of this *court.* Between the *altar,* and this *porch* of the *house,* seems to be a place of prayer; some open place where the priests used to pray, Joel. 2.17. abused to idolatry, Ezek. 8.16. This porch did join to the body of the Temple, as part of it, and the entrance into it. This is not that called Solomon's porch, wherein Jesus walked, Joh. 10.23. and wherein the people ran together to Peter and John, and the cripple healed by them, Act. 3.11. and wherein the Apostles, and the people were all with one accord, Act. 5.12. That must be some porch of the *outward court.* For into this, the people, or the Apostles, or our Savior himself never entered; as not being of the tribe of Levi, and of Aaron's line, who only were to enter into it. The true door and porch is Jesus Christ, Joh. 10.9, 28. and 14.6.

*{of the house}* That is, the Temple, so called κατ’ ἐξοχὴν, as we call the Bible; as if it were the only house worthy of that name. So 1 King. 8.27. 2 Chr. 7.12. Psal. 78.69. God's house for him to dwell in. His house of prayer, Esay 56.7. Mat. 21.13.

*{and measured each post of the porch}* We have here in this and the next verse, the porch, and the length and the breadth of it; the posts of it, and their measure, and pillars by them. We have also the breadth of the gate of this porch, and steps ascending to this porch. The length and the breadth of this porch are certainly set down in the next verse, without admitting any scruple about them: yea, and the breadth of the gate likewise, in this verse; and yet great variety, and much difficulty arises among interpreters, upon other points and passages of the words. The main ground thereof is, because some reckon the length of the porch from north to south, and the breadth from east to west. Others, clean contrary. The former, reckoning the east front of the porch, running from north to south to be 20 cubits, labor to make it up divers ways. Some thus, in these particulars, *viz.* In the middle of it a door of four cubits broad: on either side of that door a gate, or leaves of a gate, to open when occasion was, three cubits on this side, and three cubits on that side; and on either side of these leaves, cloisters of five cubits long: and thus they take up the whole length or front of the porch: the 20 cubits. Others thus; a post in the middle, whereon the leaves of the door meet, two cubits broad: the breadth of the opening leaves, three cubits a piece; and the posts whereon they did hang and turn, one a piece: and the posts or wall on either side the door, five cubits a piece; in all 20. And thus passing through the porch into the house, you pass through the breadth of the porch, not the length of it. On the other side some conceive the meaning to be this; That the length of this porch stood east and west, being 20 cubits within the walls; the breadth, north and south, being 11 cubits within the walls: which breadth was taken up thus; a two leafed gate of three cubits a piece, for the light and opening of it, like one of those, Ch. 41.24. and a middle post of one cubit whereon they met and closed; and a post on either side of two cubits a piece, joined to and with the leaf, and opening of the gate, which so made up five cubits on either side the middle post, and so justly arises the sum of eleven cubits, for the breadth of the porch within the walls: and then the length of the porch remaining, will admit of no difficulty. See Annotations on Chap. 41.13.

*{post of the porch}* To distinguish it from the post of the gate which had the leaf adjoining to it.

*{three cubits}* Each leaf of the gate: and this joining to the post of it, whereon it did hang and turn, made four cubits.

**V. 49.** *{the length}* Some take it from east to west; as the length was reckoned in the east gates; and so in the body of the Temple. But this better suits with the description of Solomon's Temple, and with the whole frame and measures of this building, to be taken from north to south, along the breadth of the Temple.

*{and the breadth}* From north to south, eleven cubits, say some: But rather from east to west. The breadth of Solomon's porch was but ten cubits, 1 King. 6.3. In this, and in many other dimensions this Temple differs from that of Solomon. The height of Solomon's porch was 120 cubits, 2 Chr. 3.4.

*{by the steps}* The text sets not down the number of these steps, as it did in the two courts. And hereupon divers men take liberty to themselves for divers opinions: Some saying 15. Some 12. Some 11. Some 10. Most 8. as were in the last, and inner court; and is so expressed in their Latin translation, though without warrant of the Hebrew Canon; and Jerom himself, the author of their Latin translation, as they will have it in his comments upon this place, leaves the number doubtful.

*{pillars by the posts}* Standing by themselves, within the porch, near the wall of the Temple, as most think; as Jachin and Boaz in the porch of Solomon's Temple, 1 King. 7.21. 2 Chron. 3.17. The measures & form of which pillars are set down 1 King. 7.15, 23. 2 Chron. 3.15, 16, 17. See the Annotations on the fifth verse of this Chapter. This opinion stands well with those, who make the length of the porch to run north and south; which opinion we best allow: But it would much impeach the entrance into the Temple, if we take the breadth of the porch, being 11 cubits, to run north and south, the breadth of the door of the Temple being 10 cubits, Chap. 41.2. such therefore are forced to make these pillars to be fixed, and as it were, half incorporated into the wall, and that by the posts, (these are the words of the text) namely, of the gate in the front of the porch. See Apoc. 3.11, 12. And thus much of the porch of the Temple: whereupon we may here again gather, That divers things touching the porch, and the upper buildings of it especially, are omitted here.

## CHAPTER 41

**Verse 1.** *{Afterwards he brought me to the Temple}* He proceeds still from the outmost to the inmost, from the lower to the higher; from the porch, now to the Temple itself; the Sanctum or the body of it. This part alone is often called by the name of the Temple, the house. This is not that Temple, so named very often, in the New Testament; wherein our Savior is said to have walked; and at twelve years old to have heard the doctors, and asked them questions. Wherein he taught the people, and healed them, and wrought miracles; and through which he would not suffer any man to carry any vessel; and out of which he cast, and whipped out, and that twice, the buyers and sellers. Not that Temple, wherein the children cried Hosanna; and Anna continued fasting and praying, neither the Pharisee and Publican went up to pray; wherein the Apostles daily taught: and the believers daily continued with one accord. The Temple in those places must be meant, of the outward court of it only; here it is taken in its proper signification, for the Sanctuary, the *Sanctum,* the body, or basilick of the Temple, called the first Tabernacle, Heb. 9.2. Into which none might enter, no not the Levites themselves, but only the priests of the seed of Aaron. This Temple figured our Savior and his body, Joh. 2.19, 21. And we, and our bodies are by grace become the Temples of the Holy Ghost, 1 Cor. 6.19. and 3.16, 17. This material Temple here, the Angel begins to measure, and proceeds to the Oracle, and the parts and ornaments of them both; and to the outward chambers and buildings, which were appendixes of them. And first.

*{the Posts}* Some here would understand cloisters, standing upon posts, on either hand the gate, at the east end, before the body of the house; each six cubits broad from the wall of the house, eastward in the court; and running along the wall, five cubits on either side the gate, to the north and south corners of the house. But this in no wise can stand with the being and description of the porch already mentioned; which joined to the Temple. Some others, understand the fronts of the wall on both sides the gate. But the measure of six cubits a piece, agrees not to the fronts: and the straining of the gate to eight cubits, to make way for six on both sides, offers too great violence to the text, vers. 2. Others, understand chambers; but this carries no show of probability from the text, or structures. Others, more likely, understand the breadth of the wall, or thickness of the wall, on both sides the gate of the Temple or Sanctuary. Or, which differs not much, the posts of the gate, the north and south post, measured here in their length, or thickness from east to west; which is one reed, or six cubits; as also was the thickness of all the walls of the Temple, vers. 5. See Annotations on vers. 21.

*{which was the breadth of the tabernacle}* Some take tabernacle here for the upper threshold, or lintel of the door: which was, say they, not arched, but square or flat; and that was according to the thickness of the wall of six cubits. But this is a very hard and unheard of Catachresis, an harsh, and abusive construction of tabernacle. More fair it is to understand, that the former two sixes, of the breadth of the posts, fell in, and agreed with the breadth of the tabernacle of Moses; as indeed they did. For Moses' tabernacle was broad eight boards, and each board was of the breadth of a cubit and an half, which made up twelve cubits, Exod. 26.16, 22, 25. And for tabernacle, the LXX. here read and translate, Posts. And this measure of them, well agrees with the symmetry and harmony of this wondrous structure.

**V. 2.** *{And the breadth of the door was ten cubits}* Door; or, entrance. This door was in the midst of the outward, or east wall of the body of the Temple or Sanctuary, joining to the porch.

*{ten cubits}* The whole tabernacle of Moses, was in all, but twelve cubits broad. And the breadth of the porch, here, was eleven cubits; and of the door-way of it six cubits, Chap. 40.48, 49. This door, in Solomon's Temple, was but five cubits broad, 1 King. 6.33. the fourth part of the wall.

*{and the sides of the door five cubits}* Those five on each side of the door, did remain of the wall to the two sides of the house, to make the whole length of that wall twenty cubits, from north to south; according to the breadth of the Temple within, from corner to corner.

*{the length thereof forty cubits}* Length, from east to west, and breadth, from north to south, were of the same measure in Solomon's Temple, 1 King. 6.2, 17. And therefore it seems the thickness of the wall here, which divided between the body of the Temple and the Oracle, is besides the length of sixty cubits, whereof forty belong to the Temple, and twenty to the Oracle. In Moses' tabernacle, and Solomon's Temple, there was but a vail, dividing those sacred places, Exod. 26.31, 32, 33. 2 Chron. 3.14. Which division, or vail was reckoned in the sixty cubits, 1 King. 6.2. and 2 Chron. 3.3. And a vail seems to be likewise in Zerubbabel's Temple, repaired by Herod; which was rent at Christ's death. Matth. 27.51.

**V. 3.** *{then went he inward}* Through the body of the Temple, the holy place; to the Oracle, the most holy place.

*{and measured the posts of the door two cubits}* This partition differs from that in Solomon's Temple; as was said in the Annotations upon the former verse. Some imagine hereby, an entry, or cloister between the two holy places, besides the door. But this is inconsistent with the space and partition between them. Neither was there any entrance into the most holy place, but by the door. But this must be understood, either of the breadth, and thickness of the partition wall; which was thinner, as reason required, than the other walls of the Temple. Or, of two pilasters, by, or upon the posts of the door, on either side; chiefly for ornament sake.

*{and the door six cubits}* Some understand this of the height of the door, and conceive that vers. 5.17.20. have a relation to this height; and that the breadth is expressed in the next words, and measure of seven cubits. Others, and the most, take them for the breadth of the door. And of these, some thus, and that with most probability, That the door was a two-leafed door; as vers. 25. and either leaf of three cubits, so making up six cubits for the opening of the door, and space of entrance; with a middle post, whereon they met, of one cubit; and so making seven cubits for the whole breadth, as in the next words.

*{and the breadth of the door seven cubits}* See Annotations next preceding. Yet many force the words to mean those parts of the wall, remaining on both sides the door; seven, say they, on either side: which fourteen, with six of the door, make up twenty, the length of that wall and partition to the insides of the Oracle. But here, the door and post of it seem only to be measured; and the side-walls, on both sides of the door, to be omitted; as of themselves plain enough.

**V. 4.** *{so he measured}* The most holy place, within the walls, was a perfect square of twenty cubits. And so was the Oracle in Solomon's Temple, 1 King. 6.20. 2 Chron. 3.8. Moses' Tabernacle, by the boards of it, and by the curtains of Cherubins, may be gathered to be forty cubits long; whereof the holy place, thirty, and the most holy place, ten. And the whole, eight cubits broad, Exod. 26.

*{before the Temple}* That is, in the inner part of the Temple. Or, thus, The breadth of it was just equal, with the breadth of the body of the Temple, and stood straight and even before it, at the west end of it; therefore he calls that here the breadth; though the length, so called, were in itself of the same measure, as the sides are in a square.

*{This is the most Holy place}* Far more holy than any other place: Because there was God's Mercy Seat, and Propitiatory. It is called the Oracle, because God spoke with Moses, and gave his Oracles and answers there, Numb. 7.89. Exod. 25.22. and 20.33. It is called the inner-house, the inner-Temple, vers. 15, 17.

**V. 5.** *{measured the wall of the house}* Now he comes to the walls of the house, and the outward buildings. The wall of all the house, was six cubits thick, fit to support the height of the house.

*{and the breadth}* Of that lustration of chambers, joining to the out-walls of the house, the breadth, or bottom, or floor, was four cubits to the lowest story of them; and consequently, five to the middle; six to the highest. It was five in Solomon's Temple, to the nethermost; six to the middle-row, and seven to the third, and uppermost, 1 King. 6.6.

*{on every side}* That is north, and south, and west. For the text clears it, that the east side, or end, which was for the porch and entrance, had no chambers.

**V. 6.** *{three, one over another, and thirty in order}* Three stories; and thirty in each story, viz. most likely, twelve on the north side, and twelve on the south side, and six at the west side or end, in each story. And all the chambers on each story were of the same height, and breadth; about six cubits square a piece, and five cubits high; as those, 1 King. 6.10. that is, about one cubit above the ordinary stature of men in those days. Some conceive these to be overlaid with gold within; by that in Solomon's Temple, 2 Chron. 3.9. But that place seems to relate rather to the high chambers over the porch of the Temple. The use of these chambers was not for the habitations of women, or nuns, as some Papists would fain, and fondly have it: but for the priests, and for the safer custody of the gold and silver vessels of the Temple, and such other things, in those safe and sacred places.

*{and they entered into the wall}* Not the outwall of five cubits thick, ver. 9. but the wall of the house or Temple; at least the beams and floors of the middle, and upmost stories did so. But so entered, that the beams might have hold, and lie only upon the outward ledges, narrowings, or rabatements of the wall, as is signified in the next verse.

**V. 7.** *{And there was an enlarging}* The stories wider above, as the thickness of the walls of the house was made narrower and thinner above; that is, abated a cubit, for the beams, floor, and use of the middle story: and yet another cubit more than that, for the beams, floor, and enlargement of the highest story. So that the walls of the Temple were six cubits thick at the bottom: five at the middle story: and four at the highest story. And the chambers broad, four cubits the lowmost rank, five the middle, and six the highest rank.

*{To the side Chambers}* For them and their use; to receive the beams of them, and enlarge the rooms.

*{the breadth of the house was still upward}* The breadth of the chambers was gained, and enlarged still upwards on the house; increasing, as is expressed in the words. Not that the stories jutted outward, one above or beyond another. And winding stairs went up to these side chambers, and then passages through them, from chamber to chamber; winding so about the house.

**V. 8.** *{I saw also the height of the house round about}* Some understand this of the height of the seats round about the house, on the outside, sustained and supported by beams coming out of the walls, and being in height from the ground six cubits. Some, of the height of the rebatements, julets, or mensolas, upon which the beams of the chamber floor were laid, that they were six cubits one from the other, drawing upwards. And other some, of the height of the house, or Temple, thus; six cubits from the ground, to the floor of the lowest story, that is, six cubits the wall upwards, that was the foundation of the stories of chambers; being of the same height with the breadth of the door of the porch, Chap. 40.48. and with the breadth or thickness of the posts of the gate of the Temple, vers. 1. And of all the walls of the house, vers. 5. and then after that, The three stories of chambers, about five cubits high a piece. Others understand it, of the height of each story in each chamber. But most, and most likely, of the height of the highest story of chambers; He saw in the house, or at the house, the height of that highest story. The foundations of which story, were a full reed of six great cubits; gained so, by the second rebatement of the wall of the Temple. The foundations of the middle story were but five: and of the lowest, but four, vers. 5. The number of them in each story; and the height and breadth of them all alike. This height from the ground was about five and twenty cubits, viz. six to the first floor; five, each story; one, the thickness of each floor; and one, the top cover, being a flat roof, as houses in Palestine and Egypt in those times used to be. And above this height, the windows of the Temple were; and room enough for them.

*{A full reed}* As Gen. 23.9. full moving. See 2 Chr. 3.3. See Annotations on Chap. 40.5.

**V. 9.** *{The thickness of the wall}* Or, *the breadth of it;* in the same sense. And yet some, without all cause, deny this to be understood of the thickness of the wall, that it was thus thick, as five cubits; but that this wall stood at so much breadth and distance from the wall of the house.

*{wall}* Of stone; not of wood, as of posts, joists, and boards; as some weakly imagine.

*{which was for the side Chambers without}* That is, the exterior wall for the outsides of this building, and substruction of the side chambers, inclosing them all, as the outward wall and part of them.

*{was five cubits}* And this also is the breadth of the place that was left, vers. 11. And the thickness of the wall of that building, vers. 12. each of them one cubit less, than the thickness or breadth of the walls of the house, vers. 5.

*{and that which was left was the place of the side Chambers that were within}* Some understand hereby, the place of the side chambers themselves, that were within these bounds, and took up the space and place that was left, between this exterior wall of five cubits, and the walls of the Temple; The chambers thus from the exterior wall standing inwards towards the house: and this place or space was also about five cubits, whether you call it *length,* or *breadth,* vers. 11. Others take this place that was left, to be a void place and space of ground, clear and free from any buildings upon it, and lying next before these Temple-chambers; and being of the breadth of five cubits, round about the three sides of the Temple, north, south, and west; whence passage was to the doors of the foresaid Temple-chambers, one toward the north, and another toward the south, vers. 11. This place is called the place that was left, that is, left void and free of buildings, vers. 9, 11. And the word there used in the Original, is nowhere else found in Scripture.

**V. 10.** *{And between the Chambers}* That is, say some, Those of the Temple last spoken of; as if between chamber and chamber there were the wideness and distance of 20 cubits. But this is incredible and impossible; as appears by what has formerly been showed. Others would make twenty cubits to arise out of all those distances between those chambers of the Temple summed together. But neither may this be admitted, upon the former grounds. The meaning is, Between those chambers of the Temple last spoken of, which were for treasuries, and other chambers adjoining to the walls of the court, to the outmost part; we mean, of this inmost court, over the porches, or porticoes thereof; which chambers were chiefly for lodgings of the priests; and did face those other chambers of the Temple; Between them was this wideness here spoken of.

*{was the wideness of twenty cubits}* The words are plain. Some take them as they sound. Some say twenty cubits, besides the five cubits of the place that was left mentioned, vers. 9, 11. Now of this wideness of twenty cubits, it is not expressly set down how it was taken up, and employed. And hereupon opinions run divers ways. Some say, ten of them were taken up in galleries, or cloisters, upon pillars; those mentioned vers. 15.16. And the other ten, in floors or spaces open to the sky; that is, five cubits on each side of those middle galleries. Others say, This whole interval of twenty cubits, was free of buildings, and lay open to the sky. However, They labor to fill up the number of an hundred cubits in this court of the Temple, from north to south; Some thus; The Temple with its substructions and chambers, sixty cubits: This wideness here mentioned 20 cubits: and the galleries and upper chambers thereof joining to the walls of the court, 10 cubits on the north side, and 10 cubits on the south side. But these heed not that these particulars corrupting the wideness of 20 on both sides of the Temple, raise the sum to 120. Others, more rightly, reckon thus; The Temple with its substructions and chambers 50. The foresaid wideness on either side 20. and the galleries and upper chambers thereof, joining to the wall on either side, five. Or, excluding those last five cubits on either side for the chambers of the walls of the court, we may take in those five on each side, mentioned vers. 11.

*{round about the house on every side}* East, west, north, and south, say some: yet the symmetry, and architecture and fabric of all the buildings in this court seem to deny any such wideness to be left on the east end. It must then be understood of the north, south, and west sides: though some would have it on the north and south sides only; and, without just ground, do deny any such wideness to be left on the west end also.

**V. 11.** *{And the doors of the side Chambers}* Chambers of the Temple; not of the counterfeits, or supposed buttresses of the Temple. Those substructions of chambers, each of them had doors. What Papists write here of these doors opening into places for prayer, is besides the text; through the error of their vulgar Latin translation, to which they are tied.

*{toward the place that was left}* Vers. 9. of the breadth of five cubits round about, as it is in the end of this verse, left clear and free from buildings, close to the sides of these chambers. Others, not so well, taking the place that was left to the Temple, make these doors looking towards the Temple, if not into it. See Annotations on vers. 9.

*{one door towards the North}* The singular for the plural; that is, one sort of these doors were toward the north; that is, the doors of those chambers that were on the north side of the Temple, looked northward.

*{and another door toward the South}* That is, the other sort: namely, the doors of those chambers that were on the south side of the Temple, looked toward the south: and so all looked towards that quarter of the heavens that lay just before them. On the east end were no chambers; though some are otherwise minded; the porch of the Temple supplied the place of the chambers there. Some conceive, without sufficient ground, that there were but two doors only, or at least, that here are meant the two extream doors only, one on the north side of the Temple, and north chambers, and placed at the corner towards the east; and another on the south side of the Temple, and south chambers, at the end or corner toward the east: and that neither looked towards the Temple, nor towards the court, or wide and open place, but served for a supposed staircase, at the east end, on either side, whereby they went unto the chambers, and so through one chamber to another.

*{and the breadth}* Some say, from the wall of the Temple, to the wall which inclosed the buttresses. Others say, the breadth of the chambers within, in their inside. But most and best understand it of the place that was left void from buildings, close to the outside of these chambers, into which the doors opened.

*{five cubits round about}* Not between the walls of the Temple, and these chambers; as if there were a void space of five cubits broad, round about, between them: which some would therefore have to be filled up with earth; so burying, as it were, the Temple deep in ground. But no such thing, nor any such void place can be admitted; as the sixth verse does evince. Neither seem these five cubits to be part of the 20 cubits, vers. 10. But to lie between them, and the Temple-chambers.

**V. 12.** Great here is the difficulty of the text, and the variety of expositions.

*{now the building}* Some understand this of the chambers of the Temple, and their stories and buildings, which can no ways agree with the description, site, and measures following in this verse. Others, of the chambers of the wall of the court, standing at 20 cubits distance from the former. And this is improbable, if not impossible, as the other. Some understand it, of a building of chambers, in the middle of the wideness of 20 cubits, vers. 10. And some, of a wall, distinguishing this court into certain parts, viz. The segment, or separate place, as it is here translated; and the utter court, as it is called, Chap. 42.13, 7, 8, 9. But how these opinions can stand with the buildings premised, and within the compass of the court of the Temple, we cannot conceive. Others, more likely, and better agreeing with the words of the text, make this to be a building, west from the Temple, measured here; and in a several court, measured vers. 15. and the wall, and the galleries or chambers of it, measured also, vers. 15. and the garnishing of them, set down vers. 16.

*{before}* Vers. 15. over against; opposite, and opposite unto it.

*{before the separate place}* Some understand this of the chambers of the Temple. Some of the chambers, together with counterforts or buttresses of the wall of the Temple. Some, of the Temple itself. Some, of the Temple, and chambers or substructions, jointly. Some, of that segment, formerly mentioned. Others, more likely, of the middle open space of 20 cubits between the chambers, of the walls of the Temple, and of the walls of the court; that lay at the west end of the Temple.

*{at the end towards the west}* Toward the west end of the building, say some. Of the separate place; say others. The one making the building to be, before the separate place. The other, behind it. Some thus, on, or from the north and south sides of the separate place; which by a direct line looked full westward.

*{West}* Sea, in the Hebrew, signifying the west, because the great Midland Sea lay west from Jury, as has been said.

*{was seventy cubits broad}* Some reckon this breadth from east to west, which in proper speech is the length; and they make up the number thus. The length of the Temple, along the sides whereof, say they, this building was, 60 cubits: and the breadth of the last story of chambers, and of the wall of the west end, 10 cubits. Or, if they amount to some more, then they make the beginning of the building and reckoning to be so much backward from the beginning of the Temple. Some reckon this breadth of 70 in its proper acception from north to south, and at the west end; and account thus; The breadth of the Oracle within, 20 cubits; The thickness of the north, and south wall of the Oracle, 6 cubits a piece; the breadth of the chambers on both sides, four cubits a piece; the breadth or thickness of the exterior wall of those chambers, on both sides of the Temple, five cubits a piece; the breadth of a void or open space between the chambers, and another exterior wall compassing that void space, five cubits a piece: And the thickness of this last named wall, five cubits on either side the Temple, north and south: in all seventy cubits. Others reckon this breadth from the walls of the Temple, to the walls of the court, thus; The chambers and outwall of them, nine cubits: The interval and void open space between the chambers of the Temple, and those of the wall of the court, 20 cubits; and the galleries and chambers over them, joining to the wall of the court, six cubits: in all 35 on the north side of the Temple, and so many on the south side: in all 70 cubits. But, reckoning this, in a several court; all doubts and scruples are taken away.

*{and the wall of the building was five cubits thick round about}* This was the breadth and grossness of the wall of this building; and is the same thickness with that of the exterior wall of the side chambers, vers. 9. and with the breadth of the place that was left, vers. 11. one cubit less than the thickness of the walls of the house, vers. 5. Some place this wall clean behind this building, contrary to the letter of the text. Some make this wall to be the only thing meant and measured in this verse, how thick in itself, where, and how far it did run in breadth, or side-wise, from north to south; and how far long-wise, from east to west.

*{and the length thereof ninety cubits}* Some understand this of the distinguishing wall, and the proper length of it, *ninety cubits,* even the length of the court floor, or open plot and flat: Whereunto the walls, or buildings to the walls, being added, makes it up a hundred cubits, as the account is, vers. 13. Others understand this of the outer wall, part of the building itself, and reckon the length thus; the porch eleven cubits, the Temple and Oracle, with their walls, seventy; the chambers, and walls of them, nine: in all ninety. Others, taking here the length for height, comparing this with the breadth of seventy, conceive this building to be ninety cubits high. In this variety of opinions and expositions, others say, they can rather tell wherein to find fault, than wherewith to content, and satisfy themselves. But we conceive, that placing this in a several and distinct court, these difficulties will be removed; as will after more fully appear.

**V. 13.** *{so he measured}* The buildings being thus platformed, he now comes to make their measures; or, what he measured in some parts, he now gathers into one sum. He seems to some, in this, and the next verse, to cast the buildings into a square, though there was some difference in the posture of the buildings, and face of the floor of the close, or inner court. But indeed, the buildings are rather in several squares.

*{the house an hundred cubits long}* Some vary somewhat, in gathering up this sum; out of which variety, some further light, perhaps, may arise. All take this length, from east to west. Some number thus; Porch twenty cubits; Wall of the Temple, six; Temple, and Oracle 60. Walls and chambers, at the west end, fourteen. In all, a hundred cubits. Some thus, The wall of the porch, five cubits; the porch itself, with its posts, or cheeks of the door, 12½. East wall of the Temple, 6¼. The Temple and Oracle, sixty cubits; the west wall of the Oracle, 6½ breadth of the west chambers, five; the outward wall of them, broad, or thick, five cubits. In all, an hundred cubits. Others thus; The wall of the porch, 6 cubits; the breadth of the porch, eleven; the wall of the Temple, 6. Then for trestles, five cubits; the Temple, 40 cubits; The east wall of the Oracle, 6. the Oracle itself, 20 cubits; the west wall of it, 6 cubits: in all, an hundred cubits. But we conceive, the rightest reckoning to be thus, stairs to the east porch, 6 cubits; or rather from them, the thickness of the wall of the porch, 6 cubits; breadth of the porch, through which the entrance was made, in the inside, eleven cubits; the wall of the Temple, 6. The Temple itself, 40 cubits; The partition wall, two cubits; The Oracle, 20. The west wall of the Oracle, 6. The breadth of the west chambers, at the bottom, four cubits; And the thickness of their exterior wall, five cubits: In all, an hundred cubits. The breadth likewise an hundred cubits, from north to south; thus, The breadth of the Temple, twenty cubits: the thickness of each wall, north and south, 6 a piece: The breadth of the chambers on each side, four; the thickness of the exterior walls of the chambers, on each side, five: the breadth of the place that was left on either side, five: the wideness between, on either side, twenty: In all, an hundred cubits.

*{and the separate place}* Some understand this of the chambers of the wall of the court, that stood facing those of the Temple, at twenty cubits distance, on the north and south sides; and that they were from the east to the west point of them, a hundred cubits. Others, take it for a separate building of chambers, standing by itself, between the chambers of the Temple, and those of the court-wall, for the use of the priests, and their lodgings: And that this building, with the walls thereof, was of the same length with the other of the house, even just so large to serve their uses. Others conceive, That this explains, what the Prophet means, by house, in the former words; And that is, even this separate place, the porch, Temple, Oracle, chambers, and walls adjoining. So that this separate place was the Temple; or at least part of the Temple, the chambers running by, and adjoining to the sides of it; and so of the same measure and length with it. But we rather, conceive here to be meant, that building in a several court; as we mentioned in the Annotations upon the former verse; and the square of that court to be an hundred cubits, thus; The wideness of twenty cubits, on the west end of the Temple; the breadth of this building, which run east and west, seventy cubits, ver. 12. And the thickness of the wall of it, on either side, five, vers. 12. In all an hundred cubits. And then from north, to south, thus, The length of this building, ninety cubits, vers. 12. And the thickness of the wall, as formerly, five on each side; which makes up an hundred cubits.

**V. 14.** *{Also the breadth of the face of the house}* Here is measured, not the house itself, but that which was before the face of it; the breadth between the court of the priests, and the porch of the Temple, say some. Or, much rather, the whole east face of the court of the house; which was an hundred cubits.

*{and of the separate place}* The meaning thus, which is the separate place, that is the separate place: Or rather, that other separate place and court, which lay in a square, just west behind the Temple. And so the face of it, was, as the face of the house.

*{Toward the east}* Which lay eastward from the Temple, the east plot, or empty space, or open floor of this inmost court of the Temple, say some. But we rather understand the breadth from north to south, of the east face of these two courts of the house, and of the separate place just behind it, as we said before.

*{An hundred cubits}* From north to south; which some do open, and cast up thus, That at the east end, between the court of the priests, and the house or Temple, there was an empty open space running from north to south, an hundred cubits. viz. twenty in the breadth of the house, twenty on either side the house, in the separate place, and 20 in the thickness of the buildings, on either side the separate place. But we much rather gather up the sum of an hundred cubits, as we did before. Some conceiving all the buildings, and void spaces, mentioned in this court, (in the middle whereof the Temple is said to stand) to be more than one court of an hundred cubits square, will contain, have thought, that this court had an hundred cubits square on each side of it; as the court of the priests had. And surely such difficulties and perplexities do arise touching the symmetries, and correspondencies of these buildings, and void places, in one court, one with another, that we conceive builders and art-men in this kind cannot reconcile, and therefore we rather make two courts of them.

**V. 15.** *{And he measured the length of the building}* Of that building mentioned, ver. 12, 13. See the Annotations there. Some here understand this of the buildings of this court of the Temple, of those porticoes, or porches, made with pilasters, and arches, joining to the wall of this inmost court, wherewith it was encompassed; or rather, the west range of them, which ran along the west quarter, at the back of the Oracle behind it. Others of the buildings, which they suppose ran on the outside of the separate place, north and south; And that these did not reach clean up to the building that stood cross to them, at the west end; but broke off just against the sides of the chambers at that end of the house, and so left a space to pass from the north buildings, through the west, to the south buildings.

Again, some understand it of the buildings of that utter court, mentioned Chap. 42.1, 3, 7, 8, 9. which was behind the separate place, behind the breast-wall of it. Which we conceive to draw nearest to the letter of the text, and meaning of the Prophet; That the building within this separate place and court had these galleries or chambers on both sides of it; and made up the measure of length in such manner, as is here set down: yet we must needs confess, that here we walk in the midst of obscurities, and difficulties.

*{over against}* Vers. 12. before. The meaning may be within the breast-wall of it.

*{The separate place}* See Annotations on vers. 12.

*{which was behind it}* Some understand this of the separate place, that it was behind this building. But most, and most likely, mean it of the building, that it was behind the separate place, or breast-wall of it; which best agrees with the harmony and symmetry of the whole frame of all the buildings in this utter court. At leastwise, this building was behind, that is, toward the west, behind that east part, which he had measured before, ver. 14.

*{and the Galleries thereof}* Some mean by these, angles or corners. Some, buttresses of this building. Some, chambers, or storehouses. Others, more likely, do mean porches of this building; or galleries, supported with pillars; or, much to the same effect, the belconies of them, as we newly call them, the ornaments of them. These being in the west had their prospect, to the west part, and hindmost part of the Temple.

*{on the one side}* North and south sides of the house, or rather, east, and west sides of this building in the separate place.

*{an hundred cubits}* Equal with the place, ver. 14. and it was according to the length of all the porches in other courts. Of these hundred cubits, see the particulars, for length, and for breadth, (this court also being an equilateral quadrat) in the Annotations upon vers. 13.

*{with the inner temple}* That is, the Oracle, the hindmost part of the Temple, or rather the whole Temple, which was the inner part of all the buildings, being in the middle of that court, encompassed with porches, or pilasters and arches and chambers over them, joining to the wall of that court; saving that on the east quarter of the Temple court, most think there were no porches, galleries, or chambers.

*{and the Porches of the Court}* Not that one porch of the Temple; but the porches of this court: Which porches being the same in this court, as in the precedent courts, the Prophet thought it enough to touch them only in this one word. These porches, say some, running into the floor of this court, divided it into several squares. Others make this verse a kind of repetition or collection of what was said before; that is, that he measured the Temple in the midst of the *Temple-court;* and the porches about it. And this the rather, because of the latter words of the verse. But those words rather bear, that the Temple, and the porches of that *Court,* were an hundred cubits; as the buildings and galleries of this separate place. And it seems upon this occasion, the ornaments of the buildings as well of the Temple, as of this separate place, are described vers. 16——21.

**V. 16.** *{The door Posts}* The Prophet in all this Chapter seems to contain himself within the limits of the court of the Temple, and of the separate place; and comes here to set forth the garnishing of the buildings in these *Courts,* vers. 16——21. The door posts then we take in a *latitude,* to comprehend those, Chap. 40.48, 49. and those verse 1, 2, 3, 11, 20, 23, 24, 25. Of this Chapter, even the door posts in all the buildings of both the foresaid *Courts.* Some extend them to the door posts in the other *Courts* also of the priests, and of the people, in their gates and porches; and also to the thresholds, and upper lintels of them, and to the pilasters for ornament upon the walls.

*{and the narrow windows}* Either the small windows in the arches of the cloisters, and porches compassing the *court* of the *Temple;* or, the windows of the chambers joining to the *Temple* itself; narrow without, and broad within; or narrow in the middle of the wall, and broad and sloped both without and within, the better to receive the light, and to dilate it and disperse it; as in Solomon's Temple, 1 King. 6.4. See *Annotations* on Chap. 40.16.

We read of no windows in Moses' Tabernacle, no not one in the holy place itself. But of one candlestick there with seven lamps or lights, Exod. 25.31, 32, 37. & Chap. 37.17, 18, 23. To be kindled, and burnt always, from evening to morning; and possibly for want of windows, and light, from morning to evening again, Exod. 27.20, 21. & 30.8. Lev. 24.2, 3. 1 Sam. 3.3. In Solomon's Temple we read of windows, and ten candlesticks besides, 1 King. 7.49. 2 Chron. 4.7, 20, 21, 22. & 13.11. of these candlesticks we have no mention here; as not of many other things besides that were in Solomon's Temple. Now these windows, by the nature of the word, seem to be, like our *lettice-windows,* or *windows of wood* full of small holes bored through, least birds might get in at them. But the matter of them seems to be of gold, as Josephus testifies of the Temple windows in his time; and the rather, seeing Solomon covered the Oracle, Temple, porch, walls, floors, and all within, with gold: and made the candlesticks, and some vessels all of pure gold, 1 King. 6.20, 21, 22, 28, 30, 32, 35. 2 Chron. 3.4——11. Chap. 4.7, 8, 19, 20, 21, 22. These windows, to light, may put us in mind of the light of the Scriptures, Psal. 19.8, 9. Prov. 6.23. 2 Pet. 1.19.

*{and the galleries}* Galleries, and chambers of the house, vers. 5——12. and of the separate place, vers. 15. and some add, of the porches at the walls of the *Courts,* and of the gates entering into them.

*{on their three stories}* Some here understand the three stories of the galleries, and chambers, joining to the walls of the court of the Temple, on the three sides; The north, south, and west side. Others, the three stories of chambers, joining to the walls of the Temple itself, on those three sides; and that these were adorned and garnished, with the Cherubins and palm-trees following; and that the east end or face of the Temple needed it not, by reason of the porch and gate, thereof. Others, for stories, translating parts, understand, those three parts or sides of the Temple itself. Others, in a greater *latitude,* and more likelihood of truth, translate and understand it thus, Galleries to those three; that is, to the Temple, to the separate place, and to the walls of the court: yet in such varieties, we desire rather to be narrative, than too determinative.

*{over against the door}* Some understand a door on either side of the Temple. Some a threshold, or half pace, or bottom of turning, winding stairs, at the east ends that led into the side-chambers. But the galleries were not over against either of these. Others, understand doors opposite one to another; Those of the north side of the court to those of the south side of the court; and that they had the same measure, and manner of adorning. We rather conceive door from doors, by an allogy of numbers; and understand, that over against each door, of the galleries aforesaid, together with the doors of the Temple itself; or, about them doors, and above them, as it is vers. 17, 20. were the garnishings following.

*{cieled with wood}* Boards of wood: most likely of cedar, or olive-tree. Not that the tops or roofs of the chambers were sieled with them, instead of arches of stone, as some would. But the *partitions* between the chambers rather, were made of them; or partly with them. And the doors, and posts of the doors, namely of the porch, Temple, and Oracle, and other places formerly mentioned, were made of them, and most likely sieled with them; as the very walls of those three sacred places were, and had Cherubins and palm-trees carved upon them, overlaid with gold, as we read in Solomon's Temple, 1 King. 6. 2 Chron. 3. and 4 Chapters.

*{and from the ground}* Some interpret, as if the doors and posts, on the outside of the house, and the windows, galleries, and side-buildings, in their several stories had coverings, penthouses, or weather-boards over them to keep off the rain from them. And so likewise under them, or at their stools; to keep the wall betwixt that and the ground unbeaten of the weather. Some understand this, as if from the ground up to the windows, between the walls of the house on the outside, and the three stories of chambers, there were an empty and void place, and that here said to be filled up with earth; and the Temple so buried, as it were in the ground. But the words rather relate to the inside of the house, and show that the walls within were sieled so high, and graven with Cherubins, and palm-trees, knops, and open flowers: as in Solomon's Temple.

*{and the windows were covered}* The windows of the house, Temple, and Oracle, though many deny any windows to the Oracle.

*{covered}* Not that they were windows in shows only, or made in fashion like to windows, as we call dammed windows: But that they were like lettice-windows, with such little holes, as one could hardly discern them at such a distance and height to be windows: or else so covered with the tops of the three stories of the side-chambers, that from the low pavements of the courts they could not be seen; or, which is most likely, covered, if not with curtains, yet with leaves of wood; as the walls were covered and sieled: And these leaves adorned with Cherubins, and palm-trees, as the walls were. These windows were in the north, south, and west walls of the house. The east end needed them not; nor had convenient place for them, unless it were above the height of the east door. These windows in the Temple, no doubt, were uniform, all of a like height and bigness, and the height great; above the three stories of the side-chambers, which must be about 25 cubits, allowing six from the ground to the floor of the first story; 6 to that story (See 1 King. 6.5, 6, 10.) and six to the second story; and six to the uppermost story, and one cubit to the flat roof of them; as the roofs of building did use to be flat, in those times and places.

**V. 17.** *{To that above the door}* Some understand these words of windows. But of them in the former verse. Here they are neither mentioned nor meant, as may appear by the words following; and specially by the 20 verse, which explains this. The particulars here named, are about, and above the doors, as it is ver. 20. the doors of the porch, exterior, and inner house; and the side-walls, north and south, round about on both sides; and the partition walls between them, within and without: all which amount to that set down in one sum in the end of the 19 verse. And all these were adorned, as follows, with Cherubims and palm-trees; and that by measures, haply drawn out into spaces, borders, and quarters, most conveniently and exactly.

*{within and without}* This is meant of the *partition walls,* as aforesaid; and not of the said walls of the house *within and without:* for the insides of them differed much in adornings from their outsides.

**V. 18.** *{And it was made}* Upon the walls mentioned in the former verse; sieled with boards of cedar, with the engravings or carvings of Cherubims, and palm-trees upon them, by the art of the goldsmith; and overlaid with gold, yea, and garnished with precious stones most likely, so as the inside of the house glistered with them, as the heaven does with the shining stars in a clear night. For thus it was in Solomon's Temple, 1 King. 6.15, 16, 18, 22, 25. 2 Chron. 3.6.

*{Cherubims}* Taken for the pictures of Angels, made like beautiful young men, with wings adjoined.

*{Palm trees}* Used anciently for emblems of victory, as well as laurels: as the Cherubims may put us in mind of the Angels ministering to Christ; so the palm-trees of Christ's victory over the Devil, & all our spiritual enemies, Luk. 11.21. Col. 2.15. Apoc. 5.5.

*{between}* A Cherub and a palm-tree were still drawn, one after another: and so a palm-tree was still between two Cherubs, and a Cherub between two palm-trees.

*{every Cherub had two faces}* These Cherubs do so much differ, from those in Ezekiel's vision, Chap. 1. and Esay's vision, Chap. 6. and in John's vision, Apoc. 4. And from those in the most holy place, in Moses' tabernacle, and in the Oracle, in Solomon's Temple, that we wonder how many learned Papists, can avow them all to be the same; and will go about to undertake the reconciling of all the differences, for the maintenance of an assertion so improbable, and unnecessary. An assimilation may rather be made between these, and the curtains of Cherubims that covered the Tabernacle of Moses, Exod. 26.1. and that vail which divided between the holy place, and the most holy, Chap. 27.31. And much more between these here, and those Cherubims, and palm-trees upon the walls of Solomon's Temple, 1 King. 6.29. But they trouble themselves with these former conceits, as they do with others of like nature; namely, that Solomon, besides his two Cherubims in the Oracle, placed there also the two Cherubims, that covered the Ark in Moses' Tabernacle.
