And again, that though Nebuzaradan, at the time of the Babylonish captivity, demolished the Cherubims in the Oracle of Solomon's Temple, yet Jeremy preserved the Ark, with Moses' Cherubims that had covered it, and hid them. As the Maccabees relate a like story, of the hiding of the fire of the altar, at the same time, Macc. 1.19.

**V. 19.** *{through all the house round about}* All the walls on the insides, from bottom to top, were thus adorned: yet the numbers and measures of the Cherubims, and palm-trees on the walls, are, by some, more presumed upon, than can with any certainty, be truly determined.

**V. 21.** *{the Posts of the Temple were squared}* The posts of the doors of the Temple were not round, like pillars, as was the entrance of the tabernacle, Exod. 26.32, 37. Nor the tops arched, or vaulted; but the form and structure of them square, both of Temple and Oracle, as in ancient buildings was usual. That they were of one stone a piece, is more than any man can determine. Some here refer square, not to the form, but to the magnitude, and quantity of the door of the Temple, that it was a square; that is, a fourth part of the breadth of the Temple; namely, one leaf of the door five cubits, and so the other, five cubits, as it is, vers. 2. And those six cubits broad on the one side, and six on the other, vers. 1. are to be understood, as comprehending in them the middle post, whereon they met and closed. But we adhere, rather to that we have set down in the Annotations, upon the said first, and second verses.

*{the appearance on the on}* Herein are compared, not the Temples of Ezekiel and Solomon; but in Ezekiel's Temple, the posts and face of the outward Temple, or holy place, and of the inward Sanctuary, or most holy place, are compared and likened together; both were of one fashion and appearance. Though not of the same quantity, vers. 2.3. As neither were those two doors in Solomon's Temple, 1 King. 6.31, 33. Nor yet in Moses' Tabernacle, Exod. 26.32, 37. But still the inner door was the lesser of the two. The posts, upper lintels, and buildings of the porch in Solomon's Temple, must needs be of an huge firmness, by reason of the height of the walls, and superstructure, arising from the ground to the top, to an hundred and twenty cubits; four times as high as the Temple was, 2 Chron. 3. vers. 4. which makes the moving and shaking of them the more wonderful; if they be included with the Temple, in those words, Esay 6.4. & Amos 9.1.

**V. 22.** *{the Altar of wood}* This was the altar of incense, that stood in the upmost end of the Temple, next the vail of the Oracle, made of wood, and overlaid with gold, Exod. 30.1——11. & Chap. 37.25——29. & 40.26. 1 King. 6.20.22. & 7.48. At this, in Moses' tabernacle, Nadab, and Abihu offered incense, with strange fire, Lev. 10.1. The brazen altar of burnt-offering in the court, had no wood in it. Christ is our altar of incense; at which we offer up our incense of prayers; in his name alone, though his only merits, and mediation.

*{three cubits high, and the length thereof two cubits}* Moses' altar was but two cubits high, and one cubit square, Exod. 30.2. And the measures of Solomon's altar are not mentioned; and therefore the more likely to be of the same measure with that of Moses. For Solomon made a new one; as he made all things else new, save the Ark only.

*{length thereof two cubits}* The length of the superficies, and square face of the top of this altar; as Exod. 30.2. Where likely was a little grate, or some vessel to hold the fire, over which the perfumes were to be burned: if there were not censers used for that purpose. And indeed such censers there were in Solomon's Temple, made of pure gold, 1 King. 7.50. See 2 Chr. 26.19. Heb. 9.4. Apoc. 8.3, 5. Some conceive a great seat upon the top of the altar: Some conceive it hung by chains, at the corner, or side of the altar.

*{Corners}* These were the square wooden posts, on which the boards were fastened, wherewith the altar was made; or rather horns of the altar; as we read in Moses' altar, Exod. 30.2, 3. Lev. 56.18. And so the altar of burnt-offerings; had four horns upon the four corners of it, Exod. 27.2. Ezek. 43.15, 20. Psal. 118.27. Apoc. 9.13.

*{walls thereof}* Sides of the altar.

*{he said}* Chap. 40.3.

*{this}* That is, Looking towards the north, (the candlestick was on the south side, Exod. 40.24.) and as it were with his finger, pointing to, not, as some would, the altar of incense, whereof, formerly, till now, he had spoken; (though that seems to be called a table, Heb. 9.2.) but to the table of showbread, standing on the north side of the Temple; whereof, Exod. 25.23——31. & 40.22. See Mal. 1.7, 12. Else also should the table of showbread be omitted; as likewise the candlesticks are: and the Ark, Cherubims, and all are omitted, which were in the Oracle. Solomon's Temple had ten tables of showbread, 1 King. 7.40. and the bread set on them all, 2 Chr. 4.8, 19. whereof, some were of gold, and some of silver, 1 Chron. 28. vers. 16. See 1 King. 7.48. Christ is that true bread, &c. Joh. 6.33, 35, 51.

*{before the LORD}* Not in the Oracle, as some have misconceived, but in the body of the Temple; as being to be used weekly, or daily; and that by others than the high priest; whether you understand it of the table of showbread, or of the altar of incense, Luk. 1.9. Heb. 9.2, 3, 4. The altar of incense, was placed in the middle of the breadth of the Temple, right before the door and vail of the Oracle. He speaks nothing of this table of showbread; of the matter, measures, crown, border, rings, staves, bread, and vessels of the furniture of it. Of these, we may read, Exod. 25.23——30. & Chap. 37.10——17. Num. 4.7. Of the candlesticks, we may read, Exod. 25. & 37. & 40. Chap. 1 King. 7.48. 1 Chro. 28.16. 2 Chr. 4.8. Where we observe, that seventy candles were light, and shining in Solomon's Temple.

**V. 23.** *{And the temple and Sanctuary had two doors}* That is, the Temple and Oracle had two doors. Not that each of them was double doored, as sometimes with us; having one door of thick boards, or planks, and another within of wainscot, or sieling; and each of them a little wicket: But that the Temple and Oracle had doors a piece; and each of them was a two-leafed door; as is expressed in the next verse. So 1 King. 6.31, 32, 33, 34. where the word used is of the dual number, implying, the two leaves of each door. Some further conceive, that each leaf doubled, and in the opening, folded within itself: and this the rather, because the breadth of them was so great, as is set down, vers. 2, 3. We read of hinges of gold to these two doors, in Solomon's Temple, 1 King. 7.50.

**V. 25.** *{And there were made on them}* The garnishing, and ornaments of the doors.

*{thick planks}* That is, the heads, or ends of thick beams, or joists, which supported the quarters, or rafters, for the boards and floorings of the chambers above the porch. These did jet out, and were seen upon the face of the porch without. And this was the ancient manner of architecture. In following ages, the same manner has been observed; but these jettings out have most been made of stone, in the void spaces of the freeze; and wrought for garnish, in various forms and figures.

**V. 26.** *{And there were narrow windows and Palm trees}* See vers. 16, 17, 18.

*{on the sides of the porch}* And of the chambers upon the porch.

*{and upon the side Chambers of the house}* Those of three stories high, on each side of the Temple and Oracle north, and south sides, and west end.

*{and thick Planks}* See ver. 25. Such also upon the sides of the porch, and of the side chambers, and garnished alike.

## CHAPTER 42

**Verse 1.** *{Then he brought me forth}* He was last in the Temple. Now he returns to the courts, to supply what was wanting to the full and perfect description of them. He mentions other chambers, both the sight, and fabric of them, ver. 1. to the 13. And the use and employment of them, ver. 13, 14.

*{into the utter Court}* This is not to be understood of that utter spacious court described, Chap. 40.5. or of the other courts you were to come into, as you went into the Temple; much less of any court for women; or for laicks only. But this was a court that was on the outside of the buildings, that stood facing those buildings that joined to the Temple, say some; which they conceive to be in the inner court, properly so called; yet called here an outer court, in comparison of those buildings which lay along before them, Chap. 41.10. These being on the back of them behind them. Others understand it as in the last clause of the former Annotation. Others, of the court of the Temple, that it is here further described; because here are many things set down, which by no means agree to the court of the priests; but suit well and accord with the court of the Temple. See ver. 13, 14, 15. Yet others understand it of the outward court of the priests; but only so, as there to take a view of the other north and south chambers, of the inner or inmost court; because the stairs, or eight steps ascending to the inmost court of the Temple, were in the outward court of the priests; and therefore that thither they must go to make this use of them. Though these opinions thus differ, yet we conceive it best to understand the utter court of the priests; as best suiting with the description and words of the text; as will appear.

*{the way toward the North}* In the Hebrew, *the way of the North.* And so it seems the court of the Temple had a north gate; and consequently a south gate, as well as an east gate. Which is to be well observed against the opinion of many.

*{And he brought me into the chamber}* One like a castle, say some. But most, and most likely, chambers; one number being put for another: which is most usual and common in the Hebrew tongue. Or, into the place or row, or file of chambers. Or, into one of them. For chambers were all along the north wall of the court, built over the gallery of pilasters, and arches there. And also a building of chambers and galleries were between those of the court wall, and those of the Temple wall; as some conceive: To which the word here may haply refer. But we rather conceive these chambers to be in the utter court of the priests; as will after better appear.

*{That was over against the separate place}* See Chap. 41.12. which stood south from these chambers; and they on the north side of it; in whatsoever sense you take the separate place here.

*{and which was before the building toward the North}* The northern buildings of the Temple; or of the north chambers of the Temple, over against the north side of the Temple, and separate place, or building: These chambers stood north from them, behind the north wall of this inmost court of the Temple. See Chap. 41.5, 6, 10, 12.

**V. 2.** *{Before the length}* Some understand these measures to be in the north portico, or porch: as if you should say, The porch wherein this door was, had an 100 cubits in length, & 50 in breadth; was an 100 cubits long from east to west; & fifty broad from north to south; as also the porches in the other courts were, Chap. 40.15, 19. & ver. 21, 23. and ver. 25, 27. and ver. 29. and ver. 33. and vers. 36. For this was that constant length and breadth of all the porches of the courts. And that the north door in this porch, and north chamber too, as some understand chamber in the former verse, (which they make to be like a castle or turret) was with these measures thus encompassed; That these measures were at the north door, before the face of it, and at the sides of it. Others make these measures to lie, not in the north porch; But that this north porch had an eastern front, which lay over against another porch, an hundred cubits long, and fifty broad. Others make these measures to lie in the court of the Temple: That this north door in this north porch had before it the length of an hundred cubits, which was the length of the court of the Temple; from north to south, running on the east end or side of the house: And the Temple itself, with its chambers and substructions was fifty cubits broad; or that fifty cubits were on the north side of the Temple, between it and this north porch. But others understand these measures to lie on the north side of these buildings northward; That, as there was a space between them, and the Temple inward: so there was betwixt them, and the north wall of the inner court of the Temple, this space of an hundred cubits long, and fifty broad. But this makes the court of the Temple exceeding spacious; very much beyond those bounds which the common stream of interpreters do assign unto it, confining it only to an hundred cubits square: which spacious enlargement of that court must yet be of necessity granted; or else this building of chambers and galleries, here, and hereafter mentioned, must be placed in a court by itself; or rather in the utter court of the priests, as we formerly signified. And thereto the words of the text, touching their sight, prospect, and measures, will best accord and agree. And thus the north door of these northern buildings, had before it toward the south, an hundred cubits, viz. the length of the Temple, and its chambers from east to west, Chap. 41. ver. 13.

*{North door}* Not of the Temple; as some would have it: and make the meaning to be, as if the north door of the Temple did face this building of an hundred cubits long, and fifty broad. But indeed the Temple had no north door. And this door appears to be in the court, or in a porch, or building, in the court of the Temple. And this door seems to open, not on the south side of these chambers & buildings towards the Temple; but on the north side of them toward the court wall; as those doors do, ver. 4.

*{And the breadth was fifty cubits}* Either the breadth of that hundred cubits long, viz. of the Temple, whose breadth with the chambers and walls on both sides, was exactly fifty: Or the breadth of these buildings wherein this north door stood, was fifty cubits; as the breadth also of all the porches of the courts were, even according to the length of their several gates, and the buildings in them, Chap. 40.21, 25, 29, 33, 36.

**V. 3.** *{Over against the twenty cubits}* Those, Chap. 41.10. which compassed round about the house on every side. This, some make the third side of the tower-chamber, standing toward the void space of twenty cubits.

*{which were for the Inner Court}* viz. of the Temple, Chap. 41.10. yet some understand this as a Periphrasis of the segment or separate place opposed, to the utter court, vers. 1. Both which were in the compass of the inmost court of the Temple, as they say.

*{and over against the pavement}* The paved floor: Namely, of the remaining part of the court of the priests. This, some make the fourth side of that supposed tower-chamber. But *pavement* here relates to the galleries following, and to their situation; which was between these two, the 20 cubits of the inner court of the Temple on the south side; and the remaining paved floor of the outward court of the priests on the north side. This, and other floors were paved, to prevent annoyance by the dirt of winter, and dust of summer. See *Annotations* on chap. 40.17.

*{which was for the utter Court}* Some understand this, and that, ver. 1. of a distinct and several court, as it stands opposed to the segment or separate place. But the fair and easy interpretation is, To take it for the exterior court of the priests and Levites; as has been said.

*{was Gallery}* Not castle-chamber, fort-chamber, tower or turret, as some would. For here is more than one chamber; a porch of galleries and chambers; and not upon the west end of the Temple, as some would; but on the north side, where now the Prophet was, ver. 1. See Chap. 41.15.

*{against Gallery}* Nor, fronting or facing one another; but backing one another, and a chamber between them. Though some make the chambers to be on each side, and the gallery betwixt them. And thus they stood betwixt two fair open spaces: The one southward, to the inner court; the other northward, in the court of the priests.

*{In three}* Some say, This supposed castle, or fort-chamber stood on a side where three of these porches did meet, the east, the west, and the north; or, as others, the west, north, and south. But the text expressly speaks of galleries, as has been said. And that these were in three, that is, in three stories, or heights, one over another, the lowest, middle, and highest, as ver. 5, 6. do plainly show: and three breadths in each story, whereof the middle was a gallery; the other two on each side were closed with walls, and made chambers or treasuries. Others, more likely, hold that the middle was a chamber, and on either side galleries, as was said. And this some make for the two upper stories; supposing the lowest to consist only of galleries, with pillars, and arches, without any chambers in it. But this we make for the two lower stories; the uppermost being lower, and shorter, not so fit for use as the other. These buildings, as they were of a great length, so they were of a great thickness. And as thus on the north side of the Temple; so the like buildings were on the south side of the Temple; as it after follows.

**V. 4.** *{And before the chambers}* That chamber, or row of chambers, ver. 1. those galleries, ver. 3. Yet some, with less probability, take these chambers to be those adjoining to the wall of the inner court within.

*{was a walk}* Lest we should think of the galleries and chambers in the former verse, that there lay nothing betwixt them, and the 20 cubits of the inner court, he mentions this walk that lay between them. And indeed there was besides that, the wall itself of the inner court, and the chambers adjoining to it. Some make, not with such warrant, this walk to be also between them, and the pavement, on the other side, that is, the north side in the outward court of the priests.

*{of ten cubits breadth}* The length seems to be the same with the length of the row of the chambers. And that seems to be 50 cubits, vers. 7, 8. not an hundred, as some would have it.

*{Inward}* That is, within the gallery, on either side, (say some: And so the galleries should take up 20 cubits of the thickness of the building. Or, The gallery or walk was thus broad of 10 cubits, lying inward between the two rows of the chambers. And thus the thickness or breadth of this row of buildings should be 30 cubits; though the breadth of the porches joining to the walls of the outward courts, was constantly 50 cubits. But the plain and easy meaning is, that this walk of ten cubits broad lay inward, out of these buildings, toward the 20 cubits of the inner court.

*{a way of one cubit}* Difficulties, and obscurities run all along; and thereupon varieties of opinions and conjectures. Some think that this way of one cubit is to be understood of a passage so narrow at the east and west ends of the Temple, that the court being but an hundred cubits, the building of the Temple took up all the space, leaving but one cubit for passage at either end. But this way of one cubit is to the chambers here mentioned. And this opinion disagrees with the true quantity of the court, and of the buildings and open spaces in it. Others understand it thus, That upon the walls of these upper chambers, (vers. 9.) or else galleries, which were one against another, on each side there were pilasters, like to them in the portico underneath, jetting out the way or space of one cubit without the wall. Or rather thus; On the outside of either gallery, or else chambers, was a way of a cubit broad; somewhat like the flagged way or path before the houses in *Cheapside,* or *Covent-Garden.* Or thus; There was a way of one cubit, into the chambers; that is, narrow entrances into them: or so narrow a way before them, between the chambers and the galleries; into which way the chamber doors opened.

*{and their doors}* *doors,* some say, of the walk. Others more likely, of the chambers.

*{toward the north}* Some conceiving two rows of chambers in this building, understand this of those chamber doors that stood to the Temple ward: And that the doors of the opposite file stood toward the south: And that the doors of both files opened into the gallery that was betwixt them. Others, which make but one row of chambers in this building, conceive their doors to be towards the north, opening into the north gallery, in reverence to the Temple: That though doors toward the south would have been nearer to the Temple, yet in reverence to that holy place, they must go to the back of these buildings, or to their north, to go into them. To the same purpose speak they, and most agreeable to the truth, that understand these galleries to be chambers, and their doors to open toward the north, into that way of one cubit.

**V. 5.** *{Now}* Here the Rabbies call again for the help of their Elias.

*{were shorter}* Cut off, abridged, strained, were lower, not so high in themselves as the inferior chambers, and so to their ceiling not so high from the floor. The lowmost bore all the weight, where the foundation on which the weight of the whole structure lay; and so were broader, of bigger and stronger proportion, than the upper chambers, built upon them; lest otherwise they should shrink under the burden of them.

*{for the galleries}* Some understand this of the galleries distinct from the chambers. Others, of both comprehended under one notion. Others, of the pilasters, and half columns.

*{were higher than these}* Some say, The galleries higher than the chambers: the better to see the sacrifices, and hear the services of the Temple. Others insist upon the Hebrew word, to signify, did eat of these; And so make the meaning this, That the walls, as they grew higher, so grew narrower in each story, straight, and contracted on the outside, and the roof still lower; and that of that highest so low, as made it scarce habitable. And this might be in some sort for the honor and dignity of the Temple, as veiling their top to it; and so much the more too, in that they stood on lower ground than the Temple did. See ver. 6. Some understand this of the pilasters and half columns, as aforesaid, set to the outside of these upper chamber walls: And that they were of a lesser proportion, and not so massive, as those beneath.

*{Than the lower}* Some make the building commodious for use and habitation, to be only in the lower, and middlemost row and story; having an eye herein to the Hebrew words, which bears translation thus, *and the building consisted* of the lower and middlemost.

**V. 6.** *{In three stories}* Both galleries, and chambers; as in the precedent verse. The middle rooms in each story had light from the side rooms. To these stories, or rather to those joining to the walls of this inner court, do those words of, 1 King. 6.36. seem to bear a reference.

*{but had not pillars}* Some understand this of the uppermost story, that it was not high roofed, with *pillars,* or *rafters,* but flat. Others, of that, and of the second stories likewise; that the two upper *rowes,* or *stories,* were not made up with *vaults,* borne up with massive *pillars,* as the lowmost row, which stood upon the plain of the *Court;* but with a continued wall, divided with little *pilasters,* or half *Columnes,* which from story to story were lessened, according to the rules of *architecture;* and they being lessened in that kind, reason would have the height also to be abated. Others conceive, that neither *chambers* nor *galleries,* stood upon *pillars* in any of the stories; as some other buildings in the Holy Verge did; For then the *Galleries,* in the second and third *story,* might have been enlarged, by jutting out towards the *Courts,* and not have trenched upon the breadth of the *chambers;* but that they were all built up with an even wall: only some large openings there were all along the *galleries,* for the more air, and better light.

*{as the pillars of the courts}* They had not *pillars* as the *Porches* of the other exterior *courts* had. No, not as the *pillars* supporting the *vaults* or *roofes* of the *galleries.*

*{therefore the building was straitened}* The *walls* or *pylasters* in thickness, and the *stories* in height. What the vulgar Latin translation adds of *50 cubits,* is more than is in the Hebrew text.

**V. 7.** *{And the wall}* Of the foresaid building, of this northern file, and stories, of galleries and chambers. Here we go on still in difficulties, about the right situation of this wall.

*{that was without over against the chambers}* Therefore no part of the *chambers* or *Galleries,* themselves: but at some distance compassing and closing them, and that whole body of building.

*{toward the utter court}* See *Annotations* on vers. 1, 3.

*{on the fore-part of the chambers}* Front, or head of them: not on the east end of them, as some would; but rather on the north side, called the forefront, because the doors were there, vers. 4. And this wall was to separate and distinguish betwixt these, and other spaces, and buildings in this *court.*

*{the length thereof was fifty cubits}* Fifty *cubits* was the breadth of the *porches* joining to the walls of all the *courts,* generally; according to the length of the several *Gates,* as has been said. And here the length of this wall was fifty *cubits;* answerable, in all likelihood, to the length of this fabric of building, from east to west.

**V. 8.** *{For the length of the chambers that were in the utter court was fifty cubits}* As the length of their wall was, vers. 7. And therefore this is given as a reason of that. Others, by length here understand the extent and breadth; and make this whole verse but an explication of that in the latter part of the second verse. See the *Annotations* there.

*{and lo}* The Angel; and the Prophet, coming from the north *chambers,* to the south *chambers,* in the way meets with these hundred *cubits,* which were before the Temple. See Chap. 41.14. that is, say some, from east to west. But we rather conceive, That these hundred *cubits* before the Temple, were the length of the Temple court, from north to south, the distance betwixt these northern, and the other southern *chambers,* to which now they were going. And these, not lying within the compass and verge of the *Temple-Court,* as some think, leaving the *walls* of the *court,* and the *porches* of them, at a distance behind them: which thing must needs enlarge much the compass of this inmost *court, The court of the Temple:* But without that *court,* close on the back side of the *walls* of it, within the *court* of the priests. Others conceive, That both those parts of the *court* of the priests, that contained those chambers and buildings last mentioned, were squares, both on the north side, and south side of the Temple; and squares of an hundred *cubits,* which the Prophet marks with this note of admiration, *Loe!*

**V. 9.** *{And from under}* Or rather, *from the place;* as the word will bear. If from under, then not perpendicularly; but as when we say, *A town or house stands under an hill;* so here, *at the lower end of the court.*

*{these chambers}* These northern chambers. Though some would understand them of the southern chambers.

*{was the entry}* Or, *He had brought me.* So variously may the word be rendered.

*{He}* That is, the Angel in man's shape, Chap. 40. vers. 3, 4.

*{on the east side}* By the coast of the east he passed; or, the entry into these chambers, was on the east side, or at the east end. The coming in of them below was toward the east.

*{as one goeth}* Or, *as he came.*

*{into them}* The southern chambers.

*{from the utter court}* That is, by the eastern tract or coast along of the utter court, that is, which stood east from these chambers. In all these words in this verse, The Prophet sets down his passing from the northern chambers, to the southern chambers, by the tract of the east on the east end of the Temple.

**V. 10.** *{the chambers}* Still here we wade in doubts and darkness.

*{were in the thickness of the wall of the court}* In the breadth of the fence, or precinct of the court; in the breadth of fifty cubits, vers. 2. in the breadth of the circuit or compass.

*{toward the east}* Along the southern side drawing toward the east, before the way of the east; having the entry on the east side, as the northern chambers had, vers. 9. and so running along toward the west, by the south side of the Temple.

*{over against the separate place}* By a direct line over against the separate place, which stood west from it, before it. See *Annotations* on Chap. 41.12.

*{and over against the building}* See *Annotations* on Chap. 41.12. There was such another building of chambers, as on the foresaid northern side. None such on the east, and west, sides or ends of the Temple.

**V. 11.** *{And the way}* See *Annotations* on vers. 4.

*{before them}* Before the chambers, and consequently before the separate place, and before the building last mentioned.

*{was the like appearance}* That is, the appearance and proportion of the way, and of the chambers, and galleries; and their length, breadth, and outgoings, were according to the fashions, and according to the doors of those in the north formerly spoken of, vers. 1——7. all alike in all respects, even the whole fabric, fashion, contrival, disposal, front, way of a cubit breadth before them, and measures, were all in every point so like those on the north side, spoken of before, that they need no further illustration. Some make not only these two side-buildings, north and south; but also that called the separate place, as another distinct thing from the Temple, to have all three, a like buildings of chambers, galleries, and all, in all respects, of the same quantities, proportions, and fashions.

**V. 12.** *{And according to the doors of the chambers}* That is, like to those doors of the chambers, was this door here described. Thus he proceeds on, in this verse also, as in the former, to show in the particulars, the likeness and correspondency between these southern, and the northern buildings.

*{that were toward the South}* The southern chambers.

*{was a door}* This, and the situation of it, is the thing described in this verse.

*{in the head of the way}* Or, *beginning of the way.* He speaks now, only of the buildings on the south side of the Temple; and therefore of the way there, and door there.

*{even the way directly}* Directly points out this way.

*{before the wall}* That wall, Chap. 41.12. or closure of the parapet. The Hebrew word is of a doubtful, and somewhat obscure signification.

*{toward the east}* In the very front of the building, at the east, there was the door. Not therefore in that way of a cubit broad, vers. 4. which ran along under the walls of the galleries on both sides, as some imagine: but as the entry was into the northern chambers, mentioned vers. 9. which was partly also before the separate place, as the vulgar Latin expresses it.

*{as one entreth into them}* Them which did run thence towards the west. See ver. 9.

**V. 13.** *{Then said he}* My guide, the measurer, Chap. 40.3.

*{which are before the separate place}* Chap. 41.1, 10. See *Annotations* on Chap. 41.12. Some would gather out of the verse following, that these chambers were included within the separate place.

*{they be holy Chambers}* Not in themselves, and their own nature, as not capable of holiness so; but in their use. The same is to be said of holy vessels, vestments, oil, &c.

*{where,* &c.} After he had described the fabric, and fashion of the chambers, he now sets down the holy use of them; as after follows.

*{the Priests}* The Levites came not into the inmost court of the Temple; but they did into these chambers, if they were seated in the court of the priests, as some take them to be.

*{that approach}* That come near to the Lord in his Sanctuary, where he dwelt in the visible signs of his presence, and grace; in the means and ordinances of his worship, while the people stood further off.

*{shall eat,* &c.} This is one holy use of these chambers.

*{the most holy things}* The Oracle is usually called the most holy, meaning place. But other things are called also the most holy, Num. 4.14. 1 Chron. 23.13. as the Tabernacle, Ark, table, candlestick, laver, anointing oil, their vessels, the perfume, the offerings, showbread, and all devoted things, Exod. 29.37. & 30.10, 29, 36. Lev. 2.10. & 6.17, 25, 29. & 7.1. & 10.12, 17. & 14.13. & 24.9. & 27.28. Ezek. 43.12.

*{there shall they lay}* Another holy use of them.

*{the meat-offering}* See Lev. 2.10. & 6.16. & 7.9, 10. which of them are not to be eaten. See Lev. 6.23.

*{sin-offering}* See Lev. 6.26, 29. which of them not to be eaten Lev. 6.30. & chap. 4.11, 12, 21. & Chap. 16.27.

*{trespass-offering}* See Lev. 7.6, 7. The difference and distinction between sins and trespasses, here mentioned, is not easy to conceive. Papists would fain get a color hence for their distinction of venial and mortal sins. Others, would understand by the one, sins of commission; by the other sins of omission. Others, and somewhat likely, by the one, sins against knowledge; and by the other, sins of ignorance, yet this falls short of a full satisfaction.

**V. 14.** *{When the Priests enter therein}* See Chap. 44.17, 19. Exod. 28.43.

*{Priests}* This is to be understood of the Levites also, in their ministry, in the court where the altar of burnt-offerings was.

*{unto the utter court}* The court of the people, Chap. 40.17. &c.

*{lay their garments}* Linen garments, linen ephods, coats, girdles, and bonnets, Exod. 28.40. 1 Sam. 2.18, 28. & 14.3. & 22.18. We read of 597 of them, Neh. 7.70, 72.

*{for they are holy}* See *Annotation* on ver. 13.

*{other garments}* Common to them with the people; yea even in the perclose of holy duties, and sacred actions, when they are to be done out of the holy place, Lev. 6. vers. 10, 11.

*{to those things which are for the people}* Not meaning the sacrifices of, and for the people; but the things of civil, and common converse with them.

**V. 15.** *{Now when he had made an end}* When he had thus measured all the particular buildings, He comes now to measure the circumference of the whole holy place, or the holy mountain. So that here the whole compass, or outmost wall of all the buildings is measured; and so this whole description of the courts, Temple and chambers, ended.

*{the inner house}* The Temple, and all the substructions, chambers, and buildings in the inmost court, the court of the Temple: and those chambers, about which he was last employed; and consequently all the courts and buildings, inclosed within the compass of the outmost wall. Chap. 40.5.

*{toward the gate}* That gate, Chap. 40.6. as some would have it. Or rather, the gate of the outmost wall, Chap. 40.5.

*{and measured it}* The outmost wall, Chap. 40.5. Not the way to it, as some Papists do imagine.

*{round about}* The four equilateral sides of it. Not that the wall was round.

**V. 16.** *{He measured}* Here is great variety of opinions, both touching the thing measured; and also touching the measure itself. For the thing measured, some take it to be the wall of the city Jerusalem. But those being of 18000 measures or cubits, Chap. 48.35. Therefore, others do rather take it to be the wall of the upper city, or upper part of the city Jerusalem, called the city of David, on, or next to mount Sion; See 2 Sam. 5.7. Others, make it to be the wall inclosing, and compassing the exterior court, the court of the people, Chap. 40.17, 20. And this they much press, and labor to evince, by a geometrical demonstration; taking the inmost court (of the Temple likely) to be an hundred cubits square, Chap. 40.27.; and the outward court (of the people) to be likewise an hundred cubits square, Chap. 40.23. That then it will undeniably follow, that each side of that square of the court of the people, is, and must be, five hundred cubits. And they second this by that, Chap. 45.2. where the compass of the Sanctuary is described, five hundred square, as is here set down; and fifty cubits by name, round about for the suburbs thereof, are expressly set down. And so make this wall, to be that separating and distinguishing wall, between the Sanctuary, and the profane place, Chap. 42.20. And yet, at the best supposed grounds of this demonstration, are but seeming probable conjectures. And the text itself is strong enough, to clear it sufficiently to be meant of the outmost wall, that breast wall or parapet, in compassing all the foresaid buildings, belonging to the courts and Temple; whose height and breadth, was first set down, Chap. 40.5. as may after appear. For the measure itself, some make it to be five hundred cubits, measured out by so many reeds, each of six cubits and one hand breadth; as would make up, or reach unto that number of five hundred cubits. And this they do, though with much violence to the text, only to avoid that great, and unproportionable length of the wall, and huge quantity of compass, that otherwise would arise, if they should take them for reeds. Yet others, understanding them of reeds, would solve the foresaid inconvenience, thus; by saying, these five hundred reeds were the total sum, arising out of all the four sides of the square; allotting so, an hundred and five and twenty reeds, to each side of the square: which is as repugnant to the text, as the former opinion. The issue may be this; to take the meaning, as the text holds it forth; and so understand this wall to be long, five hundred reeds: For so the text expresses reeds, thrice in this verse, and twice a piece, in each of the three verses following. And these five hundred reeds, to be on each side of the square, as the text also expressly avouches it: and so the whole sum in the compass, to arise to two thousand reeds.

This exceeding largeness and compass may well serve to typify the exceeding great largeness and diffused compass of the Church of Christ under the Gospel. For the inconvenience surmised, it is not so much to be thought of in this mystical vision, purposely, set forth under types, wherewith the Jews, God's people then, were best acquainted, to represent the glorious state of the Church of Christ under the Gospel: yet so as the Holy Spirit is far off from tying himself strictly to those types in all particularities. Which thing we see, as here; so most plainly, in the allotment out of the portions of land, for the priests, and for the Levites, and for all the twelve tribes of Israel, Chap. 45. & 48. in placing the city above 15 miles distant from the Temple; and in the circuit of it, Chap. 45. and in the vision of the waters, and trees, and their monthly fruits. Chap. 47. as hereafter will more fully and plainly appear.

*{the East side}* This is not then the measure of the whole round about; but the measure of each side, or square.

*{five hundred reeds}* Not cubits but reeds; as in the Annotations aforesaid. And here the Hebrew is מאות [Hebrew reading uncertain]; whereupon the Keri of the Massorites will have it to be read מאות; as it is in the same measures following, vers. 17, 18, 19, 20. And the LXX. Jerome, and Targum do read it so.

*{measuring Reed}* Which was used to such large measurings. See *Annotations* on Chap. 40.3.

**V. 19.** *{west side}* Heb. *the Sea;* taken usually for the west; because the great Inland Sea, or Mediterranean Sea, lay west from Judea, being the west bounds and border of it.

**V. 20.** *{He measured it}* The wall aforesaid. Though some would make this another wall, differing from the former; and understand this of the wall of the court of the people. And that it was of five hundred cubits square. But all this we conceive as was formerly said, to be without any warrant of the text; or rather, against it: which only here sums up the former dimensions, of the aforesaid wall. And therefore though *reeds* be not expressed in the text, yet the word to be supplied, must not be of any other measure than that of *reeds* formerly so oft expressed, vers. 16——19.

*{to make a separation}* This shows the true end, and due use of it, to make a separation] This shows the true end, and due use of it, to make a *separation* betwixt the Sanctuary, and the profane place. And hereupon some would have the measure here to be taken of the compass of the mount itself whereon the Temple was built: the structure; fabric, and proportion of the buildings, have been thus platformed, measured, and viewed. It will not be impertinent a little, to look at the scope and intention of the description, or at the substance shadowed under these figures.

First, therefore it is to be observed, That there was never any Temple built, of these dimensions, and composure. That, built within a while after this vision, by Zerubbabel and Joshuah, had no relation to this. And indeed the measure of the circumference of the bounding wall, or that which separates betwixt the holy and profane, utterly denies any such thing; being a measure that exceeds, or at least equals the full compass of all Jerusalem, Temple, and all; yea though the cubit be understood of the shortest size; if we take the reeds in the proper sense, which the letter of the text seems to urge and require. And so also the portions of the priests, and of the Levites allotted to them, amount to space near as big as all the land of Canaan, Chap. 48.8——15. These things therefore are spiritually to be understood. And howsoever the description of this house and land were a prophetic assurance of the people's return out of Babylon; (where Ezekiel wrote these things) yet do they chiefly aim at Christ, and his coming among them after their return, and the gathering of the numerous congregation of the Gentiles; and the framing the spacious Temple and city of the Christian Church under the Gospel. And therefore this can no way countenance a temporal kingdom of the Messias yet to come, as the Jews imagine; nor a new Temple, or city to be built, since that the Temple here described, is as big as the city; and the holy ground of the priests, Levites, prince, and city, Chap. 45. near as big as all Canaan.

Secondly, it is observable, That as Ezekiel measures the Temple and the city here, Chap. 40.41, 42, 48. so does John, a new Temple also, Rev. 11.1, 2. and a new Jerusalem, Rev. 21. Now in the opinion of some, though these agreed in this, that they all aimed at the spiritual Temple and city, the Church under the Gospel; yet in this they differed, that they had respect to different times, and aimed at times and seasons, in succession one after another. This platform and description of Ezekiel relates, say they, to the Church, under the Gospel, betwixt their return out of captivity, and the destruction of Jerusalem; when the Gentiles first came in, and they and the Jews made one people. That in Rev. 21. to portray, say they, at the new Jerusalem, which should be after the old one was destroyed, the Church under the Gospel, after the rejection of the Jews, and destruction of their city; which has now taken up about one thousand five hundred and seventy years. And therefore that Jerusalem is far larger than this in Ezekiel. In that, there is no Temple mentioned, nay there was no Temple at all, as the text expressly, Rev. 21, 22. utters: for where there is mention of a Temple, there is some thing to do with Jews; but the Church of this space, say they, consisted only of Gentiles, the Jews being all the while under obduration. Now the eleventh of the Revelation speaks of a Temple measured, as the case is here: For as this place speaks of the Church of the Jews, when the Gentiles were called in, and joined to them, at Christ's first coming; so that place, say they, speaks of the Church of the Jews, when they shall be called in and joined to the Church of the Gentiles; which is yet to be before Christ's second coming. And it is observable, that in both these places, there is no mention of a court for the Gentiles; as there may seem to be in the material Temple: But all Jerusalem must be supposed for that Ezekiel reckons many courts; as has been showed: but for the Gentiles, there is no court there at all, expressly named and bounded, or immured for them, because of the boundless dilating of the Gospel: and so Rev. 11.1, 2. there is only one court mentioned, the court of the priests, or that where the altar stood: But the outer court is not measured, but cast out without any bounds, because it is given to the Gentiles to tread in the worship of God, (it is not καταπατησουσι,) and to come into it with the Jews; for when they shall be called, the Gentiles and they shall join together to serve the Lord, and to tread his court.

## CHAPTER 43

**Verse 1.** *{He brought me}* From the west side, where he had been measuring, Chap. 42.19.

*{toward the East}* To the east gate of the utmost wall, Chap. 40.6. so that the Angel of the Covenant, in the appearance of a man, Chap. 40.3. having showed to the Prophet that magnificent Temple, whereby the spiritual Church is prefigured; and having described it, and the buildings thereof in the precedent Chapters; The proportion and measures whereof, in all the parts, was hard to find out and express; as that which is spoken of them shows. He comes now in these two next Chapters, the 43. and 44. to set out the sacred ministry and worship therein. More particularly, In this Chapter is set down, the returning of the glory of God into the Temple; and the promise of his continual dwelling there amongst his people, upon their repentance; whereunto they are exhorted. Afterwards, the altar of burnt-offerings is described; And the manner of its consecration, and its use prescribed, In these first words, the Prophet is brought from the west side, where he ended the circumference of all the holy place, Chap. 42.19. to the gate where he began, vers. 15. of Chap. 42. and Chap. 40.6. which gate by a direct line looked toward the Temple. See Chap. 44.2, 3.

**V. 2.** *{The glory of the God of Israel}* The vision of his glory, set down in the next verse.

*{Came from the way of the East}* In Chap. 11.23. the glory of the Lord departed from the Temple, because of the profanation of it, and from the city because of the profaneness of it, and stood upon mount Olivet, east of the city. And now that the Lord had platformed to himself a new Temple, his glory returned to it again.

*{way of the East}* Where the sun rises, Luk. 1.78. Let us walk as in the day, casting off the works of darkness.

*{and his voice was like the noise of many waters}* Chap. 1.24. Rev. 1.15. & 14.2. & 19.1, 6. Such is the power of the Gospel; such the efficacy of it: it cannot be hindered or stopped.

*{the earth shined}* Chap. 1.27. He is that sun of righteousness, Mal. 4.2. enlightening all. Joh. 1.9. the light of the Gentiles, Lev. 2.32. The fault is in men that are not enlightened by it, Joh. 1.5. & 3.19. 2 Cor. 4.3.

**V. 3.** *{And it was according to the appearance}* Chap. 1.4. & 8.4. & 9.3. & 10.1, 4, 16. The very same glory that had departed away, now returned. God returns to penitent sinners, Chap. 33.11, 13. Jer. 18.7. Joh. 3.10. Jer. 18.8.

*{when he came to destroy the city}* Or, *when I came to prophesie that the city should be destroyed.* See Chap. 9.2.5. So Jer. 1.10. Esay. 6.10. So Lev. 13. The priest shall make the leper clean, or make him unclean, in the Hebrew; that is, shall pronounce him so, as our English has well rendered it; warranted so to be from Lev. 14.57.

*{and the visions were like}* The whole, and each part of the vision.

*{that I saw}* Chap. 1.

*{I fell}* See Chap. 1.28. with reverence and fear adoring Christ.

**V. 4.** *{By the way of the gate}* Returns by the same way that it departed.

**V. 5.** *{Inner court}* From the east gate of the utmost wall, ver. 1. to the inner court next the Temple; that he saw the glory of the Lord filling the house; and heard the Lord speaking unto him out of the house, vers. 6.

*{filled the house}* Likely, in a cloud, which was a visible sign of the presence of God's majesty; as 1 King. 8.10, 11. Lev. 16.3. So Ezek. 44.4. God's presence is the full glory of the Temple, and of every pious soul.

**V. 6.** *{And I heard him speaking to me out of the house}* As God to Moses, out of the Tabernacle, Lev. 1.1.

*{and the man stood by me}* Chap. 40.3.

**V. 7.** *{the place of my throne}* That is, Jerusalem, the city of the great King, Psal. 48.2. Jer. 3.12. Mat. 5.35.

*{the place of the soles}* My footstool, 1 Chron. 28.2. Lam. 2.1. Psal. 99.5. and 132.7.

*{no more defile——by their whoredom}* That is, by their spiritual fornications of idolatry: And this prophecy was fulfilled in them to the very letter. For after their return out of the captivity of Babylon, even to this day, they have forsaken their idols; and though they worship not the true God, as they should, yet do they not worship false gods at all. And thus are they cross and contrary in their impieties; and yet impious still. Before their captivity they were all for idols, and nothing for the true God at all; and since that captivity, they are not for idols at all; and yet not for the true God neither.

*{nor by the carcasses of their Kings}* So also vers. 9. Let them put the carcasses of their Kings far from me: not for that they defiled themselves by touching their dead bodies; or, for that they were grown into the idolatry of the heathen, to deify their dead Kings, and to reserve their corpses for adoration; for we nowhere read at any time of that their idolatry; but rather that they defiled themselves with human carcasses, which they had sacrificed alive to their idols; which in Scripture are sometimes understood by the name of kings, as the Ammonites used to call their idols. See Lev. 18.21. 1 King. 11.5. 2 King. 23.10. Amos 5.26.

**V. 8.** *{In their setting of their threshold by my thresholds}* That is, in adding their traditions to my precepts, Isa. 29.13. we must not add or diminish, Deut. 4.2. and 12.32. 1 Cor. 3.12, 13. Apoc. 22.18, 19.

*{and the wall between me and them}* Or, *for there was but a wall between me and them.* This wickedness was as a wall of separation between me and them, Isa. 59.2. which has caused me to consume them, by bringing on them the judgments threatened by the Law, Lev. 26. & Deut. 28. Or, for setting their idols, and preferring their service in my Temple, or in places and chapels near to the place set apart to my service.

*{wherefore I have consumed}* Heb. *eaten them in mine anger.* See Chap. 8.3. & 23.39. & 44.7. 2 King. 16.14. & 21.7. Jer. 11.15.

**V. 9.** *{Now let them put away,* &c.} That is, all the true elect, and especially the Jews converted under the Messiah, shall cast off idolatry, and serve God in all purity: which future time is expressed by *Now,* which in a sort was present to the Prophet, namely, by vision, or revelation from God.

**V. 10.** *{Shew the house to the house of Israel, that they may be ashamed.} And again, ver. 11. {If they be ashamed, shew them the form of the house}* The house being an emblem and resemblance of the Church of the Gentiles under the Gospel, might shame the Jews to look upon it, by considering that they had become so abominable, that the Lord had refused them for his Church, and had chosen the heathen: but if they would be ashamed with an holy shame, and sorrow with an holy sorrow, that they had been thus offensive, then acquaint them with the form, laws, and ordinances of the house, that they may know it, and have the description, marks, and delineation of it perfectly in their minds; for they also then shall have their interest in it; as it fell out, and was with the many thousand Jews converted in the time of our Savior, and his Apostles; and as it shall be with them at their conversion near the end of the world.

*{pattern}* Or, *sum,* or, *number.*

**V. 11.** *{And all the forms thereof}*

*{and all the forms thereof}* These words are repeated; and is as much, as show them, show them the forms, both of the courts and house; and of the house both within and without.

**V. 12.** *{upon the top of the mountain}* By steps they ascended to it, Chap. 40.2, 6, 22, 26, 49.

*{the whole limit thereof}* As it is measured and bounded, Chap. 42.16, 17, 18, 19.

**V. 13.** *{And these are the measures of the altar}* The fabric of it seems to be thus, one cubit high was the basis, or foot, or bottom, bosom, or settle, breadth, or projection of it, one cubit; border of it, by the edge thereof, one span. From thence two cubits, to the round ledge, or bench, or settle of a cubit broad, that went round about it. And the thickness of that ledge being one cubit, raised the height one cubit. This ledge or bench seems to be for them that served at the altar, to stand upon, and to go upon, round about the altar, and to rake up the ashes that were within the altar: and therefore this altar was not close boarded up upon the sides everywhere; as the altar made by Moses was, Exod. 38.7. which was movable, and of small dimensions; but this was boarded up, only those two cubits that were betwixt this settle and the ground, or basis, to keep the ashes together: but above this settle it was open at certain places, that they might go in, and get the ashes out. About this ledge or bench the altar did rise four cubits; and this part of it seems to be called הראל the Mount of God. And then, another ledge or bench, of the same breadth, round about the altar likewise: upon which the priests might stand, for the disposing and ordering of the sacrifices themselves. And these benches being a cubit broad, it caused that their compass increased two cubits above the compass of the altar itself; and that the standing of the priests might be firm upon these benches, it seems there was a ledge of half a cubit broad, fixed upon the outward edge of them that the priests might not slip off into the court: and a ledge, which the text calls a bosom, fixed upon their inner edge, that the priests might not slip into the hollow of the altar: & from thence upward were the horns at every corner. This uppermost ledge being likewise one cubit thick, raised the whole height of the altar to nine cubits. And this uppermost part, or the hollow part of it, where the wood and fire were put, seems to be called אר אל, the strong Lion, or the Lion of God. The top of this altar was 12 cubits square, ver. 16. The settle made it at that part 14 cubits square, ver. 17.

*{even the bottom shall be a cubit}* Heb. *the bosome.* Meaning as it seems, that ledge that went betwixt the highest bench, and the altar.

*{And the breadth a cubit}* Of the bench itself.

*{and the border thereof by the edge thereof a span}* A border carved, a span breadth upon the edge of that bosom or ledge, most likely that rose betwixt the bench and the altar.

**V. 15.** *{So the altar shall be four cubits}* From the lower to the upper bench: this is properly called the altar, because that space beneath the lower bench, was the place of ashes. In the Hebrew this verse is literally thus: And the Mount of God, Hareel, four cubits; and from the Lion of God, Ariel, and upward, four horns. Where the body of the altar, as was said, betwixt the two benches, is called the mount of God, as if God dwelled upon the altar, in a singular manner, as on a little mount, in the greater mount of the Sanctuary. And the grate, whereon the sacrifices were laid, is called the Lion of God, because it devoured so much flesh, and so many beasts in sacrifice. See Exod. 27.4, 5. & 39, 39. This may mind us of our altar, Jesus Christ, who sanctifies our sacrifices of prayers, and praise, and service, and obedience, Mat. 23.19. Apoc. 8.3, 4. 1 Pet. 2.5.

*{four horns}* So in the altars of Moses and of Solomon; both in the brazen, and golden altars. See Apoc. 9.13. These were of the same material with the altar itself, Exod. 27.2. Some of the blood of some sacrifices, was put upon the horns of the altar, sprinkled thereon by the finger of the priest, Lev. 16.18, 19. See Psal. 118.27. The horns were counted very sacred. Adonijah and Joab, fled to them, as to a sanctuary, 1 King. 1.50, 51. & 2.28. Let us thus fly to Christ, and lay hold on him.

**V. 16.** *{shall be twelve cubits long, and twelve broad}* Eight and forty cubits round about. The altar in the Tabernacle, was but twenty cubits round about, Exod. 27.1. being made to be movable, and portable. Solomon's altar was twenty cubits, on each square side, eighty in all, 2 Chron. 4.1. So this here came short of that 32 cubits. The height of Moses' altar was three cubits, Exod. 27.1. Of Solomon's, ten cubits, 2 Cron. 4.1. Of this, some think the height to be but six cubits; but rather it seems to be nine cubits; as was formerly showed, upon the thirteenth verse.

**V. 17.** *{and the settle shall be fourteen cubits——in the four squares}* That is, fourteen, from side to side; the bench being a cubit broad, on each side of the altar.

*{and the border about it shall be half a cubit}* See Annotations on vers. 13.

*{and his stairs shall look toward the East}* Steps are forbidden to be made to an altar, Exod. 20.26. But this was to an altar that was built occasionally; as Gideon's, Judg. 6.24. and Elijahs, 1 King. 18.32, &c. However, the Lord is of power to dispense with, and at liberty, to vary from his own temporary commands; especially, in matters of this nature and quality. The stairs of this altar were eastward; so that, as the priests mounted them, their faces were toward the Temple: and when they were risen to the higher bench, they walked on the north, and south side of the altar; and so, many of them might stand there, and order the sacrifices together.

**V. 18.** *{And he said unto me}* The manner of cleansing, purging, and purifying the altar.

**V. 19.** *{give to the Priests}* Compare with these, Exod. 29. and Lev. 8.

*{of the seed of Zadok}* Chap. 44.10, &c.

*{a young bullock}* And a goat, and a ram, vers. 22, 23, 25.

**V. 20.** *{And thou shalt}* Ezekiel being a priest, Chap. 1.3.

*{four corners of the settle}* The upper settle, wherein the horns were.

**V. 21.** *{and he shall burn it}* It shall be burnt.

*{appointed place of the house}* Within the utmost great compassing wall. See Lev. 4.12. & 6.11. & 8.17. & Exod. 29.14.

**V. 22.** *{a kid of the goats}* The Law appointed, instead of this, another ram, *Exod.* 29.15, 19. And in the other sacrifices following, divers such differences do also appear. These differences may haply serve, to show, that the Levitical ceremonies were changeable, and to end in Christ.

*{and they shall}* They of the seed of Zadock, vers. 19.

**V. 24.** *{salt}* Lev. 2.14. Mar. 9.49. to signify, the eternal, and incorruptible sanctity of the covenant, altar, and worship; and our duty, in the holy and incorrupt observation of them.

**V. 25.** *{Seven days——a Goat}* Thus enjoined, lest it should seem strange, being not enjoined in the Law of Moses.

**V. 26.** *{shall they purge}* You, and they together.

**V. 27.** *{And when these days are expired}* After the consecration ended, thenceforth the ordinary sacrifices and services, are to be performed on it, *Exod.* 29.38, &c. See Hebr. 13.10.

## CHAPTER 44

**Verse 1.** *{Then}* In this Chapter are set down, touching the east gate of the Sanctuary, and the use of it, The former polluting of the Sanctuary, objected now, and inhibited, hereafter. The Levites that were offenders therein disenabled in their office, others, the sons of Zadock, confirmed. And several ordinances for the priests, renewed, and enacted.

*{he brought me back}* From the inner court, next the Temple, Chap. 43.5.

*{outward Sanctuary}* Outward, in relation to the *Sanctum sanctorum.*

*{which looketh towards the East}* That is to say, to the east gate of the inner court; some say, of the body of the Temple.

*{shut}* So he was only brought to it.

**V. 2.** *{and no man shall}* Except only him, in the next verse. See *Exod.* 19.21, 22.

*{hath entered by it}* Chap. 43.2, 4. In an holy reverence and remembrance thereof. See a somewhat like case, in the holy oil and perfume, *Exod.* 30.32, 33, 37, 38.

**V. 3.** *{for the Prince}* It is safest to understand this of the high priests; and the second priests, such as Zephaniah was, 2 King. 25.18. Jer. 52.24. and Eleazar, in his father Aaron's lifetime, Numb. 3.32. and Zadock, in Abiathar's lifetime, 2 Sam. 15. See 1 Chron. 24.2, 3. Christ alone is the high priest of the New Testament. He alone opened the way into the holiest of all, by his own blood, offering himself, through the eternal Spirit without spot to God, Heb. 9.——15, 8. He enters himself and opens the door of salvation to all believers, Act. 4.12. Joh. 14.6.

*{sit in it, to eat}* Or, *by it to eat.* See Lev. 24.9. & 8.31. Exod. 29.32.

**V. 4.** *{the way of the North Gate}* He might not enter at the east gate, vers. 1. North gate, of the inner court.

*{filled the house}* Chap. 43.5.

*{fell upon my face}* See Chap. 1.28.

**V. 5.** *{Mark well,* &c.} As Chap. 40.4.

**V. 6.** *{Let it suffice you}* As 1 Pet. 4.3.

**V. 7.** *{Into my sanctuary strangers}* That were not of the seed of Israel, Act. 21.28, 29. & 24.6. Yea, strangers to be priests, that were not of the house of Levi; that were not of the circumcision itself. And such they had brought in, in the times of their heathenish idolatries, in the days of Solomon, Ahaz, Manasses, Amon, and others: and the ten tribes, as usually, so chiefly, in the days of Ahab. Unlawful ministers, false in doctrine, foul in life, are not to be admitted but ejected.

**V. 8.** *{Set keepers of my charge}* Contrary to the law, Num. 18.4. 1 Chron. 23.

*{for yourselves}* According to your own pleasures; and to your self-ends and purposes.

**V. 9.** *{no stranger}* A charge against that sin, vers. 7.

**V. 10.** *{And the Levites that are gone away}* The priests seem here to be meant; and for their apostasy into idolatry, to be degraded, and put into the place and function of the Levites, upon their repentance, in the four verses following. See 2 King. 23.8, 9, &c.

**V. 11.** *{they shall slay the burnt-offering}* This was part of the priest's office; not he that brought the offering, but the priest was to slay it, and flay it, Lev. 1.5, 6. So Moses, at first, supplying the place of the priest, was commanded, Exod. 29.10. and did it, Lev. 8.15. The Levites only helped them therein, in case of necessity; as 2 Chron. 29.34. Here then these lapsed priests, are either readmitted to this one part of the priest's office; or else are but only to stand before them to minister unto them, at that time; as the words following seem to import.

**V. 14.** *{keepers of the charge}* Partly they were in the case of such of the seed of Aaron, as had blemish in their bodies, Lev. 21.17, &c. partly, in the case of the Levites superannuated, Num.

**V. 15.** *{the sons of Zadok}* Who came from Eliazar, and from Phineas, to whom the covenant of an everlasting priesthood was given, Numb. 25.12, 13. And though it came to Eli, of the seed of Ithamar, and continued in that time in the days of Saul and David, yet the restoring of it to the line of Phineas, was implied in that threatening against Eli, 1 Sam. 2.30, 31, 35. and performed in Zadock. 1 King. 2.26, 27, 35. In whose house it was continued still after. And notwithstanding that interruption in Eli, yet the catalogue of high priests, is reckoned only in the race and line of Eleazar, 1 Chr. 6.4; &c.

*{that kept the charge of my sanctuary}* As it required in Deut. 33.9, 10.

*{they shall come near to me}* Chap. 40.46.

*{to offer unto me the fat}* Holy sacrifices in an holy manner, Lev. 3.16, 17. & 7.23, &c.

**V. 16.** *{they shall enter}* Laws for the priests in their ministration. Compare them with those in Moses.

**V. 17.** *{that when they enter in——linen garments}* Exod. 28.42, 43. & 20.26.

*{Inner Court, and within}* Court, and Sanctuary itself.

**V. 18.** *{linen bonnets}* And linen coats or ephods, and linen breeches, Exod. 28.4, 42, 43. 1 Sam. 1.18, 28. & 14.3. & 22.18. for honesty sake; to cover their nakedness.

*{that causeth sweat}* As woolen garments would do; which are therefore forbidden, ver. 17.

**V. 19.** *{utter court——they shall put off——}* Chap. 42.14.

*{the people with their garments}* Chap. 46.20. See the like, Exod. 29.37. & 30.29.

**V. 20.** *{only poll}* No affectation on either side; but gravity and modesty required, against the long hair of ministers, 1 Cor. 11.14.

**V. 21.** *{drink wine}* Lev. 10.9. Occasions of evil to be avoided; specially in sacred things, Psal. 93.5. and by sacred ministers. They of all men, must not be given to wine, 1 Tim. 3.3. Tit. 1.7. See Esay 56.12.

**V. 22.** *{a widow, or——}* See Lev. 21.13, 14. Priests to be more holy than others. That not lawful to them, which is to others; yet marriages allowed them. And so in the New Testament, 1 Tim. 3.2, 4. against Popish and devilish doctrine, 1 Tim. 4.1, 3.

*{or a widow, that,* &c.} The regard had of priests' widows.

**V. 23.** *{And they shall teach}* Lev. 10.10, 11. Their duty of teaching and preaching; apt to teach, 1 Tim. 3.2. Tit. 1.9. Act. 20.28.

**V. 24.** *{And in controversy}* Deut. 17.8, 9. Controversies belong to them. Their rule to judge them by.

**V. 25.** *{at no dead person}* Lev. 21.1, &c. They must have more rule over their passions, than others: and not suffer them to disturb or interrupt the duties of their calling.

**V. 26.** *{seven days}* See Num. 6.9. & 12.14.

**V. 27.** *{the Inner court}* Is called here the Sanctuary.

**V. 28.** *{And it shall be}* The sin-offering, and other offerings, oblations, first-fruits, &c. as is in the following verses. The Levitical priests plentifully provided for; all things considered.

*{I am their Inheritance}* And their possession, in the end of this verse. And he that possesses him, possesses all things, Deut. 18.1, 2. Num. 18.20. This shows their esteem; and is indeed their true treasure.

**V. 29.** *{every dedicate thing}* The best and daintiest thing are the priests.

**V. 30.** *{And the first of all}* Exod. 22.29, 30. Num. 15.20. & 18.12.

**V. 31.** *{That is dead of itself, or torn}* Lev. 22.8. Exod. 22.31. This forbid to all the Israelites.

## CHAPTER 45

**Verse 1.** *{Moreover}* In this Chapter are set down, the portion of land for the priests: and Sanctuary; for the Levites; for the city: for the prince. And ordinances for the prince and people; in rules of justice: and for oblation the prince's part and duty for offerings in all feasts and solemnities. And particularly in the first month, first day, and seventh day; and in the feast of the Passover, and of Tabernacles.

*{divide by lot the land}* Chap. 48. This much differs from the division made by Moses, and by Joshuah: and is such as never was; nor shall be when the Jews shall be called to the Gospel, Ro. 11.25, 26. But by this of the land, as also of the city and Temple, and worship, God applies himself to the weakness of his people that then were, shadowing out hereby, as by the best means unto them, the perfect instauration and glorious state and condition of his Church under the Gospel. And generally, this land of Canaan is made a type of the heavenly Canaan, Heb. 11.9, 10.

*{an holy portion of the land}* For the Sanctuary, priests, and Levites. They, in the first place are cared for, and provided. After them, the prince. Thus the Church, and magistrate should first be rewarded and provided for; and then the people and tribes.

*{five and twenty thousand}* Neither reeds nor cubits are expressed. Our translation understands it of reeds or rods. And a reed, being six cubits, and eighteen inches, as some will have it, Chap. 40.5. This length of five and twenty thousand reeds, (counting the eighteen inches for an half cubit) comes to 162500 cubits. Which taking the cubit for an half yard; and a thousand paces, or yards to a mile) comes to 81 miles and ¼. And taking the cubit for a yard, comes to miles 162½. But counting the reed to be 6 cubits, and 2 inches, or one hand breadth; as we rather must. See Annotations on chap. 40.5. Then this length reckoning 18 inches for a cubit, and the cubit so to be one half yard, comes to 77 miles and a little more. And counting the cubit for a yard, it comes to 154 miles and somewhat more. Now, if we leave reeds, and understand this 25000 of length, to be 25000 cubits, as some do; Then it comes to 12 miles and ½, after the half yard to the cubit: and to 25 miles, at the rate of a yard to the cubit. This length of 25000 runs east and west, chap. 48.10.

*{and the breadth shall be ten thousand}* Our translators understand it of reeds. And so the breadth will be, 65000 cubits; (counting therein still the eighteen inches for an half cubit) which number of cubits, taking the cubit for half a yard, comes to miles 32½. And taking the cubit for a yard comes to 65 miles. But instead of 18 inches to each reed, taking but three, as was said before. So the breadth will come to 30 miles and above ½ mile of ½ yard to the cubit. And to about 61 miles counting the cubit for a yard. Others, with less probability, understand this 10000 breadth, to be meant, not of reeds, but of cubits, do raise the account thus; to five miles, taking the cubit for ½ yard; Or to 10 miles, taking the cubit for a yard. This breadth runs north and south, chap. 48.10.

**V. 2.** *{Of this}* Part of that, ver. 1.

*{for the sanctuary}* Described, Chap. 40 & 41. and circumscribed, chap. 42.15, 16, 17, 18, 19, 20. Though some would fain understand this, not of the outmost wall, but of the wall of the court of the people. See *Annotations* on Chap. 42.16.

*{five hundred——square round about}* For the Sanctuary, or utmost compassing wall of it. And understanding it of reeds, as most do, by comparing it with chap. 42.15, &c. it so comes to 3250 cubits, counting 18 inches to be put to each reed. Which reckoning a cubit for half a yard, arises to one mile, and half mile, and half a quarter, and reckoning it for a yard, it arises to three miles and a half, on each square. But add only three inches to each reed, the account comes to something less. And understanding it, not of reeds but of cubits only, it so comes to ¼ of a mile on each square, after half a yard to the cubit. And to ½ mile on each square, after one yard to the cubit. Some, as we said, apply this measure to the wall of the court of the people: But very improperly, and improbably.

*{fifty Cubits round about for the Suburbs thereof}* Here cubits are expressly named. Which may seem to sway against the opinion of reeds, in the former words of this verse, and in the former verse; were it not that, chap. 42.16. seems to overrule it. See the Annotations there. And this comes to 25 yards, after half yard the cubit; or, to 50 yards round about, at a yard the cubit.

**V. 3.** *{And of this measure}* This may seem to relate to the measure of cubits, which are so lately mentioned in the words aforegoing: and none other expressed in the two former verses. But the due consideration of those measures, mentioned Chap. 42.16, 17, 18. seems strongly to oppose this opinion of cubits here to be meant. This verse shows, that the Sanctuary and most holy place, and that utmost compass or wall mentioned and measured, vers. 2. shall be part of, and placed within that holy portion of land, mentioned, and measured, ver. 1.

**V. 4.** *{for the Priests}* The Levites might also seem here to be included. But much rather, the Levites have as much for their several portion. ver. 5. and chap. 48.13, 14, 20.

**V. 5.** *{shall also the Levites have for themselves}* Another 25000 long, running east & west along by the side of the priests; and 10000 broad, joining to the south side of the priests, and running southward; both ways as much in quantity, as the priests had.

*{the ministers of the house}* Porters, singers, and adjutants, to the priests in their ministrations. Such of them as should not be rejected, as those were, Ezra. 2.60, &c. and those, chap. 44.9, &c.

*{for twenty chambers}* Twenty rows of chambers. And these, not to be, and lie in that possession of land of 25000 in length, and 10000 in breadth: But in the compass and building of the Sanctuary. There being ten gates, viz. one in the wall of the utmost surrounding compass, the east gate; and three, viz. east, south, and north, in each of the three courts. And each gate having porches; there were two rows of chambers in each gate, one on either side along between the porches, and so twenty in all.

**V. 6.** *{the possession of the city}* Described. chap. 48.15, &c. and ver. 30. to the end of that 48. Chapter.

*{five thousand broad}* Comes to miles 32½ or to 16¼: or to 5, or to 2½ according to the several ways of reckonings by reeds, or cubits, and the quantity of the cubit, set down on the first verse of this chapter.

*{and five and twenty thousand long}* Over against the oblation of the holy portion of the land; or, by the side of it; This comes to 162 miles and a half: or, to 81 and a quarter: or, to 25: or to 12 and a half; according to the several ways of reckonings, by reeds or cubits, and the quantity of the cubit, set down on the first verse of this chapter. Reckoning here to the reed 18 inches, beside the 6 cubits. But adding only three inches, or one handbreadth, to each reed; as the [10] rather is: Then the measures by the reeds fall out to be proportionably less.

*{it shall be for the whole house of Israel}* This metropolitan city, shall be common to all the tribes: and therefore it is divided into 12 streets, and a gate assigned to every several tribe, Chap. 48.31, &c.

**V. 7.** *{For the Prince on the one side}* On the west, and east side of that holy portion of the land; and of the possession of the city. This situation of his portion, some way, points out the nature of his office and duty, to be in the middle of his kingdom; ready to take care of all; but first, and more immediately, of the Church, and worship of God.

*{and the length shall be}* Length of the prince's portion on the west side of the holy portion, for priests, and Levites; and of the possession of the city, shall be to the West Sea. And the length of the prince's other portion on the east side of the holy portions of land, and of the possession of the city, shall be to the utmost bounds of the land eastward. And the breadth of them are the same that is the breadth of the holy portions of the land, and of the possession of the city, running north and south. So that these three, the priests, cities, and princes, two portions, took up a breadth of 25000 from north to south, running clean through the land, from west to east; and lying betwixt the tribes of Judah and Benjamin, Chap. 48.7, 8, 23.

*{over against one of the portions}* Each one of the three portions, viz. for the priests, Levites, and city.

*{from the west border}* Of the three said portions, to the West Sea, is the prince's west portions.

*{unto the east border}* From the east border of the said three portions, to the East Sea, is the prince's east portion. Thus the prince and civil magistrate, must have his due, Mat. 22.21. Rom. 13.6, 7.

**V. 8.** *{and the rest of the land}* As is appointed, Chap. 48.1, &c. Or, the princes are commanded to rest fully contented with their large and double portion; no more belongs to them, much less all; that they oppress not the people any more, but give their lands to them, quietly to enjoy them; as is further enjoined in the next verse. Thus laws are prescribed to them. And so Chap. 46.16, 17, 18. Their will is not a law, nor they above the laws. The rule belongs to them, which is prescribed to masters, Col. 4.1.

**V. 9.** *{Let it suffice you}* Your double portion; or, as in 1 Pet. 4.2, 3.

*{execute judgment}* Rom. 13.4. Esay 1.17.

*{exaction}* Whereby through force, and fraud, you expulse my people from their lands and possessions.

**V. 10.** *{Just balances}* Lev. 19.35, 36. Prov. 11.1. & 16.11. & 20.10, 23. Mic. 6.10, 11. In Christ's kingdom all must be just, without wrong, deceit, or oppression, 1 Cor. 6.1, 6, 7, 8, 10. Mat. 7.12.

**V. 11.** *{The Ephah}* The Ephah is a measure of dry things; as the Bath is of liquid things: both of the same measure and capacity. And whereas the Ephah before contained ten Homers, Exod. 16.36. and so ten Bathes; here the Homer contains ten Ephahs, and so ten bathes. ver. 14. The Epha before was the same with Amphora Attica, or Medimnus, containing in dry things, as much as 5 gallons, one pint, 4 ounces, 1 charat, & 16 grains, or thereabout. The bath was thereabout, 1 King. 4.21. and 7.26. And the Homer formerly was three pints and a half, or thereabouts. The change therefore is great that is here made. See Lev. 27.16. The Homer here is made the same with Cor. ver. 14.

**V. 12.** *{And the shekel shall be twenty Gerahs}* Exod. 30.13. Lev. 27.25. Num. 3.47. Gerah is penny half penny, and so 20 Gerahs made up the shekel of the Sanctuary, Num. 18.36. which was two shillings six pence. The common, or king's shekel was half so much, that is one shilling three pence, or a quarter of an ounce of silver.

*{shall be your maneh}* Or, *mina,* or, *pound weight,* 1 King. 10.17. 2 Chr. 9.16. The common pound weighed 15 shekels, that is, one pound, 17 shillings, six pence: the king's 20, that is, two pound ten shillings; the Sanctuaries, five and twenty, that is three pound two shilling six pence. But still in monies the account must go by the shekels; as formerly in measures it was to go by the Homer.

**V. 13.** *{This is the oblation}* The Law indeed, Num. Chap. 28. & Chap. 29. provided for the offerings, for the daily sacrifice, and other ordinary services: but here it is enjoined to the people more punctually and particularly.

*{the sixth part}* See on ver. 11. They must offer unto God the three score part of their wheat, and of their barley.

**V. 14.** *{Of oil the tenth part}* That is, the hundred part.

**V. 15.** *{And one lamb}* Out of two hundred.

*{for a meat-offering}* For the daily sacrifice, and other ordinary sacrifices, and meat-offerings.

**V. 16.** *{This oblation}* Mentioned in the three former verses.

*{for the Prince in Israel}* Or, *with the Prince in Israel;* that is, either prince to receive the said oblations; and thereout, and together with his own, to furnish the sacrifices, and services. Or rather, the prince to offer likewise out of his wheat, barley, oil, lambs, his like share; and thereout, or otherwise of himself to provide for the public sacrifices and services, as is set down in the verses following, to the end of the Chapter.

**V. 18.** *{Thou shalt take}* Speaks to the prince. So vers. 20, 22. he is expressed by name; and so understood in the verses following.

**V. 19.** *{Of the settle of the Alter}* This is more than was in the Law of Moses: As divers other things also.

**V. 21.** *{The Passover}* Compare what follows with Exod. 12. Lev. 23. Num. 28.16, &c. And see the variations.

**V. 25.** *{In the seventh month}* See Num. 29.12, &c. And observe the variations; showing thereby, that those sacrifices, rites, and ceremonies, were alterable in themselves, and to end at Christ's coming; as shadows vanishing, when the body came.

## CHAPTER 46

**Verse 1.** *{Thus,* &c.} In this Chapter are set down ordinances in God's worship, for the prince, and people. In particular, The prince his place, and standing; his offerings punctually on the Sabbaths, and in the new moons; his coming in, and going out. The people's also, and their offerings.
